#174. Bank of England’s inflation response ‘risks higher interest rates for longer’

Thinktank Niesr says timid rate rise last week likely to make inflation more persistent feature of UK economy

The Bank of England is risking higher interest rates for longer after a hesitant response to rising inflation, according to leading thinktank.

In a searing critique of the central bank’s tactics, the National Institute of Economic and Social Research (Niesr) said a timid increase in interest rates last week was likely to mean inflation staying above the Bank’s 2% target until at least 2030.

The thinktank said inflation was likely to become a more persistent feature of the UK economy unless the Bank took tougher action.

The Bank’s monetary policy committee increased the cost of borrowing from 3.75% to 4% last week and hinted at another increase to combat inflationary pressure unleashed by the Iran war.

The director of the Niesr, David Aikman, urged the Bank of England to be more aggressive in tackling inflation, saying all the ingredients were in place for workers to demand inflation-linked salary rises.

“Inflation is set to rise above 4% in the new year, just as most pay awards for 2027 are being agreed, so the risk that it becomes embedded is rising.

“One further quarter-point rise is unlikely to be enough, and the longer the MPC waits, the more it will eventually need to do.”

UK inflation stands at 3.5%, well above the Bank’s 2% target, driven higher by the soaring cost of fuel. Oil and gas prices have risen sharply since the end of February, when the US and Israel launched their war on Iran.

Brent crude oil has persistently traded at more than $100 a barrel in recent weeks – compared with about $70 before the war – but on Tuesday it traded below $90 at one point, the lowest since 31 August, after Donald Trump downplayed the need for military action against Iran “at this point”.

Investors, however, reined in their enthusiasm after US inflation climbed by 0.4 percentage points from September to October, revealing that high oil and gas prices were continuing to drive up costs. The S&P 500 index of leading US companies was down 0.9% by early afternoon on Wall Street.

The Niesr said its forecasts for growth in the UK and the global economy were based on oil prices at $105 a barrel. However, it cautioned that while prices had dropped, continued difficulties shipping and refining meant consumer prices would decline slowly.

Stephen Millard, an economist at the institute, said the lower oil price was also dependent on Trump sticking to his new policy. “We’ve seen him change his mind before,” he said.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

The Niesr expects the UK economy to grow by 1.6% this year, but by only 0.9% in 2028 as high inflation, high interest rates and tight government spending take a toll on consumer and business confidence.

The chancellor, John Healey, was criticised for deferring difficult decisions in the budget last month, which the institute said would leave a £30bn hole in the government’s finances if the pattern of doing so over the last decade was repeated next year.

Aikman said financial markets would punish the Treasury by raising the price of loans to the UK if it became clear that government spending was going to soar in areas such as defence without savings being made elsewhere.

He said there was a “double whammy” from higher energy prices and rising global borrowing costs that was not being addressed.

“Those pressures reflect global developments and cannot be avoided,” he said. “How monetary and fiscal policy respond to them is a domestic choice, and on both fronts decisions are being deferred.”

#169. Disabled people in England ‘have to wait up to two years for wheelchairs’

Ombudsman’s report says delays have profound consequences and limit users’ ability to lead independent lives

Disabled people are having to wait up to two years to get a wheelchair because of delays involving NHS bodies and private firms that make them.

The situation is revealed in a report by the parliamentary and health service ombudsman for England, Paula Sussex, who said hold-ups were having profound consequences and limiting wheelchair users’ ability to lead independent lives.

In one case a three-year-old boy with cerebral palsy was unable to start nursery at the same time as his twin brother because he had to wait seven months for a wheelchair. He hurt himself several times when he fell from an adapted buggy his parents were using while they waited.

Sussex launched an investigation into NHS wheelchair provision after a sharp rise in complaints – from eight in 2020-21 to 110 in 2025-26.

Four out of five relate to the handful of private firms that provide wheelchairs to at least half of all children and adults who need one to help them lead independent lives.

Most are about people who need a wheelchair having to wait longer than the supposed 18-week maximum to receive either the device or parts for it.

“For people who rely on a wheelchair, delays, unsuitable equipment or poor repairs can affect every part of their life”, said Sussex.

“There can be profound consequences, from physical pain and emotional distress to limiting their ability to lead full lives.”

Elliot Cruz Gardiyanoglu, now six, was three when he faced a seven-month wait for his wheelchair. That also meant that he was unable access education or specialist therapy.

“We were never told there would be such a long wait for the wheelchair,” his mother, Jania Cruz, said. “If we had known we would have explored other ways to get it, especially so that Elliot did not miss out on starting nursery at the same time as his brother.

“It was very hard for him to see his brother going to school while he couldn’t.”

Rachel Perrin, the director of Newlife, a charity for disabled children, said Elliot’s experience showed how “the wrong wheelchair – or no wheelchair at all – can be dangerous, as well as keeping children and their families trapped at home and leaving them unable to attend school and take part in activities with siblings and friends”.

Graham Woolner, a 68-year-old in Bishop’s Stortford who has spinal muscular atrophy, a degenerative condition, has needed a wheelchair for the last 15 years.

He was meant to get a new one by August 2023 but it did not arrive until September 2024, and when it did, it failed to work properly. He did not get one in full working order until October 2025, 26 months past the 18-week deadline.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

“After a year of waiting, the wheelchair they gave me broke on the first day. I only made it 100 yards before the batteries died,” Woolner said. “The battery issue was never resolved, so I couldn’t go anywhere for a year with any certainty that I would get there and back.

“I like to be independent, go into town and take my dogs for a walk. But it got to the point where I wouldn’t go out without my son in case I needed him to push me home.

“The wheelchair is effectively my legs. I need it to live my daily life.”

Nick Goldup, the chief executive of the Wheelchair Alliance, said the report shone a light on the systemic problems wheelchair users faced.

“Getting wheelchair provision right means giving people the freedom to live their lives and to enable them to contribute meaningfully to society,” he said.

Sussex highlights a “regulatory gap” in wheelchair services such as assessment, fitting and repair. The Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency oversees the equipment, but the remit of the Care Quality Commission – the NHS care watchdog – does not cover the services wheelchair users receive because they are not supervised by a health professional.

Integrated care boards (ICBs), NHS regional bodies that commission wheelchair services, have “unreliable information” about how many people locally need a wheelchair and are faced with “an uncompetitive wheelchair market” which is dominated by a handful of private providers. Nor are ICBs good at telling each other when they encounter problems with private firms that provide poor services.

An NHS spokesperson said:“Long waits for wheelchairs are unacceptable, denying people the independence and quality of life they deserve.

“That’s why our wheelchair quality framework, co-produced with wheelchair users, sets a clear expectation that 92% of people receive their equipment within 18 weeks, and requires commissioners to hold regular performance meetings with providers to identify and tackle delays.”

#167. ‘No one is in charge’: social care system in England in chaos, Louise Casey to say

Head of major review into adult social care will say that governments have been ‘asleep at the wheel’ of vital system

Successive governments in England have been “asleep at the wheel” on social care, leaving the system to grow as a chaotic patchwork of local services with no clear vision, Louise Casey, the head of the social care commission, is to warn.

Lady Casey, who is preparing proposals for a big review of adult social care, will say the result is that in effect “no one is in charge” of a system that provides vital services for millions of vulnerable people.

People’s experiences of care have varied depending on where they live, with different standards, assessment processes and waiting times, while the private care sector has grown dominant and regulation of services has often been ineffectual, she will say.

She will add that England has no clear “system stewardship” for social care, making it difficult for governments to target improvements to services or translate policy into practice, unlike in comparable countries such as France, Germany and Japan.

Casey is expected to call for a “new settlement” for care, “one based on values I still believe this country shares: decency, contribution and a duty to care for our neighbours, our loved ones and ourselves”.

Casey’s latest robust critique of adult social care, together with her view that the system faces a “moment of reckoning”, has additional force given Andy Burnham’s ambitions to introduce a new national care service and reform care funding and the workforce.

The prime minister has embraced the Casey review, accelerating its completion to next year – 12 months quicker than envisaged by the Starmer government – with the expectation that a national care service plan will be in Labour’s next election manifesto.

He has already announced he would use savings from scrapping the state pension triple lock, starting in the next parliament, to target investment into a care service based on the Scottish approach of free personal care, with individuals paying for “bed and board” costs.

Speaking at the annual national children and adult services conference on Wednesday, Casey will also say she has written to the Covid-19 inquiry agreeing with its findings on social care.

She is expected to say: “The Covid-19 inquiry took apart the argument that a ‘protective ring’ had been thrown around care homes during the pandemic. Many old people died without their loved ones by their side. Many care workers left their jobs after what they had seen.

“It is a national shame that this was allowed to happen. It should never happen again.”

The Covid-19 inquiry chair, Heather Hallett, last month described the adult care sector during the pandemic as “something of an afterthought”, with care home residents and staff given insufficient protection. More than 43,000 people died from Covid-19 in UK care homes between March 2020 and July 2022.

Casey has drawn on research that highlights how the lack of oversight and a “clear coherent narrative” in English social care is a “significant weakness” in the system, exacerbated by the historic low visibility of social care among successive governments.

The research, by the Nuffield Trust and the Institute for Government, found the lack of clarity in the system made it difficult and slow for policy change to happen. But it warned politicians against embarking on disruptive structural changes without a clear purpose or political determination.

“One last and inescapable point is that successful reform of social care requires political will and bravery to make the choices outlined throughout this report,” it said.

“No amount of reorganisation of governance structures or processes will be sufficient in the absence of political impetus to drive through change and dedicate resources, time and energy to social care. Maintaining social care visibility and priority within the government’s agenda will be essential.”

#166. The superpower battle over energy – and what it means for your bills

The superpower battle over energy â and what it means for your bills

A gas rig in the North Sea. An oil field in Texas. A solar panel factory in China. They can feel a world away, but your energy bill says otherwise.

In January, it could jump by more than a quarter â around Â£460 extra a year for the typical UK household â according to the latest forecasts from the big suppliers British Gas, EDF and E.ON.

For the second time in four years, a war thousands of miles away is landing in British inboxes.

Since US and Israeli strikes on Iran in February effectively closed the Strait of Hormuz, oil and gas prices have soared.

Winter is expected to push them higher still: demand for gas is rising just as Europe's winter gas stores are at their lowest since records began in 2011.

And there's a bigger threat looming over all of this: the warming climate. Cutting carbon emissions is essential to limit future warming and avoid the most damaging effects of climate change. But keeping bills down while cutting emissions is proving hard â and not just for the UK.

As world leaders gather this week in Antalya, Turkey, for COP31, the UN climate summit, the same tension is playing out around the world. Energy and climate policy has become the front line in a contest between the world's two energy superpowers, leaving everyone else squeezed in between.

America is betting on fossil-fuel molecules: pumping record volumes of oil and gas and pressing its allies to buy them. China is betting on electrons: flooding the world with the solar panels, batteries and electric cars that can replace those fuels.

Whether the bet on fossil fuels or renewables wins will shape not just what the world pays for energy, but how fast it can cut the emissions that are heating the planet.

The UK, which already buys nearly a quarter of its imported gas from America and much of its clean energy kit from China, is being pulled both ways. So which path will it take? Is there a middle way and what will it all mean for your bills?

On the Moray Firth near Inverness, a long spit of sand and dunes curls out into the sea at Whiteness Head. Behind it lies a yard where, for nearly 30 years, thousands of workers built the giant platforms that opened up North Sea oil.

Ming Yang, one of China's biggest wind turbine makers, wanted to bring it back to life with a Â£1.5bn factory and up to 1,500 jobs initially generated. In March, after US lawmakers had urged the UK to block it, ministers did just that, citing national security.

Panorama, Fire and Floods: Our Extreme Weather

Last month, they approved a new North Sea gas field, Jackdaw. No to Chinese wind turbines; yes to North Sea gas.

Few countries are more exposed to the energy shock than the UK. Gas heats most homes and, much of the time, sets the wholesale price of electricity. The last shock, after Russia's invasion of Ukraine, cost households an extra Â£63bn â around Â£2,200 each â according to the Energy and Climate Intelligence Unit and E3G, two climate and energy research organisations.

Wind turbines represent homegrown energy, but they come with potential problems

Less than half of the UK's gas now comes from its own North Sea fields, and the energy we import often comes from places that turn out to be unstable or unreliable.

At the same time, Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh says tackling climate change remains a priority after Britain's hottest summer on record. The heatwaves, droughts and wildfires it brought would have been almost impossible without global warming, the Met Office's chief scientist told BBC Panorama.

But when I pressed Fahnbulleh on which matters most, bills or climate, she was unequivocal: "With the cost of living biting, affordability is my overriding objective, without a doubt."

"We are far too exposed to global markets we have no control over," Fahnbulleh told me. "What's the solution to that? Homegrown clean energy."

There are two ways to make Britain's energy more homegrown: drill for more of it, or build more of it.

Within hours of Jackdaw's approval, US President Donald Trump - at war with wind turbines since objecting to a wind farm near his Aberdeenshire golf course in 2006 - took to Truth Social. "They should go FULL BLAST!!!" he wrote. "TAKE DOWN THE COSTLY AND RIDICULOUS WINDMILLS!"

Now the UK government faces a tougher choice - whether to approve Rosebank, west of Shetland, the UK's largest undeveloped oil field.

Rosebank is far bigger than Jackdaw - Fahnbulleh has said it would produce about seven times the emissions - and it is mostly oil, not gas. The subject has reportedly split the cabinet. A decision is expected within weeks.

Fahnbulleh, centre, at Jackdaw last month. She says the drilling will have minimal impact on energy prices

The government's opponents are already blaming high bills on its reluctance to drill. Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch has told the prime minister to "stop faffing about" and give permission, calling drilling "the only way we can protect families from rising bills".

"If we use our own gasâ¦ that will bring our bills down," says Reform UK's Richard Tice.

Last week's US midterms were a reminder of the political risks. Soaring petrol prices are widely seen as a key reason Trump's Republicans lost control of Congress.

But when I asked Fahnbulleh whether producing more British oil and gas would bring bills down, she was clear. "No, it doesn't," she said. "In the end, the oil and gas is sold on global markets."

Critics go further. Rosebank "doesn't really do anything for energy security", argues Tessa Khan of the campaign group Uplift, which has fought the field in the courts. "What it does do is send a very dangerous signal about the UK's seriousness about addressing climate change after the summer that we've just had."

Zoom out, though, and the row looks rather different. The North Sea is in long-term decline. Output has fallen by more than two-thirds since its peak in 1999, and the Office for Budget Responsibility expects it to keep falling by around 10% a year â even with Rosebank, which its forecast assumes goes ahead.

Whatever ministers decide, the basin's most productive days are behind it. Jackdaw's reserves amount to only a few months of UK gas demand, while Rosebank contains less than a year's worth of our oil consumption.

The debate around Rosebank and Jackdaw has sparked vehement opposition from climate protesters

And last week the government introduced a bill to write its ban on new exploration licences into law. Rosebank, which is already licensed, is not covered. That could let ministers have it both ways: approve the last big field, while promising there will never be another.

With bills set to rise again in January, that may prove a tempting combination.

Gas is bought, burned and bought again. A wind turbine or solar panel costs money to put up but can then generate electricity for decades without needing shipments of fuel.

The government's climate advisers, the Climate Change Committee, calculate that a future price shock like 2022's would push household energy bills up 59% if Britain stayed on a high-carbon path - but only 4% if it focused on renewables.

The cost of power from new solar farms fell by 90% between 2010 and 2024, and from onshore wind by 70%, according to the International Renewable Energy Agency. But the recent bout of global inflation has been pushing initial costs back up. Last year the Danish developer Ãrsted scrapped plans for Hornsea 4, a giant wind farm off the Yorkshire coast, blaming soaring supply chain costs.

Higher interest rates have made things worse. Most of the cost of a wind or solar farm comes up front, so developers must borrow heavily before they generate any power.

And there is another problem. Britain needs expensive new electricity networks to bring that renewable power to the towns and cities where it is needed. Meanwhile, gas-fired stations still frequently set wholesale power prices - meaning any cost savings are not always reflected in customer bills.

All of which means building more renewables does not automatically mean lower household bills.

And there is another issue - China's dominance of clean tech. It makes the vast majority of the world's solar panels and batteries, and around two-thirds of its electric cars.

And China's lead is growing. In March, its exports of solar panels, batteries and electric cars hit a record, up 70% on a year earlier, according to the think tank Ember.

The UK wants to escape the fossil fuels that leave households exposed to price shocks and keep adding to global warming. Much of the technology needed to replace those fuels comes from China - a country it is increasingly reluctant to depend on.

I asked Fahnbulleh whether Britain was swapping dependence on petrostates for dependence on Beijing. "Well, look, we are," she said, adding: "we've got a diversified global market".

Britain will keep buying from China where it needs to, says Fahnbulleh, but she wants more of the kit made here. "I want to see turbines," she said.

Yet UK-built turbines were exactly what Ming Yang was offering.

"There was a security risk, and national security comes first and foremost," says Fahnbulleh. The government has never publicly explained what that risk was.

Two days before Jackdaw was approved, Japan's Sumitomo Electric opened Britain's first HVDC subsea cable factory at Nigg, a short way up the coast from Whiteness Head, creating up to 250 jobs.

Foreign investment, then - just not from China.

Earlier this year the US formally left the Paris climate agreement and is not expected to send a delegation to this year's COP. Without American pressure, others have less reason to make bold promises: national pledges now add up to a cut in global emissions of just 6% by 2035, the UN says â a tenth of what scientists say is needed to limit warming to 1.5C.

Donald Trump is a big exponent of fossil fuels

China is beginning to fill the space. Li Shuo, of the Asia Society Policy Institute, calls it a "role reversal". Once "a very conservative player" at climate talks, China now sees pushing the global climate agenda as serving its national interest â and, he says, "we're only at the beginning of this". It aims to hit one of COP31's flagship goals, getting 35% of its energy from electricity, five years early.

And increasingly it is Chinese companies, rather than governments' promises, that are driving the transition. "It's really just a China story," says Li Shuo.

If that is right, it leaves the UK in an uncomfortable place. Hard choices â and they come when household budgets are already stretched thin.

Thatcher's Right to Buy created a generation of homeowners - did it also fuel the housing crisis?

The NHS is relying more on charities - and that might be creating problems for patients

#165. A new approach to a Covid-19 nasal vaccine shows early promise

Scientists in Germany say they’ve been able to make a nasal vaccine that can shut down a Covid-19 infection in the nose and throat, where the virus gets its first foothold in the body.

In experiments in hamsters, two doses of the vaccine – which is made with a live but weakened form of the coronavirus that causes Covid-19 – blocked the virus from copying itself in the animals’ upper airways, achieving “sterilizing immunity” and preventing illness, a long-sought goal of the pandemic.

WHO experts revise Covid-19 vaccine advice, say healthy kids and teens low risk

Although this vaccine has several more hurdles to clear before it gets to a doctor’s office or drug store, other nasal vaccines are in use or are nearing the finish line in clinical trials.

China and India both rolled out vaccines given through the nasal tissues last fall, though it’s not clear how well they may be working. Studies on the effectiveness of these vaccines have yet to be published, leaving much of the world to wonder whether this approach to protection really works in people.

The US has reached something of a stalemate with Covid-19. Even with the darkest days of the pandemic behind us, hundreds of Americans are still dying daily as the infection continues to simmer in the background of our return to normal life.

As long as the virus continues to spread among people and animals, there’s always the potential for it mutate into a more contagious or more damaging version of itself. And while Covid infections have become manageable for most healthy people, they may still pose a danger to vulnerable groups such as the elderly and immunocompromised.

Researchers hope next-generation Covid-19 vaccines, which aim to shut down the virus before it ever gets a chance to make us sick and ultimately prevent the spread of infection, could make our newest resident respiratory infection less of a threat.

China approves its first mRNA Covid-19 vaccine

One way scientists are trying to do that is by boosting mucosal immunity, beefing up immune defenses in the tissues that line the upper airways, right where the virus would land and begin to infect our cells.

It’s a bit like stationing firefighters underneath the smoke alarm in your house, says study author Emanuel Wyler, a scientist at the Max Delbruck Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association in Berlin.

The immunity that’s created by shots works throughout the body, but it resides primarily in the blood. That means it may take longer to mount a response.

“If they are already on site, they can immediately eliminate the fire, but if they’re like 2 miles away, they first need to drive there, and by that time, one-third of the house is already in full flames,” Wyler said.

Mucosal vaccines are also better at priming a different kind of first responder than injections do. They do a better job of summoning IgA antibodies, which have four arms to grab onto invaders instead of the two arms that the y-shaped IgG antibodies have. Some scientists think IgA antibodies may be less picky about their targets than IgG antibodies, which makes them better equipped to deal with new variants.

The new nasal vaccine takes a new approach to a very old idea: weakening a virus so it’s no longer a threat and then giving it to people so their immune systems can learn to recognize and fight it off. The first vaccines using this approach date to the 1870s, against anthrax and rabies. Back then, scientists weakened the agents they were using with heat and chemicals.

The researchers manipulated the genetic material in the virus to make it harder for cells to translate. This technique, called codon pair deoptimization, hobbles the virus so it can be shown to the immune system without making the body sick.

“You could imagine reading a text … and every letter is a different font, or every letter is a different size, then the text is much harder to read. And this is basically what we do in codon pair deoptimization,” Wyler said.

In the hamster studies, which were published Monday in the journal Nature Microbiology, two doses of the live but weakened nasal vaccine created a much stronger immune response than either two doses of an mRNA-based vaccine or one that uses an adenovirus to ferry the vaccine instructions into cells.

The researchers think the live weakened vaccine probably worked better because it closely mimics the process of a natural infection.

The nasal vaccine also previews the entire coronavirus for the body, not just its spike proteins like current Covid-19 vaccines do, so the hamsters were able to make immune weapons against a wider range of targets.

As promising as all this sounds, vaccine experts say caution is warranted. This vaccine still has to pass more tests before it’s ready for use, but they say the results look encouraging.

Health officials consider another round of bivalent boosters for the most vulnerable Americans

“They did a very nice job. This is obviously a competent and thoughtful team that did this work, and impressive in the scope of what they did. Now it just needs to be repeated,” perhaps in primates and certainly in humans before it can be widely used, said Dr. Greg Poland, who designs vaccines at the Mayo Clinic. He was not involved in the new research.

The study began in 2021, before the Omicron variant was around, so the vaccine tested in these experiments was made with the original strain of the coronavirus. In the experiments, when they infected animals with Omicron, the live but weakened nasal vaccine still performed better than the others, but its ability to neutralize the virus was diminished. Researchers think it will need an update.

It also needs to be tested in humans, and Wyler says they’re working on that. The scientists have partnered with a Swiss company called RocketVax to start phase I clinical trials.

Other vaccines are further along, but the progress has been “slow and halting,” Poland said. Groups working on these vaccines are struggling to raise the steep costs of getting a new vaccine to market, and they’re doing it in a setting where people tend to think the vaccine race has been won and done.

In reality, Poland said, we’re far from that. All it would take is another Omicron-level shift in the evolution of the virus, and we could be back at square one, with no effective tools against the coronavirus.

“That’s foolish. We should be developing a pan-coronavirus vaccine that does induce mucosal immunity and that is long-lived,” he said.

At least four nasal vaccines for Covid-19 have reached late-stage testing in people, according to the World Health Organization’s vaccine tracker.

The nasal vaccines in use in China and India rely on harmless adenoviruses to ferry their instructions into cells, although effectiveness data for these has not been published.

Two other nasal vaccines are finishing human studies.

Covid vaccine maker Novavax says it may not survive

One, a recombinant vaccine that can be produced cheaply in chicken eggs, the same way many flu vaccines are, is being put through its paces by researchers at Mount Sinai in New York City.

Another, like the German vaccine, uses a live but weakened version of the virus. It’s being developed by a company called Codagenix. Results of those studies, which were carried out in South America and Africa, may come later this year.

The German team says it’s eagerly watching for the Codagenix data.

“They will be very important in order to know where whether this kind of attempt is basically promising or not,” Wyler said.

They have reason to worry. Respiratory infections have proved to be tough targets for inhaled vaccines.

FluMist, a live but weakened form of the flu virus, works reasonably well in children but doesn’t help adults as much. The reason is thought to be that adults already have immune memory for the flu, and when the virus is injected into the nose, the vaccine mostly boosts what’s already there.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

Still, some of the most potent vaccines such as the vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella use live attenuated viruses, so it’s a promising approach.

Another consideration is that live vaccines can’t be taken by everyone. People with very compromised immunity are often cautioned against using live vaccines because even these very weakened viruses may be risky for them.

“Although it’s strongly attenuated, it’s still a real virus,” Wyler said, so it would have to be used carefully.

#163. UK ministers told not to engage formally with Indian mega-zoo after lobbying

Guardian and Mongabay reveal approaches to officials and MPs on behalf of Vantara zoo ahead of UK-chaired Cites meeting on endangered animal imports

British government ministers were told not to engage formally with Vantara, the controversial mega-zoo run by the Indian billionaire Anant Ambani, after lobbying efforts by the private animal sanctuary in the run-up to a UK-chaired meeting on the trade in endangered species, according to documents.

This week, the UK heads a 32-country committee meeting in Geneva of Cites, the United Nations body that regulates trade in endangered species. The meeting will examine how India has responded to compliance concerns about imports of endangered animals.

Documents obtained by the Guardian and the news outlet Mongabay under the UK’s Freedom of Information Act reveal Vantara’s efforts to lobby British officials and ministers before the meeting.

Ambani, 31, is the heir to the oil and gas fortune of one of India’s richest men, Mukesh Ambani. He set up a 1,400-hectare (3,500-acre) private zoo near his family’s oil-refining facilities in Gujarat, where more than 150,000 animals have reportedly been amassed since 2022. Vantara boasts high welfare standards for animals in its care, including facilities such as an elephant hydrotherapy pool, where more than 260 elephants can also receive oil therapies and Ayurvedic massage.

But conservation groups have repeatedly questioned how such a vast number of species, many of which are endangered, were gathered so quickly. Last year, India’s supreme court cleared the zoo’s operators of any wrongdoing.

A subsequent Cites fact-finding mission looking into the zoo raised concerns about India’s compliance with international rules on animal imports in 2025. It recommended temporary restrictions on India, although the recommendation was later withdrawn.

Its November 2025 report said: “There is a need for caution to ensure that imports of large numbers of animals by these facilities are not inadvertently creating a demand for illegally sourced animals.”

In May, officials from the UK’s Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (Defra) met a representative from Vantara and the US lobbyist firm Checkmate Government Relations, founded by Ches McDowell, a hunting friend of Donald Trump Jr.

Before the meeting, internal briefing documents said Checkmate was “seeking to persuade the UK to take a more sympathetic view of India’s handling of imports to Vantara and of Checkmate’s argument that responsibility for verifying legality sits primarily with exporting or re-exporting parties rather than with India as importer”.

During the meeting, the Vantara representative invited UK officials to visit the zoo – an invitation that was subsequently declined to ensure the impartiality of the UK’s Cites chair role.

After the meeting, a representative from another lobbying firm, Atlas Strategy, working on behalf of Vantara, approached the then secretary of state, Emma Reynolds, and then nature minister, Mary Creagh, also raising the prospect of a visit to the zoo.

When a department official asked for advice, a note was issued recommending that ministers did not engage formally with Vantara.

The government documents also reveal concerns about an application by Vantara to import falcons and falcon hybrids, after an unnamed organisation made a £50,000 “donation” to a breeder in the UK, whose identity was also not disclosed. British Cites officials classified the transaction as commercial, according to the documents. The falcons were not exported to India.

A spokesperson for Vantara said it had not imported falcons from the UK, nor had the zoo hired or paid a lobbying firm. It is understood that Checkmate and Altas Strategy were working pro bono for Vantara and not required to register with the Office of the Registrar of Consultant Lobbyists.

After recent scrutiny, Vantara has sought to “reset” its activities by voluntarily suspending animal imports for a year and hiring John Scanlon, a former Cites chief, to oversee its operations. In an interview with the Sunday Times this year, Ambani said he only brought animals to Vantara legally to ensure their welfare, mentioning a group of hippos he had saved from a hunting lodge in South Africa and jackass penguins saved from a shopping centre in Qatar.

“I think since I was a baby I have been taking care of animals. It’s always been a passion,” he said.

Vantara was used to host Ambani’s lavish pre-wedding celebrations in 2024, which were attended by celebrity guests and politicians. In September, Newcastle University awarded the oil heir a professorship of practice for “his work in conservation, biodiversity protection, animal welfare and environmental stewardship”, sparking a backlash from conservationists.

A Defra spokesperson said: “The UK has strict rules in place to protect endangered species and ensuring international wildlife trade is legal and sustainable, with every application assessed individually against internationally agreed and domestic requirements.”

Roza has decided to share her story with the BBC

A woman raped by Jeffrey Epstein says the president of the model agency representing her put her in danger, when he sent her to two photoshoots at the convicted sex offender's Florida beach house.

At the time, in 2009, Epstein was serving a jail sentence for sex offences against a minor but was allowed to work each day from his home.

"My agency's job was to protect me, and they failed. In fact, they put me in danger. And for that, they should be held accountable," she tells the BBC World Service.

Roza was 18 when she was first sent to Epstein's Palm Beach home by MC2 Model Management's US branch, having only arrived in the US from Uzbekistan about a month earlier. MC2's US president at the time, Jeff Fuller, now runs another US model agency called The Source Models.

"My agency knew exactly where I was. So Jeff knew exactly where I was going," says Roza.

Jeff Fuller now runs The Source Models agency

She shows us an expense statement suggesting Fuller himself booked her flight home from Palm Beach, following her second shoot at Epstein's home. Fuller has previously claimed he did not know Roza was going to Epstein's house.

She says the photoshoots led to her being employed as an assistant to Epstein. Other survivors have explained how the financier used the recruitment of assistants as an elaborate ruse to allow him to continue abusing young women.

Roza says Epstein went on to systematically rape her.

Fuller has not responded to the BBC's allegations. He has previously said he did not know Roza was being abused.

We discovered through the Epstein-related documents released by the US Department of Justice [DOJ] that 10 models from MC2 met, or were in communication with, Epstein after his conviction.

The files also show Epstein gave $1m (Â£742,000) to MC2 in 2005 - despite the agency owners telling a US publication two years later that Epstein had no involvement in their company.

A DOJ statement from last year, that no evidence has been uncovered which would warrant further investigation against "uncharged third parties", is now being challenged by New York State senator, Zellnor Myrie.

He has proposed a new sex trafficking bill to give police greater power to investigate Epstein's alleged enablers.

Senator Myrie told the BBC he wants those who knew - or should have known - about Epstein's abuse to be held accountable

Sara Ziff, founder of the Model Alliance, which campaigns for stronger protections for models in the US, approves.

#160. The private creative world of Rabindranath Tagore, the man Yeats called ‘greater than any of us’

A previously unseen family archive offers a glimpse into the Indian Nobel prize winner’s artistic process and personal relationships in his final years

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

It’s easy to run out of words to describe Rabindranath Tagore. The first non-European to win the Nobel prize in literature, Tagore was a Bengali poet, musician, novelist, philosopher, songwriter, painter, humanist and educator.

Born in 1861, Tagore left an indelible mark on the subcontinent’s literature, music, education and art. Among his enduring legacies are the national anthems of India and Bangladesh.

As Nobel laureate economist Amartya Sen once wrote, Tagore was “a towering figure in the millennium-old literature of Bengal”, whose songs resonate across eastern India and Bangladesh, while his ideas on education, nationalism and freedom of the mind remain relevant today.

Yet his work also made a deep impression on the literary world far beyond the shores of India. WB Yeats is said to have described Tagore as “greater than any of us”. Gitanjali, the collection of poems that won Tagore the Nobel prize that same year, was praised by the committee for its “profoundly sensitive, fresh and beautiful verse”. Tagore’s English translation was published in London that March and reprinted 10 times by the time the prize was announced in November.

Tagore’s life and works have, of course, been well documented, but a collection of material preserved in a private family archive is now being brought into public view for the first time at an exhibition by Christie’s, opening in Mumbai this month. The archive draws from Tagore’s visits to the family home of his protege, Maitreyi Devi, in the bucolic village of Mungpoo in Darjeeling between 1938 and 1940. He died in 1941.

The collection on display – artworks, manuscripts, letters, photographs and personal effects – offers an intimate glimpse into Tagore’s creative process and personal world in his final years.

I spoke to the exhibition curator, Nishad Avari, head of south Asian modern and contemporary art at Christie’s, about the stories behind the archive.

One instructive document Avari points to is a manuscript from 1940. It appears to be a draft of Tagore’s memoir Chelebela or My Boyhood Days, in which he reflects on his life – from growing up in a large family in Calcutta (modern-day Kolkata) to the rapidly changing world ushered in by electricity and the motorcar.

For Avari, what stood out was how the ideas in the document echoed the educational philosophy Tagore put into practice in Santiniketan (or Abode of Peace), the experimental school he set up about 100 miles from the state capital.

“He speaks about how education is like being in prison and that it should be open and free … about the idea of wonder and even fear as inspiring creativity,” Avari told me.

“That’s a direct relationship to Santiniketan and the open model of the school where people sat under trees with their gurus and learned in the open. Education didn’t feel like a restriction, prison or imposition,” he added.

Avari points to another detail that makes this particular document valuable. Of the 10 complete manuscripts in the exhibition, it is the only one dictated to Devi, whose father was a close friend of Tagore’s. For Avari, this speaks to their close relationship and the trust he placed in her.

“If you look at the collection as a whole, she has copies of almost every single one of his publications signed to her by him,” he says. The collection includes hundreds of letters, too: first between Tagore and her father, and later between Tagore and Devi herself.

There are doodles among corrections, Avari explains, which show how his literary practice fed into his artistic practice. Alongside the manuscripts and correspondence are vintage photographs and personal effects, including a kurta and a lungi that Tagore left behind at the family home.

Among the artworks in the collection is a portrait of Devi, particularly notable because Tagore’s figure studies were not generally attributed to specific individuals in the way that portraits by his nephews, Abanindranath and Gaganendranath, often were, Avari said. There is also a landscape depicting the forests of Mungpoo, which Tagore embraced as a retreat.

On the international art market, Tagore’s paintings command significant prices. This year, Sotheby’s sold one of his watercolours for £1.02m (Rs 12.97 crore), nearly four times its low estimate.

Institutions such as Visva-Bharati in Santiniketan and the National Gallery of Modern Art hold much of Tagore’s archival materials, making the discovery of a private archive particularly rare.

He hopes that making these materials public will offer historians, scholars and admirers alike additional perspectives on Tagore. “We hope to add to the understanding of the artist, their career, and in this case, of course, our understanding of the man beyond his art as well.”

Rabindranath Tagore and Mungpoo runs between 10 November and 18 December at Christie’s space in south Mumbai.

Politics | India has invited Pakistan to attend the Shanghai Cooperation Organisation’s Civilisational Dialogue Forum in New Delhi later this month, the Indian Express reported. It is the first invitation since the two neighbouring states engaged in a military conflict last year.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Academia | Indian academic Mahmood Kooria, a lecturer at the University of Edinburgh, is among this year’s recipients of the Philip Leverhulme prize in Humanities. The £100,000 award recognises his work on the premodern Indian Ocean world.

A recommendation from someone with great taste

Freddy Birdy, a copywriter whose delightful words have earned him a devoted Instagram following, recommends writer and historian Malvika Singh’s cookbook, Cooking for My Firefly.

“This book isn’t about recipes, though it does include them. It is a voyage through another time and place, tracing the social fabric of India of the 60s, 70s and 80s, trickling down to today. Told in Malvika’s beautiful prose, it takes us to a time when people entertained in homes, not in restaurants; when dishes were cooked in-house and there were no caterers or party planners. An era long before Instagram posts, WhatsApp invitations, celebrity chefs and food delivery apps, when the point of having a party was simply to have fun. It is an archive of photographs, food, culture, gossip, movie stars, politicians, royalty, set against the spectacular backdrop of Malvika’s dining tables through the decades. I like to dip into it every now and then, much as one might do standing in a dark kitchen with the light of the refrigerator enticing you to midnight goodies.”

Indians lost Rs 22,495 crore (£1.77bn) to cyberfraud in 2025, Times of India reported citing data from the Home Ministry. While the figure is marginally down from the previous year, losses have risen sharply since 2023.

At a time when public anger has spilled into the streets, artist Aamir Aziz’s new track, Dopeher Blues (Afternoon Blues), captures the frustration and angst of a generation that has lived through many crises.

Aziz describes the song as an expression of a period of deep suffocation. Issues such as farmer suicides, unemployment and conflicts in Palestine and Kashmir, he told me, are things he has grown up with even as public attention shifts with the news cycle. It is also a warning, in part, against internet culture, where injustices risk becoming normalised.

Aziz, 35, works in acting, music and writing, and became an internet sensation with his defiant poem Sab Yaad Rakha Jayega (Everything Will Be Remembered) during the citizenship protests. At the heart of his new track is a note of defiance and hope: “We’ll find a way to sing,” he says, as he calls on everyone “to keep speaking up even in a climate of fear, misinformation and oppression.”

#158. Polish unity on show as thousands join silent march for Krakow shooting victims

Thousands gathered at Krakow's historic Market Square to honour the victims of Saturday's attack

Thousands of people joined a silent march through central Krakow on Monday night, in memory of those killed and injured when a gunman went on a violent rampage at the weekend.

The crowd were led by Poland's president and prime minister, the bitter political rivals putting on a rare show of unity after the country's deadliest attack in modern times.

As they reached the historic Market Square, a bugler played from the tower.

Many people were carrying flowers or candles to leave at the shrines that now dot the cobblestones, each one marking a spot where someone died.

Fiercely proud of its reputation for safety, Poland has been profoundly shaken by this attack. It has left people struggling to make some kind of sense of what happened.

The alleged gunman has been named as 24-year-old Michal Oksinski by local media.

Critically injured in a shoot-out with police, he did not regain consciousness and died in hospital early on Monday.

Initially, the interior minister claimed that Oksinski was driven by far-right, extremist views. The national prosecutor's office now says that it is too soon to say he was "inspired" by any ideology.

"The minister wanted to win domestic political points. That's not responsible behaviour," Pawel Musialek of the Jagiellonian University suggests, in a country where politics is highly - and often viciously - polarised.

"So far we've seen no clear message that this guy was strongly ideological, and that it's not more like something from the darknet."

There has been no mention of any letter or manifesto that Oksinski left. One neighbour told Polish news site Onet that the 24-year-old was a "bit of a loner" but few who knew him well have spoken publicly.

What we know so far after Krakow gun attack

In addition to his electronic devices like his phone, investigators are examining a number of badges that Oksinski had attached to his military-style outfit.

The prosecutor's office says a yellow Gadsden flag with a coiled snake symbol was displayed in his bedroom. "It has various meanings of freedom and rebellion," a spokesman emphasised. "Not necessarily negative."

It was at Oksinski's home that the bodies of his parents and sister were discovered.

It is alleged that he shot them, before driving some 200km (125 miles) south to continue the killing in Krakow.

Candles and flowers have been placed around the square in places where someone died

Two days on, shops and cafes around the market square are beginning to fill up again, but the mood is subdued.

A Zara clothes shop is still boarded up, after bullets smashed the glass. A local man in his 50s died just outside.

Jo, a Scottish tourist, saw paramedics "who got there ridiculously fast", fighting to save him. "It was all pretty grim."

Like many, she initially mistook the rifle shots for fireworks.

On the opposite corner of the square, beside another cluster of flowers, people keep pausing to take pictures of the holes that were blasted into the wall by gunfire.

One of the six victims has been identified as a Polish painter in her 50s, who was a mother of four, and in town with friends for an exhibition opening.

A British citizen and an Austrian who died are yet to be named publicly.

Several drivers of the horse-drawn carriages that are usually poised to trot tourists around the square were seriously injured.

Olha, a Ukrainian citizen, moved to Krakow for safety when Russia launched its full-scale invasion

Olha, who works in a nearby chocolate shop says everyone is now nervous.

On Saturday, people rushed into her store to escape the gunman who was tearing around in his car, firing through the window.

Olha is Ukrainian, and moved to Krakow when Russia invaded. "We left our own country for safety. This is supposed to be a safe place!" she said.

"It's so sad, and so scary. Especially that he drove all the way here for two hours, planning this and thinking about it."

She regrets that the gunman did not survive: "So many are dead, so many lives have been ruined, and now there will be no justice."

Political differences were set aside between President Karol Nawrocki (left) and Prime Minister Donald Tusk (right) at the vigil on Monday

When Oksinski's car was searched, four firearms and "hundreds of rounds of ammunition" were found, all owned legally by the alleged killer and his father.

Both were members of the Swit Starachowice sports club, where there is a shooting range. A spokesman recalls Michal Oksinski as unremarkable: average competition results and normal behaviour.

He had been just three times this year, most recently in August with his father. The club spokesman is adamant that it does not train combat firing, only shooting from a fixed point.

Gun ownership in Poland has grown considerably since war broke out in Ukraine just across the border, but this is the first mass shooting anyone here can remember.

Inevitably, it has brought scrutiny of the checks over who qualifies to handle such weapons, including psychological tests, as Michal Oksinski passed all of them.

On Monday, the government announced an urgent review.

"Thank you Mr Policemen, get better soon"

At the spot where Oksinski's car was intercepted and he was shot by police there is another memorial, this one in honour of the officers still in hospital.

Some have brought flowers, but a girl called Bianka has left a card that reads: "Thank you Mr Policemen, get better soon."

A couple of steps away, there's a series of bullet holes in the glass.

"This man was a real hero, he didn't hesitate," Ania says, referring to officer Jakub Kulik who was shot in the chest. His condition remains serious. "We're here to help him fight for his life."

"Nothing like this has never happened before, it's terrible," a young man called Wojtek tells me.

He's only glad that the gunman responsible for such horror turned out to be Polish.

"People here are already against immigration, so that would have been even worse."

Ania thinks Poles will pull through, though the country's image as a low-crime oasis has been shattered.

Most of all, she's angry that Michal Oksinski died without being held to account for the devastation.

#157. Environmentalists criticise choice of Earthshot prize venue owned by fossil fuel firm

Mumbai facility is part of Reliance Industries, owner of world’s biggest oil refinery and funder of controversial mega-zoo

The Prince of Wales’s Earthshot prize will host its forthcoming annual awards ceremony at a venue owned by the billionaire Ambani family conglomerate, drawing criticism from conservationists over their fossil fuel businesses and their foundation’s controversial mega-zoo.

A spokesperson for the Earthshot prize, which awards £1m to five winners every year to invest in conservation work, confirmed to the Guardian and the news outlet Mongabay that this year’s ceremony will be held at the Jio World Convention Centre in Mumbai on 17 November.

The venue is part of Mukesh Ambani’s Reliance Industries, which also owns Jamnagar, the world’s biggest oil refinery in Gujarat on India’s western coast. The group’s philanthropic arm funds the 1,400-hectare (3,500-acre) private zoo Vantara, which was founded by Mukesh’s son Anant Ambani and inaugurated by the Indian prime minister, Narendra Modi, last year. The zoo is embroiled in a long-running controversy over its vast collection of more than 150,000 animals, which reportedly includes some of the world’s most endangered species – from the radiated tortoise, native to Madagascar, to the Spix’s macaw, endemic to north-eastern Brazil but now considered extinct in the wild.

Megan Carr, of the Wildlife Animal Protection Forum South Africa (WAPFSA), warned that using a venue owned by Reliance “could lend an unearned environmental prestige to a corporate group currently under question by the global conservation community”.

Conservation groups, including WAPFSA, have raised concerns about the speed with which Vantara has amassed so many animals, and about how they were sourced. The site, near the family’s oil refinery, houses more than 2,000 species of animals.

This week, the 32-member committee that governs Cites, the UN-administered body that regulates trade in endangered species, requested that India submit another report on the steps it is taking to address concerns relating to its imports of protected species.

The committee also requested the Cites secretariat review 11 cases where there are unresolved questions over how animals were sent to Vantara.

Joanna Swift, the executive director of communications at the Earthshot prize, said it had not sought a philanthropic partnership for its award ceremony venue and that it was paying the standard commercial rate to rent the space.

“The [Jio World Convention Centre] was selected because it allowed us to host the event in Mumbai while meeting the practical requirements of an event of this size. It offers world-class facilities, technical infrastructure and experience of hosting major international events, including environmental ones,” Swift said. “In February this year, JWCC hosted the first ever Mumbai climate week.”

Vantara, Kensington Palace and JWCC have been approached for comment.

Vantara has previously denied allegations of wildlife trafficking and says its purpose is “to rescue wild animals in distress, provide treatment along with lifelong care, and rehabilitate those fit for the wild”.

“With regulatory approval, we also breed select endangered or extinct-in-the-wild species, serving as a modern Noah’s Ark to ensure their survival under human care and restore wild populations through reintroduction,” Vantara says on its website.

Last year, India’s supreme court cleared the zoo of allegations that animals had been acquired illegally or mistreated.

A later Cites fact-finding mission raised concerns about India’s compliance with international rules on animal imports in 2025. It recommended temporary restrictions on India, although the recommendation was later withdrawn.

Susan Cheyne, the vice-chair of the International Union for Conservation of Nature’s small apes section, said that Vantara’s model has not yet been shown to support conservation and “may be driving demand for wild animals in trade”.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

“For gibbons and other primates, this matters a great deal. Moving animals out of their range countries into a private facility does nothing for wild populations unless there is a transparent, credible plan for those animals: for welfare, for any conservation breeding with clear goals, and for return to range states where that is possible. Real rescue and sanctuary work for primates is done by underfunded centres in range countries,” Cheyne said.

Daniela Freyer, co-founder of the German NGO Pro Wildlife, added: “Our planet is increasingly threatened by the overexploitation of wildlife and by climate change driven by fossil fuels. This week, the Cites Standing Committee, which oversees international wildlife trade, decided to keep India under its compliance procedure, as questions remain about the origin of some of the world’s rarest animals imported to Vantara. A prize whose goals include protecting nature and fixing our climate should be careful not to lend its reputation to these questions.”

Vantara had sought to lobby the UK, the chair of this week’s Cites meetings, attempting to persuade officials to take a more sympathetic view of India’s handling of imports to Vantara, the Guardian and Mongabay reported earlier this month. Ministers were advised not to engage formally with Vantara, internal documents revealed.

The Earthshot prize, founded by Prince William in 2020, is billed as the most prestigious environmental award. William appeared on the BBC’s Graham Norton show last month to promote the prize, saying it was designed to “talk about urgency and optimism together”.

Swift said that it was vital to host an edition of the Earthshot prize in India.

“Mumbai is the sixth largest city on earth. It is home to exceptional environmental solutions and the people behind them, with an impact felt in India and around the world. Spotlighting progress like this, to the largest possible audience, is why the Earthshot prize exists,” Swift said.

This article was amended on 9 November 2026. An earlier version said the Vantara zoo covers 400 hectares; in fact, it is 1,400 hectares.

#156. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#154. Inside devastated front-line city that Russia wants Ukraine to give up

Meet the Peaky Blinders, Ukraine's drone unit protecting devastated front-line city

The dangers in Slovyansk are clear from the start.

"If I say run, you run," Tetiana tells us. She and her colleague Yasha are escorting us into a city now under constant bombardment from Russian drones, missiles and glide bombs.

It is raining and cloudy as we enter the city, and our military escorts call this "good" weather, meaning it is bad for drones.

But the threat is still there, and Yasha keeps his eyes peeled on the skies, while 21-year-old Tetiana scours her phone for the latest military intelligence on likely threats from the air.

They are members of a drone unit who call themselves Peaky Blinders and their distinctive emblem is a flat cap of the type made popular by the cult BBC TV series, although theirs are camouflaged.

Slovyansk is one of four cities that make up Ukraine's "fortress belt" - a system of heavily reinforced defence lines in the parts of eastern region of Donetsk still under its control.

Russia wants Kyiv to give up the fortress cities and they are central to talks on a possible peace deal that have resumed after nine months between Ukraine, Russia and the US in Abu Dhabi.

Ukrainians worry that doing so would make it much easier for Russia to advance further west, including to the capital Kyiv.

Very little of the city has been left unscathed by months of heavy bombardment

Once inside the city, we leave our vehicles hidden under the cover of trees and venture out on foot. Yasha carries a shotgun â a last resort to shoot down Russian drones. In halting English, Tetiana tells us walking is safer than travelling by car.

Abandoned vehicles litter the streets â recently destroyed by remotely operated Russian first-person view drones. The anti-drone netting that covers the roads is no guarantee of safety.

Tetiana shows us a video on her phone taken a few days earlier.

You can hear several drones buzzing above her, and then the cacophony of small arms fire as Ukrainian forces try to shoot them down. Tetiana admits it can be scary but explains how she felt compelled to help her country, abandoning her dreams of becoming an opera singer.

Slovyansk is now a husk of its former self. Very few buildings have been left unscathed, with many reduced to rubble.

Tetiana shows us a school recently destroyed by Russian glide bombs. Across the road there is a gaping hole in an abandoned residential block. Broken glass litters the pavement. The few shops still functioning are boarded up â making it hard to distinguish what is still open. Most have closed.

Tetiana says the Russians are doing everything they can to intimidate people and drive them out of the city. That, she says, "is their strategy â to make people leave in chaos, gathering up their last belongings and fleeing amid enormous fear and suffering. They want people to have nowhere left to live".

It is a familiar pattern already seen in other Ukrainian towns and cities in the Donbas â like Pokrovsk and Bakhmut. These are now ghost towns reduced to rubble, but eventually falling into Russian hands.

Russia has already taken nearly 90% of the Donbas â made up of the regions of Luhansk and Donetsk - but its advances this year have all but stalled in the face of strong Ukrainian resistance.

The White House is sounding positive about the revived talks in Abu Dhabi, despite Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky's refusal to cede the territory.

October was the deadliest month for Russian troops since the start of its full-scale invasion â with more than 54,000 killed or injured, according to Ukraine's general staff. Despite this, Russian forces are still making life in the Fortress Belt cities almost impossible.

Iryna, who works in one of the city's last remaining cafes, is one of the few residents who decided to stay

Before the war, Slovyansk was a city of more than 200,000 people. Less than 10% of those remain and the population diminishes by the day.

But amid the wail of near constant air raid warnings, there are still signs of people clinging on to life here. Iryna works in one of the city's last remaining coffee shops. Slovyansk is where she was born.

"I'm forced to walk around crying," she tells me. "How can they do this?"

Life, she says, has become incredibly difficult. She has already lost her house. But despite her tears, there is still defiance: "I won't leave Slovyansk. This is my home."

Inga, 53, was also born and raised here and continues to work in the city despite the danger.

"It's stressful. Honestly, I don't even know what to say," she says, but she is staying because of what she calls the "fear of the unknown".

"I simply have nowhere to go, and I don't have the money to leave or support myself afterwards." Nor, she says, is there anywhere else in Ukraine she would consider truly safe.

Commander Oleksandr of the Peaky Blinders special forces unit says neither side is able to advance because of the large number of drones in use

We first met the Omega National Guard Special Forces drone unit two years ago fighting in the Kharkiv region, in north-eastern Ukraine.

Just four of the original team remain - the rest have moved to other units, or they have been wounded or killed.

Among the dead was the brother of the man in charge of the unit, Oleksandr, who now describes his unit as his family.

He jokes it is just like the Peaky Blinders TV show â "but without the crime and the drugs". Back at their base they share a meal every evening.

Oleksandr reads out a prayer of thanks for surviving another day â he says it strengthens their spirits. The meal is accompanied by classical music played on a speaker. It helps drown out the noise of the explosions nearby.

"I feel this is the most difficult situation we've ever faced," says Oleksandr, who underlines the importance of holding the line.

"We must be here to inflict maximum damage on the enemy. We're fighting here because this is where the most intense phase of the war is taking place. Russia's claim that they will reach the borders of the Donbas, seize it, and then the war will end is absolutely false," he insists.

The remaining fortress belt cities provide Ukraine with a strong defensive line and are a major obstacle to any Russian advance. The saturation of drones in the area is also now making it nigh-on impossible for either side to advance here.

Peace talks may have resumed, but Oleksandr notes they have not led to anything so far.

"I really want this war to end," he says, "but do I believe these negotiations will succeed? No, I don't really believe they will".

The streets are largely empty, with increasing numbers of residents evacuated as drone attacks intensify

The next morning we are not the only ones making an exit. A dozen civilians have arrived at an evacuation point in the centre to be escorted out.

They hold all the belongings they can carry and are taken into a bunker for their safety. Outside there is another heavy blast of small arms fire, signalling more drones are nearby.

Alina Shyrko, herself a resident of Slovyansk, is co-ordinating the move of civilians out of the city and says the situation has deteriorated rapidly over the past few months. Families with children have already been ordered to leave.

"Everyone is thinking about leaving," she says, "I'm thinking about it too."

Ukraine still holds Slovyansk but its lifeblood, its people, are ebbing away.

Additional reporting by Rebecca Hartmann, Volodymyr Lozhko and Moose Campbell.
