#151. Grenades, grief and a deepening political divide: five takeaways from Sonia Gandhi’s new memoir

From the assassinations of her husband and mother-in-law to press stonewalling under Modi, Belonging offers an insider’s account of India’s political evolution

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

Few love stories have been as closely entwined with a nation’s history as that of Sonia and Rajiv Gandhi. The former’s much-awaited political memoir, Belonging, offers a strikingly candid account of her journey from a small town in Italy to the highest echelons of power in India – all for love, she says.

The veteran Congress party leader has been fiercely protective of her personal world. In Belonging, she opens up for the first time about the extraordinary twists and turns of a life that has largely unfolded in full public view. (The Guardian’s Hannah Ellis-Petersen has a rare in-person interview with Gandhi, and you can read an excerpt from the book here.)

Belonging, published today, contains moving glimpses of Gandhi’s private grief. She writes about the loss of her first child, who died shortly after birth, and the chilling discovery of grenades in a room of their house after Operation Blue Star, the 1984 military operation against Sikh separatists at the Golden Temple in Amritsar. She also provides a ringside view of several turbulent chapters in Indian politics, not least the violent assassinations of her mother-in-law, Indira Gandhi, in 1984 and her husband, Rajiv, in 1991.

Her book also revisits some of the darker episodes that have dogged the Gandhi family and the Congress party, including Indira’s imposition of the Emergency in June 1975, a 21-month period during which civil liberties were suspended, political opponents were jailed and the press was gagged, which caused fear and dismay across the country. In Gandhi’s account, it also caused a rift within the family.

Ultimately, Gandhi’s memoir is a vivid record of six decades of India’s political transformation from the Congress-dominated secular republic she moved to in 1968 until today, when Hindu nationalism has become the dominant political force under Narendra Modi.

Here are five key takeaways from her memoir and what they reveal about the state of Indian politics today.

Gandhi has had no real contact with Modi for the last 12 years

Gandhi’s account offers a glimpse of how deep the political polarisation runs in Indian politics today. In one revealing passage, Gandhi writes that she has had “practically no real contact” with Modi in the 12 years he has led the nation. In fact, at times when the two have been in the same rooms, such as the speaker’s small chamber, Modi “tends to look away, avoiding eye contact”, she says. On occasion, she adds, he has asked briefly about her health after a hospital stay.

For her, this breakdown is a departure from the past, when leaders on opposing sides of the aisle “would treat one another with cordiality”. After the 2001 terror attack on parliament, Gandhi immediately called then-BJP prime minister AB Vajpayee to enquire about his safety, a gesture he appreciated.

Gandhi sees an increasingly authoritarian style of politics, in which those in power seek to “belittle and torment” opponents. For years, Gandhi’s son, Rahul, has been the subject of BJP’s ridicule, which calls him Pappu, a derisive term intended to portray him as inexperienced and naive. She adds that similar things are happening elsewhere in the world, an observation that can be read in the context of US President Donald Trump’s use of personal insults and ridicule against opponents.

It’s lonely at the top for India’s female politicians

India has an illustrious history of women occupying top leadership positions, long before many other democracies. But their rise has often come at a cost – that of loneliness in navigating a male-dominated political world.

In the book, Gandhi describes a 2012 conversation with Hillary Clinton on the countrys’ female leaders and their backgrounds. In her recollections of Indira, she shares that, despite wielding authority, Indira could feel besieged and profoundly alone, and this was particularly acute, Gandhi writes, in the months leading up to the declaration of the Emergency by her mother-in-law.

Gandhi also writes of her own isolation, including when she decided to take on the leadership of the Congress out of a sense of duty to her family’s political legacy.

Those in power are increasingly stonewalling the press

Among the many ways in which Gandhi charts the erosion of India’s democratic conventions in Belonging, one particularly telling shift is the changed relationship between the media and those in power. She recalls a time when journalists had opportunities to question prime ministers directly.

In 12 years in power, Modi has not addressed a single press conference on his own. He has sat in on one press briefing at the conclusion of an election campaign, and appeared alongside US presidents at joint press encounters after bilateral meetings, where they took questions from the press. Modi has consistently avoided facing journalists in an open forum, and his media interactions have largely been one-on-one interviews with sympathetic, pro-government news anchors or in one much publicised exchange with a Bollywood actor who asked him how he liked to eat mangoes.

The book’s publication has brought renewed attention to concerns about the space for free expression in contemporary India, and the extent to which apprehensions about repercussions may influence what can be said, written and published.

Penguin Random House India was to publish Gandhi’s memoir but withdrew from the project in August. Gandhi told the Guardian that it was “uneasy in general with the criticism of the government” and said it had dropped the book after she declined certain suggested edits. Penguin has disputed her account, saying that the suggestion it decided not to publish the book because of refused editorial changes “does not accurately reflect the circumstances”.

The book, released in the US by Alfred A Knopf, is being published in India by HarperCollins.

In June, Penguin had also pulled out of plans to distribute graphic journalist Joe Sacco’s The Once and Future Riot, about the 2013 Muzaffarnagar communal violence. The publisher cited an inaccurate map of India and unresolved citation issues. Sacco, however, blamed the decision on the “current political climate in India”.

She’s had quiet fallouts with the ‘thoroughly dishonest’

Western political memoirs, particularly by former heads of government or senior politicians, are often forthcoming in naming political adversaries, recounting private exchanges and offering candid assessments of colleagues. David Cameron’s 2019 memoir, For the Record, took aim at his former friend, Michael Gove, describing him as “mendacious” and a “foam-flecked Faragist”.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Gandhi, in contrast, is restrained. She writes warmly of the closeness between the Gandhi family and that of Bollywood superstar Amitabh Bachchan, whose parents she lived with when she first moved to India, but she makes no mention of the subsequent estrangement between the two families. In another instance, she does not name the political leader she describes as “thoroughly dishonest” and who she says she trusted in 1999, when Congress unsuccessfully attempted to form a coalition government.

Art | Art Mumbai runs from 12-15 November at the Mahalaxmi Racecourse. Among the highlights of this year’s edition is artist Baaraan Ijlal’s collaboration with the celebrated lyricist Javed Akhtar, turning his evocative Urdu couplets into paintings. The two will be in conversation with curator Ranjit Hoskote on Friday.

Government | The official bungalow of Andhra Pradesh’s chief minister, N Chandrababu Naidu, was renovated by a government-owned enterprise for Rs 6.5 crore (£518,000), according to the Indian Express (£). The renovation work on the Delhi property included was said to have had a major makeover.

Film | Bollywood’s ambitious rendering of Hindu epic Ramayana, starring Ranbir Kapoor and Sai Pallavi, was released on 5 November. Touted as the most expensive Indian film ever made, it earned praise from the Guardian’s Peter Bradshaw, who described it as “thoroughly engaging, rousing and spectacular”.

Readers from India and around the world on the festival of lights and tell us how they celebrated.

double quotation markIn India, Diwali comes to you. You can see it in the streets, hear it in the neighbourhood and feel it in the atmosphere. In the UK, it is different. Life carries on around you. But traditions do not have to disappear when you move countries – they can change shape. A small light in a quiet room can carry the same meaning as an entire street illuminated for Diwali. Aman, Plymouth, UK

double quotation markMy favorite part of Diwali back home was getting up before sunrise on Narak Chaturdashi and having a ceremonial bath, followed by a breakfast of traditional Marathi Diwali snacks. I have been living abroad for over 10 years now and it’s always a bittersweet time of the year where I miss my family and the general vibe of Diwali in India. But I am trying to build new traditions as one half of an Indian-Austrian couple, which includes trading the traditional boxes of mithai for Manner Schnitten and marzipan. Charu, Germany

double quotation markThere used to be more of a community spirit to Diwali. As kids, we would be tasked with going door to door, exchanging sweets and snacks with neighbours (sometimes to our great displeasure, because it made for awkward conversation with those we had annoyed with our antics). Nowadays, the celebrations are mostly private. Group celebrations are walled off within exclusive clubs, families light their own fireworks almost competitively, and people send each other sweets through a delivery service. Samyuktha, Chennai

The government has approved the sale of a 60.7% stake in IDBI bank to Canadian Fairfax Holdings for Rs 52,878 crore (£4.22bn).

It’s hard to take one’s eyes off Muhammed Riswan when he’s at work. The 23-year-old football freestyler from Areekode in Kerala’s Malappuram district conjures tricks with the ball in a delicately choreographed dance. It glides between his feet, next it is rolling on to his back right before he leaps into the air to catch it with one hand.

Riswan, who’s been freestyling for eight years, is one of the two Indians to win Instagram Rings 2026 award recognising creators. The other is Chanel model Bhavitha Mandava.

Riswan’s big break came with a video filmed at Keralamkundu waterfall, where he pulled off a freestyle move before gracefully arching the ball into the water. The clip has since clocked more than 600m views. Last year, he was invited to watch his sporting idol, Lionel Messi, play in Argentina.

#150. 'They will kill you too': Sonia Gandhi's turbulent journey in Indian politics

Sonia Gandhi has been one of the most powerful political figures in India's history

On the morning of 31 October 1984, Sonia Gandhi was a young wife and mother, living in the prime minister's residence in Delhi. She was about to take a bath when she heard shots ring out in the garden.

Her mother-in-law, Indira Gandhi, India's prime minister, had just left for her office in an adjoining building. Moments later, she would be assassinated by two of her Sikh bodyguards, in retaliation for the Indian army's storming of the Golden Temple in Amritsar, Sikhism's holiest shrine, earlier that year.

The sound was like firecrackers, but "different". The children's nanny came pounding on the bathroom door, hysterical, pointing towards the garden.

Sonia ran towards the path. A white Ambassador car was moving slowly towards her. Inside, Indira lay covered in blood, her sari stained, her eyes open, her face wearing what Sonia remembered as a look of surprise.

"Mummy, Mummy," she tried to say. No words came. At the hospital, she waited for hours before a doctor's shake of the head told her what she feared.

The ordeal did not end there. Rajiv Gandhi, her husband and the elder son of Indira Gandhi, summoned from a rally in West Bengal, arrived to tell her that party leaders wanted him to become prime minister.

Sonia's response was fury and terror. "They will kill you; they will kill you, too!" she pleaded. Rajiv replied: "They will kill me anyway." That evening, as Indira's body lay at home, Rajiv was sworn in.

It is one of the most searing passages in Belonging: A Journey of Love, Sonia Gandhi's new memoir: private devastation unfolding alongside the pressures of public life.

The woman who had largely stayed away from politics was now married to India's prime minister, and would eventually find herself at the head of the Congress party, leading it through two general election victories and becoming one of the country's most consequential political figures.

The passage also foreshadows another devastating loss. In 1991, Rajiv himself was assassinated.

Indira Gandhi was asssassinated by her Sikh bodyguards in October 1984

For Sonia, the years that followed were marked by retreat.

At 19, a young woman from a small Italian town, she had met Rajiv, an Indian student, in England. Their relationship took her into a new country and family, and a close bond with Indira followed. India became home through her husband and her mother-in-law.

After Rajiv's killing, even the public appearances required by the memorial trusts she headed became an ordeal. Before speeches, she feared tears would overwhelm her.

When the Dalai Lama asked whether she felt vengeful towards her husband's killers, she answered, "No, I don't," and burst into sobs. Slowly, she writes, Rajiv became "a presence within me rather than a piercing absence".

But grief returned at family milestones. At her son Rahul's Cambridge graduation in 1995, she and her children were overcome by the thought of how proud Rajiv would have been.

Politics, meanwhile, was closing in. The Congress party, the political vehicle of her husband's family for generations, was losing cohesion and electoral ground. Senior leaders were defecting, and the calls for Sonia to step in were growing louder.

She initially refused. She writes she had seen politics through Rajiv: its achievements, but also its "ambition and greed, hypocrisy and treachery". "I'd always disliked politics," she writes. It had taken first her mother-in-law, then "the love of my life, my children's father".

Yet the party's decline began to feel personal. Walking past photographs of Indira and Rajiv, she was seized by "a mixture of pain and guilt". She feared that the forces they had fought to "protect India's pluralistic ethos" were gaining ground: a rising Hindu nationalism that, in her view, threatened the "secular and pluralistic vision" of India they had sought to defend.

Congress leaders argued that without her, the party could splinter, leaving little to resist communal politics. The family name, she believed, carried an appeal beyond religion, caste, language and region. This, she says, was "the most compelling argument".

Rajiv Gandhi took oath as India's youngest prime minister hours after his mother's death

Her doubts were also about her own credentials. How could an outsider occupy a position held by two formidable figures? At home, Rahul and Priyanka feared losing their mother as they had lost their grandmother and father. Before a New Year holiday, they extracted a promise: "Don't you do anything crazy while we're away."

The "answer", she says, came through the Bhagavad Gita. Though not religious in adult life, Sonia arranged private lessons with a Sanskrit scholar in January 1997. They read the text verse by verse. She writes that its central message â to perform one's dharma, or duty, without attachment to the fruits of one's actions â eventually resolved her inner conflict.

"I had the answer to my own doubts and questions. I had to step out and go where my duty called. And I did."

The memoir's most dramatic political scene, however, comes seven years later, after Congress's 2004 election victory. Sonia had led the party's campaign.

Sonia goes with Manmohan Singh to meet India's president at the presidential palace in Delhi and formally stake the Congress-led alliance's claim to form a government. Everyone assumes she will become prime minister. She says nothing.

At home, she tells a senior aide she intends to refuse. Party workers gather at the gates; senior colleagues demand a meeting.

"You fought the election, you led us," says one. "People believed in you. How can you let them down like this? Our workers will revolt. You think they'll accept another leader?"

Alliance leaders arrive to persuade her. They surround her, she recalls, and one insists: "We will not take no for an answer." Overwhelmed, she murmurs "Theek hai" - all right. They take it as consent and begin congratulating her.

Her children are appalled, she writes. Rahul storms in: "You said yes?" Her daughter Priyanka urges her not to be forced into a decision.

Sonia writes she spends a long night arguing with herself, friends and family. The risk is not merely personal. She believes the main opposition Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) would continue to exploit her foreign birth and that accepting office would allow the controversy to "create havoc" and hurt the country. By morning she is clear. "I've made up my mind. I'm going to stick to my stand," she tells Rahul.

Sonia Gandhi (centre), with daughter Priyanka and son Rahul, at the funeral of Rajiv Gandhi in 1991

She chooses Singh, a trained economist and a party member, instead, describing him as a man "above reproach", with a "deep and consummate understanding of governance".

The book is at its most revealing when it turns from family memory to the India she believes has been lost.

Sonia says that when she entered public life there was "a certain decency and decorum in parliamentary politics".

Political attacks and harsh words were not new, but she sees a sharper appetite among those in power "to belittle and torment those who differ from them, to intimidate those they perceive as opponents". It is, she says, part of a wider international drift towards "a heartlessness that accompanies authoritarian regimes".

Her portrait of prime minister Narendra Modi of the Hindu nationalist BJP is unusually direct.

The two have had "practically no real contact", she writes, beyond formal greetings at parliamentary gatherings and occasional exchanges - "Theek hain, aap?" ("Are you all right?") - when she returned from hospital. "He tends to look away," she says, "avoiding eye contact."

Sonia Gandhi with Manmohan Singh, whom she chose as prime minister after declining the post herself when Congress returned to power in 2004

Modi's idea of India, she writes, was shaped by the Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh (RSS) and "affirmed a fundamentalism in which his faith and culture superseded all others". His is an India of "hierarchy, order, and control"; hers is "an India against hegemony", standing for "inclusiveness, secularism, and social justice".

There is a striking concession amid the polemic - she says she understood Modi's "discomfort with the ruling elite of our times".

Sonia says she felt uneasy in Delhi's privileged drawing rooms, where political conversation seemed "very far removed from the reality of India".

Modi's origins, she writes, "may not be as humble as his PR team" suggests, but he "had certainly worked his way to power within the RSS from an underprivileged world". It is a rare acknowledgement of the social distance between the Congress establishment and the electorate Modi came to represent.

The memoir also offers some glimpses of Sonia's encounters with world leaders.

She remembers Vladimir Putin as an "engaging personality" who took her on a boat ride in St Petersburg, pointing out monuments along the way. She says she was impressed by his grasp of geopolitics and his "determination to restore Russia to its former powerful status" - "long before the Ukraine war, a different era".

Sonia Gandhi writes that she and PM Modi (foreground) have had "practically no real contact"

A more amusing encounter involved France's Nicolas Sarkozy, who arrived in India in 2008 without his then partner Carla Bruni because they were unmarried.

At a state banquet, the Indian vice-president's wife cheerfully grilled him about when he planned to marry her, whether he missed her and whether a date had been fixed. Sarkozy obligingly answered her questions while messaging Bruni on his phone.

Belonging is not a neutral history. The Bofors gun scandal, which badly damaged Rajiv Gandhi's government and dogged the family for decades, gets a relatively brief treatment.

The 502-page memoir is less forthcoming about the less flattering aspects of Congress's own record. Sonia has much to say about political corruption, hypocrisy and the decline of public standards under her opponents, but largely sidesteps the culture of patronage, cronyism and corruption that dogged Congress during its years in power. There is little reckoning, either, with the party's own share of responsibility for the erosion of public trust.

She is at pains to defend the political inheritance of her husband and mother-in-law, even as the party they built has suffered a prolonged decline. The story she tells is as much about the costs of carrying that inheritance as it is about a woman who says she never wanted the life she ended up leading.

That gives the memoir's final pages an unmistakable melancholy. Sonia, 79, is now a member of the Rajya Sabha, the upper house of parliament. Rahul, 55, is a four-term MP and leader of the opposition; Priyanka, 53, is also a member of parliament.

Sonia says the challenge was to remain faithful to "a just and humane society", through pain and loss, victory and defeat, and the manoeuvring that politics demands. But she ends not with triumph, nor even with the assurance that history will vindicate her.

"At this moment it seems to me that, although I tried my best, I didn't succeed in beating back the forces I'd set out to fight."

What will Sonia Gandhi's memoir reveal about India's most powerful dynasty?

#149. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#146. Disgraced former leader Sheikh Hasina plans return to Bangladesh from exile

Former prime minister faces death penalty upon return after being sentenced in absentia over deaths in 2024 uprising

Sheikh Hasina is “confident” she will return to Bangladesh in December, according to exiled members of her party, after the disgraced former prime minister applied for a new passport this week.

Hasina has been living in self-imposed exile since August 2024 when she was toppled from power by a mass uprising against her authoritarian rule. She crossed the border to India, where she has since been allowed to live freely in Delhi.

Last November, a tribunal in Bangladesh found Hasina guilty of crimes against humanity and sentenced her to death over her crackdown on a student uprising that left an estimated 1,400 people dead.

Her return to Bangladesh is likely to be disruptive for the country where Hasina’s party, Awami League, is still banned and thousands of its members are in jail. Should she be allowed to return, she is likely to be arrested immediately.

Hasina first raised the prospect of going back to Bangladesh in August. Speaking to the Guardian this week, several exiled Awami League leaders also living in India confirmed that Hasina was serious and “confident” about returning by next month. They confirmed that several other party figures had already returned in anticipation.

On Monday, a British lawyer acting on behalf of Hasina wrote to the Bangladesh high commission in Delhi requesting a new passport and national identity card to be issued “in a timely fashion”, in a letter seen by the Guardian.

“The reason our leader Sheikh Hasina has applied for a passport is that she genuinely wants to return,” said a party leader based in the Indian city of Kolkata, who spoke on condition of anonymity because he is not authorised to speak to the media on the party’s behalf.

Hasina has rejected the charges against her and claimed the tribunal that convicted her was illegitimate. “She is confident about her return, which is why she has applied for a new passport and a national identity card. If the authorities do not issue her a passport, she will apply for a travel pass to make her return to Bangladesh possible,” said the Awami League leader.

Since Hasina’s conviction, the Bangladesh government has put pressure on the Indian authorities to extradite the former prime minister. However, the Indian government, which was close to Hasina while she was in power, has ignored the demands, straining ties between the two countries.

This week, the Bangladesh government said that, as a “terrorist”, Hasina would only be allowed to return via an “acceptable prisoner exchange” under the India-Bangladesh extradition treaty.

Aminul Islam, the chief prosecutor of the tribunal that sentenced Hasina, said there was “no legal scope” for her to be issued a new passport as she was a “convicted fugitive”.

Islam emphasised that Hasina would be hanged if she returned, as the deadline for her to appeal against her conviction had passed. “Sheikh Hasina has no option but to face the execution of the verdict,” he said.

Despite the abuses and oppression that mired her 15 years in power and subsequent conviction for crimes against humanity, Hasina still commands significant popularity among the thousands of Awami League cadre still in Bangladesh, many of whom have been driven underground.

Analysts said they believed her decision to return was driven by an attempt to revive her support base and destabilise the newly elected government, led by Tarique Rahman. It came to power in February during Bangladesh’s first democratic election in decades.

The government has since struggled with inflation and protests that shut down garment factories, which the Awami League hopes to use to its advantage.

Another Awami League leader said all those in exile intended to follow Hasina back to Bangladesh, despite many of them facing serious criminal charges and having been given lengthy prison sentences in absentia.

“Some of us have been sentenced in absentia by kangaroo courts, and we know we will be jailed. But we are ready to face the consequences and return to our own people. We will fight this politically,” said the senior leader, who is based in India.

Some senior party figures have returned in recent months, including the former MP Mustaque Ahmed Ruhi. He was immediately arrested on his return in September and faces prosecution over an alleged murder during the 2024 uprising.

Exiled Awami League leaders have continued to meet in Kolkata, where many of them are living, and are routinely summoned to Delhi to meet Hasina.

Speaking over email this week, Hasina told the Associated Press: “If death is my destiny, I would rather die on the soil of my own country, among my own people.”

#145. Real Madrid's Vinícius Jr sends surprise message to boy who lost his legs in Sudan's war

Zaher is one of millions of children whose lives have been devastated by Sudan's civil war

In a dark room in Cairo, a 13-year-old boy looks up at a television screen, barely blinking.

The screen flickers and Real Madrid's superstar winger VinÃ­cius JÃºnior appears addressing the young boy directly, in Arabic: "Zaher, mahaba" - Hello Zaher.

Zaher barely moves, his eyes wide, locked on his favourite footballer.

We first met Zaher a year ago in the ruins of Sudan's capital, Khartoum. He was a small quiet child, scarred by war - one of millions of children whose lives have been upended since the country's army and the paramilitary Rapid Support Forces (RSF) started fighting in 2023.

More than 17 million children are now in need of humanitarian assistance in Sudan, according to the UN children's charity, Unicef. It says it has been able to verify that at least 4,300 have been killed or maimed, although it believes the true figure is far higher.

Zaher had lost both his legs in a drone strike. With little access to specialist treatment, he dreamed of the day he might walk and play football again.

More than a year later, as we walk into an apartment in the Egyptian capital, Zaher greets us with an enormous hug. His mother, Habibah, embraces us too.

In his video message, Real Madrid star VinÃ­cius Jr invited Zaher to watch a Real Madrid match together

The boy we remember as shy and withdrawn is almost unrecognisable. He is confident, cheeky and full of smiles. And he is standing tall on two new prosthetic legs.

For almost a year, Cairo has been his home. Here, he has undergone surgery and months of rehabilitation, and is now able to walk and kick a ball again.

When we met in 2025, he had told us his favourite footballer was VinÃ­cius JÃºnior. We were able to pass this on to the Real Madrid star, who said he wanted to send a personal video message of support. Reunited in Cairo, we gather round to watch it together.

"I was made aware of your story. I know how difficult the last few months must have been for you," the Brazilian footballer begins.

VinÃ­cius has become a leading voice challenging racism in football and supporting black people, and a smile spreads across Zaher's face as the player tells him: "You are a hard-working black man. I really hope you will make good use of your new prosthetic legs, that you return to playing football and that you find happiness. You deserve it."

Then comes an invitation: "I would like to invite you to attend a Madrid match. We can spend the day together."

Overwhelmed, Zaher gets up and leaves the room. A few moments later, he returns wearing his new VinÃ­cius Jr football shirt. He hopes that one day he will be able to get a visa and accept the offer.

"Tell VinÃ­cius, I love him," he says. "His message has really uplifted me." His mother Habibah says she feels "like the whole world is cheering Zaher on, wanting him to succeed".

Zaher in 2025 - his legs were amputated after he was hit in a drone strike

Zaher lost his legs in 2024, when he and his mother had been trapped in Khartoum. Much of the city had become a battleground as it was torn apart by fighting between Sudan's army and the RSF.

Food and basic supplies were running short and the family did not have enough money to flee. To survive, they sold lentils on the streets.

Then one morning, as Zaher and his mother worked side by side, a drone struck.

"I looked at him and he was bleeding. There was blood everywhere," Habibah explained last year.

She was devastated. "He would wake up and ask: 'Why did you let them cut off my legs?' I couldn't answer."

Back then, Zaher was wheeling himself through the rubble of his neighbourhood in a wheelchair, past burnt-out cars, abandoned tanks and shattered homes.

Before the war, Zaher had loved football. After the drone strike, he could only play on his knees, a painful process that put him at risk of serious infection.

"I wish I could have prosthetic legs so I can play football with my friends like I used to," he said at the time.

There seemed little prospect of that happening, but his story resonated far beyond Sudan, prompting an outpouring of support from people around the world.

Among them was Dena Latif, a UK-based lawyer of Sudanese origin. After seeing the BBC report, she helped spearhead an online campaign raising Â£45,000 ($60,000) for his medical care.

Unable to get the treatment he needed in Sudan, Zaher had to leave the country, which was far from straightforward. He and his mother had no passports and their birth certificates had been destroyed during the war.

Zaher has learned to use his new legs with months of support from doctors and physiotherapists in Cairo

After months of planning, he and his mother were able to fly to Cairo where an apartment had been prepared for them, with food in the fridge and medical appointments arranged.

For the first time in years, their immediate concern was no longer simply how to survive.

'I can't afford to save both twins': Sudan's war left one mother with an impossible choice

A simple guide to what is happening in Sudan

Zaher underwent further surgery, followed by months of physiotherapy and training to learn to use his prosthetic legs. He also received psychological support to help him process the trauma he had been through.

"He was shy, a little bit depressed, sad," says Dr Bintelmuna Eltayeb , one of the doctors involved in his care. "To get a smile out of him was almost impossible, but now you can see he's laughing. He has great confidence in himself."

His transformation has been as much psychological as physical, say those close to him, and Dena says Zaher now approaches life with a determination that has surprised them.

As we spend time with Zaher, he laughs and jokes, making up for some of the childhood that war has taken from him.

He is eager to try new things, from horse riding to cycling, and wants to become an airline pilot. He hasn't had a game of football yet but is looking forward to playing with his friends back home.

For Habibah, watching her son regain his confidence has brought enormous relief. But she worries about his future, his education and how he will be received in Sudan, a country still locked in civil war.

Khartoum has suffered extensive damage, with many buildings damaged and their contents stolen

The state of emergency has been lifted in their home city, Khartoum, with the Sudanese army back in control there. But throughout the capital, buildings have been left destroyed - burned, blackened and looted.

Concerns over security remain and 41% of Sudan's population are experiencing high levels of food insecurity, according to a recent statement released by the US and 30 other Western states. The group also warned of "potential famine".

"I hope that Zaher's story doesn't end with him," says Dena. She hopes his recovery will attract more attention and funds for the thousands of other Sudanese children who have suffered life-altering injuries.

Zaher and his mother are now back in Khartoum. They hope that as the city is more stable at the moment, he will be able to get his prosthetic legs checked and adjusted there as he grows.

Despite the safety they found in Cairo, they missed their home, family and friends. And while Zaher has been given a chance to reclaim his childhood, millions of other Sudanese children are still waiting for theirs.

Additional reporting by Scarlett Barter and Abdelrahman Abutaleb

#142. Why does Australia want Brits to swap Sydney for the outback?

Paige Tomlinson from Barnsley loves the "chilled" lifestyle of Cairns in northern Australia

In a Mexican restaurant in north-east Australia, the tacos are served by staff from France, Italy, Ireland and Argentina.

Cairns has long attracted a young and transient international crowd, who enjoy the tropical lifestyle while earning money for travel.

But one nationality is in shorter supply than it used to be.

"You get far fewer British backpackers looking for jobs here these days," says Mexicairns owner Peter McLeay.

It's a trend McLeay has noticed since mid-2023, when a free trade deal between the UK and Australia kicked in.

That agreement - as well as tariff-free trade in Tim Tams and Penguins - included special treatment for Brits on working holiday visas: exemption from having to find jobs in regional or remote places if they wanted to remain beyond a year.

That, along with an increase in the age limit from 30 to 35 saw backpacker visas issued to Brits surging from 47,200 in 2023-24 to just over 77,000 in 2025-26, according to the latest Australian government data.

Without the incentive to go regional, many are choosing to base themselves in the big cities, or, according to Prime Minister Anthony Albanese, "Bondi or Coogee" - two of Sydney's most famous beachside suburbs.

Now though, with Brits making up almost a quarter of all 323,000 backpacker visas issued last year, his government wants the free trade deal to be revisited.

Many British backpackers end up in Bondi, PM Anthony Albanese says, instead of exploring the rest of Australia

"Many Brits don't treat it as a working holiday anymore. It's more a three-year career break," says Dan McConnell, general manager of the Mad Monkey backpacker hostel chain.

He says Brits used to be the largest clientele at its Cairns accommodation but "now French outnumber British three to one".

"We see the Brits get to Sydney, decide it's the coolest place in the world, and stay there," he adds.

It's a privilege not enjoyed by working holidaymakers from other countries who must do at least 88 days of "specified regional work" in industries that need a seasonal or temporary workforce - including agriculture, fisheries, tourism and hospitality - if they want to quality for a second year extension, and six months to stay a third year.

Barely a month ago, Albanese ruled out forcing British backpackers to do the same, accepting it as part of the trade deal.

But, under pressure from businesses here in Far North Queensland and other regional Australian communities, who are concerned about a lack of labour, the mood music has changed.

Last week Albanese met UK counterpart Andy Burnham at the meeting of Commonwealth leaders in the Caribbean.

And alongside AI, gender violence online and the AUKUS defence pact, backpackers were on the agenda.

Despite speculation in the Australian media, no immediate announcement was made. Albanese instead told reporters that he had pushed for a change in the rules on backpackers and that Burnham had been "very constructive about it".

"We'll work those issues through over coming weeks," he said adding that the free trade agreement was not set in stone and tweaks could be made "if there are good policy reasons".

Backpackers make up one in seven farm jobs across Australia, with about 52,000 people on working holiday visas doing jobs in horticulture each year, according to the National Farmers Federation.

It welcomed Albanese's discussions with Burnham, but said the issue of worker shortages in agriculture was far broader than the UK deal.

Of greater concern are wider proposals, announced in September, to cut net migration into Australia, under political pressure from the resurgent right wing populist One Nation party and the opposition coalition.

That includes an overhaul of the working holiday visa system, introducing a ballot for backpackers wanting to extend their stay â capped at 45,000 for people wanting a second year in Australia (down from 57,000 who qualified this year) and just 5,000 allowed to remain for a third year (compared with 31,000 this year).

This, the federation says, is "a low blow".

Those plans are still out for consultation, but immigration officials have slowed processing rates for some working holiday visa applications and paused them from several countries.

Tourism Tropical North Queensland chief executive Mark Olsen has also been among those lobbying Albanese in Canberra - telling him that backpackers brought both the spending power and the temporary labour needed to fill seasonal roles in a region famed as a gateway to the Great Barrier Reef and the Unesco world heritage listed Daintree rainforest.

Young Brits temporarily living and working in this region peaked at around 20,000 he says. Now it's closer to 11,000.

"Numbers have dropped significantly since the UK free trade agreement kicked in when border arrangements returned to normal [after the pandemic] in 2023 ," he said.

Peter Inderbitzen (right) relies heavily on seasonal workers including backpackers at his farm in Queensland

Cairns restaurateur Fintan Rafferty says that of about 180 staff on his payroll across four venues, only five are British. Prior to the visa rule change, that was closer to 25.

UK workers came with good skills in hospitality and "were willing to work, and to work flexible hours", he told the BBC.

And, he said, it was a two-way street. "The pay is very good, the conditions are very good. And one of the great things about the 88-days rule is that it forces people to travel. It's enriching their experience."

Bucking the trend, at the bar of Gilligan's backpackers, Paige Tomlinson serves vodka and soda with plenty of ice and a broad South Yorkshire accent.

The 27-year-old, from Barnsley, has been in Cairns for four months, attracted by the "chilled" lifestyle and local waterfalls.

"Most people prefer Sydney and they question me all the time why I'm here," she laughs. "But I got to Sydney and I just didn't like it. It was too busy, too much like London and I don't like London."

She says she'd like to see the regional work requirement brought in for all Brits, "getting them out their comfort zone".

"If every other country's got to do it, why should we not?"

About 200km north, at Red Valley Farm in Lakeland, general manger Peter Inderbitzen agrees.

Workers mainly from Tonga, hired on a Pacific labour scheme, pick bananas there year-round,

But Inderbitzen also relies heavily on backpackers, especially at peak avocado season, employing about 25 people for a couple of months each year.

"It's very short, sharp, get it done, get it out kind of work," he told the BBC. "But without those pickers, the avos stay on the tree."

Ethan Phillips, 23, from Buckinghamshire says the new Australian proposals make sense

While a few British backpackers were still applying for work, numbers had "completely dropped off" since the rule change, said Inderbitzen, who'd "love" to see British travellers having an incentive to head to the regions.

"As much as it wasn't perfect, [that programme] was working."

Back in Cairns, most of the young Brits the BBC tracks down are on tourist visas - but many want to return for a working holiday.

Among them is Ethan Phillips, 23, from Buckinghamshire who has been having way too much fun to keep up to date with what politicians have been discussing.

"It makes sense for them to want us to do [regional work] again," he says. "I mean I'd rather not do it, but if we have to, it's only three months â¦ it can't be that bad."

#141. Iran was behind cyber-attack on French supermarket freezers, Paris says

French foreign minister vows European sanctions against Tehran over ‘unprecedented’ hacks last month

France has said Iran was responsible for a massive cyber-attack on supermarket freezers and chiller cabinets that was described by its foreign minister as being an act of “unprecedented scale and unprecedented aggression”.

Jean-Noël Barrot said “Europe-wide sanctions” would be imposed against those responsible, adding that the attacks – which took control of digital thermostats to push temperatures up to above 50C – constituted “a major violation of the nation’s interests”.

At a press briefing during the G7 summit in Paris, he said: “Thanks to the remarkable efforts of government agencies, I am able to confirm that the Islamic Republic of Iran’s Revolutionary Guards are responsible for this attack.”

He said the Iranian ambassador had been summoned “to protest in the strongest possible terms against this extremely serious act.”

Swathes of French supermarkets were hit in the cyber-attacks on 11 and 12 October. Some were forced to throw away entire inventories of perishable food.

Similar incidents have been reported since, including on supermarket fridges in other European countries last week and on a French blood bank.

The Paris public prosecutor, Laure Beccuau, said on Monday that specialist investigators and government agencies had identified “a modus operandi” characteristic of the Iranian group Shahid Kaveh, a cyber warfare unit linked to the Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps (IRGC).

This included “the use of Farsi and IP addresses traced back to devices located in Iran”, she said.

Beccuau said her office had opened an investigation against the head of Shahid Kaveh, his deputies and “others who will be identified” on charges of “fraudulent attacks on an automated data processing system committed as part of an organised crime ring and of causing damage dangerous to persons”.

She said the offences were “aggravated by the fact they were committed to serve a foreign power – in this case Iran”.

Gérôme Billois, a partner at the cybersecurity and technology company Wavestone, said Shahid Kaveh had been active since 2020 and was known to have carried out cyber-attacks mostly on water distribution systems in the US.

“Two countries are known for this kind of attack: Iran and Russia. Given that recent cyber-attacks on supermarkets and the blood bank in France concerned freezers it is reasonable to think Iran is directly linked to both,” he said,

“For the moment they are attacking easy equipment but as a state group that has the means, the determination and the time, we should be prepared to see attacks on other systems like water or electricity.”

During the attack last month, hackers remotely overrode the temperature setting on thousands of freezers, fridges and chiller cabinets in as many as 700 supermarkets and shops across France, pushing them from as low as -27C (-16.6F) to well over 50C (122F) in some cases.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

Thousands of tons of food had to be dumped and in some stores the extreme temperature rise sparked fires, triggering sprinklers and water damage.

Hackers last week accessed control systems at cold stores stocking blood products. The French blood service (EFS) said temperature and defrosting thresholds and been raised to “very high level” but that technicians had been alerted by alarm systems allowing them to disconnect the cold stores before the products were destroyed. It is not clear whether the two attacks are linked.

The following day retailers in 11 European countries including the UK, Belgium, Estonia, Ireland, Finland, Luxembourg, the Netherlands and Sweden reported problems with refrigeration systems.

A regional branch of France’s Crédit Mutuel bank also reported losing more than €100m in a cyber-attack on Friday. The bank said it had recuperated some of the money.

The European Commission did not immediately comment on the French foreign minister’s statement. Kaja Kallas, the head of foreign affairs for the EU said: “We have the foreign affairs council next Monday, so we will have the opportunity to discuss this. These cyber incidents occurring in Europe, if attributed [to Iran], also call for a strong response on our part.”

In a significant decision this year, the EU designated the IRGC a terrorist organisation, ending years of division over the issue. The designation became possible after France dropped its long-held opposition, having previously feared it would damage diplomatic channels and the interests of French nationals in Iran.

#138. Witnesses recount struggle to save people buried in ‘horrifying’ Gaza landslide

High winds and heavy rain triggered collapse of sand bank overlooking camp for displaced families

Witnesses have described the frantic struggle to save three members of a family buried by a landslide triggered by high winds and heavy rain in Gaza.

A sand bank overlooking a camp for displaced families south of Deir al-Balah collapsed on Sunday, killing five people and crushing at least three tents.

“It was a horrifying sight,” said Abdul Karim al-Sahhar, 30, originally from Izbat Beit Hanoun in northern Gaza. “We saw people buried beneath the sand and rocks. None of them was moving or making a sound. Even the tent had disappeared completely. There was no trace of it left.”

Among the casualties were an eight-year-old, a six-year-old and their mother.

Sahhar, whose own shelter was badly damaged in the landslide, said the children’s father was outside the family tent and so survived. “One of his children had called him to come and eat with them,” he said. “He was a little late returning, and then the collapse happened.

“The father started shouting: ‘Save my children! Save my children!’ People came and started digging [but] there was a huge boulder on top of the victims. The young men had to break it into pieces so they could reach the people buried underneath.”

Abu Darbali, another witness, said the collapse of the embankment sounded like an explosion. “We found that three tents were covered completely after the mountain collapsed on top of them,” he told Reuters. “People were under the rubble and we couldn’t see them.”

Video posted to social media showed people using hammers to break large rocks and others removing sand with shovels, using flashlights to search for those trapped underneath.

The UN said more than 26,000 households had reported damage to shelters or belongings after the weekend’s rainstorms.

The vast majority of Gaza’s population of 2.3 million live in tent encampments or the ruins of bombed-out buildings, ‌with reconstruction yet to begin after Israel’s relentless two-year military assault launched after the Hamas attacks on southern Israel in October 2023.

“The collapse [this weekend] was waiting to happen. [Gaza] is extremely overcrowded. People are just trying to find a place wherever,” said Toby Fricker, a spokesperson for Unicef, which has been working to prevent similar tragedies and improve drainage in camps for displaced people.

In a statement, the Hamas-run government media office said about “70,000 tents sheltering tens of thousands of displaced people were flooded on the first day” of rains. It also noted the “collapse of dozens of cracked walls and roofs, which caused injuries”, some critical, in various areas of the tiny territory.

A US-brokered ceasefire last year called for the eventual reconstruction of Gaza, but little progress has been made. Negotiators have failed to agree on terms for Hamas to disarm or for Israel to withdraw its troops, which control about two-thirds of the territory ‌from which residents have been ordered to leave.

Israel says no reconstruction can happen before Hamas is disarmed.

Sahhar said he and his family were now homeless. “My tent and everything I own are now buried under the sand. My mattress, household utensils and all my belongings – everything was buried. I could not retrieve anything,” he said.

Mustafa Qazzaat, a member of the Gaza City council, said Israel had not allowed prefabricated homes to shelter the displaced since the truce was announced, and only permitted the entry of limited numbers of tents.

Many so-called “dual use” items remain restricted, such as metal tent poles. Humanitarian workers said they instead had used wooden poles fashioned from palettes used to transport food.

Cogat, the Israeli defence ministry body that oversees aid to Gaza, said more than 91,000 tents and over 8,200 trucks containing tarpaulins, blankets and winter clothing had reached Gaza, as well as convoys carrying supplies of fuel and engine oil to power pumping facilities and other humanitarian installations.

Earlier this year, an assessment compiled by shelter specialists in Gaza revealed that thousands of tents supplied by China, Egypt and Saudi Arabia to shelter displaced Palestinians offered only limited protection against rain and wind.

#136. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#133. Meet the Peaky Blinders, Ukraine's drone unit protecting a devastated front-line city

The BBC's Jonathan Beale reported from inside Slovyansk, one of four cities that make up Ukraine's "fortress belt" - a system of heavily reinforced defence lines in the parts of eastern region of Donetsk still under its control.

The town has become almost uninhabitable and has been under heavy bombardment from Russian drones, missiles and glide bombs.

Members of a Ukrainian drone unit that call themselves Peaky Blinders, named after the cult BBC TV series, have taken on the task of intercepting Russian drones.

Russia wants Kyiv to give up the fortress cities and they are central to talks on a possible peace deal that have resumed after nine months between Ukraine, Russia and the US in Abu Dhabi.

#132. Trump says US war in Iran ‘effectively over’ as he plays down escalation concerns

President, after talks with Qatari PM, suggests end to war coming ‘fairly soon’ and military escalation not needed

Donald Trump has said that the war in Iran is “effectively over” as he played down concerns that the US would escalate its military campaign against Tehran after the midterm elections.

During a White House press conference, Trump said that Israel and Gulf allies in the region were well protected from Iran and that an escalation was not needed.

“At this point, taking the military action doesn’t seem to be a very necessary thing – but we’re locked and loaded, ready to go for any reason,” he told reporters during a briefing at the White House.

The remarks followed talks in the Oval Office with the Qatari prime minister, Sheikh Mohammed bin Abdulrahman Al Thani, who has lobbied Trump alongside other Gulf countries not to resume the fighting against Iran.

“We’re seeing momentum toward the end of this conflict. I think it’s going to be ending fairly soon,” he added.

Trump has frequently exaggerated claims about the status of negotiations with Iran, saying a final peace deal was imminent dozens of times during the nearly nine-month long war.

The US had continued to move troops to the Middle East in the run-up to the midterm elections. There was speculation that the Trump administration was waiting for the elections to take place before resuming a military campaign, in order to prevent a backlash at the polls.

Republicans were nevertheless routed in a historic defeat in which they lost both houses of Congress, with the vice-president, JD Vance, declaring soon after that the Iran war was a key reason for the defeats.

Trump earlier on Monday said on Truth Social that the leaders of Qatar, Saudi Arabia, UAE, Bahrain, and Kuwait would prefer the “status quo, in other words, a continuation of the Greatest Naval Blockade in History”.

He repeated a mantra that Iran would not be allowed to develop a nuclear weapon, and added that if the situation changed then the US was ready to resume full-scale military action against Iran.

“At this point, however, that seems to be quite unlikely,” he continued. “Israel, and the entire Middle East, are protected from Iranian danger and threats. We are seeing positive momentum toward an end to this Conflict, and will ensure that America’s interests are at all times protected. At the conclusion of this Military Engagement, the American People, and the World, will be far safer and more secure.”

#128. New Knesset speaker could be key to ousting Netanyahu as Israel’s PM

Opposition politicians get first chance to take control of levers of power, even as they struggle to form government

Israel’s new Knesset will be sworn in on Tuesday, giving opposition politicians their first chance to take control of some levers of power, even as they struggle to form a new government to unseat Benjamin Netanyahu.

High on the agenda will be replacing the speaker, who wields immense influence by controlling the legislature’s agenda and committee chairs. The current speaker is Amir Ohana, a close ally of Netanyahu.

He will be replaced by Matan Kahana, from the Yashar party led by Gadi Eisenkot, the former Israeli military chief who got the presidential mandate to try to form a coalition government at the weekend.

Kahana, a former minister, fighter pilot and special forces officer, has the explicit backing of all four Jewish Israeli parties in Eisenkot’s “change” bloc. The two other Palestinian-led opposition parties have said replacing Ohana is a priority so are expected to give Kahana their votes.

The opposition parties hold 67 seats in the 120-seat legislature, enough to install a consensus choice for speaker. They will request a vote on Tuesday afternoon, and Ohana then has up to a week to bring it to the floor.

Avigdor Lieberman, who heads another opposition party, is nominated to head the foreign affairs and defence committee. It will play a central role in drawing up planned legislation covering the conscription of ultra-Orthodox men, a key campaign issue for Lieberman.

The effort to wrest away control of the Knesset is significant enough that more than 160 former top security officials, including heads of the military, intelligence and police, warned at the weekend that rightwing activists were planning to disrupt the day with violent attacks.

“Actors who oppose replacing the Knesset speaker intend to go to the Knesset on the day of the swearing-in ceremony and to prevent the MKs and their family members from entering the building,” a forum of retired commanders said in a letter to serving security chiefs.

“These same actors are encouraging tens of thousands of citizens who support their stance to come and to help them with their mission,” Ma’ariv newspaper quoted the letter as saying.

All opposition parties have described Netanyahu as a threat to the state and are keen to start moving against him in the Knesset, despite divides blocking the formation of a coalition.

Several of the Jewish-Israeli opposition parties in Eisenkot’s bloc have sworn not to sit with the Palestinian-led parties, which hold more than a quarter of the bloc’s seats, or even join a minority government that has their backing.

Eisenkot has vowed to broker a national emergency government, promising to achieve it with “creative and unconventional political acts”.

In a sign of challenges ahead, he effectively warned that some election promises would have to be broken to unseat Netanyahu. “No one will be able to fulfil all the [campaign] promises they made, and all of us will need to compromise. That is also leadership, to know when to decide and set priorities,” he said.

There have been protests across Israel urging Jewish-Israeli opposition parties to put unseating Netanyahu ahead of a racist commitment to excluding Palestinian-led parties from government.

Palestinian citizens of Israel make up about 20% of the population and voted in unprecedented numbers this year, giving the parties 19 seats. Without them, the opposition bloc would have fewer seats than Netanyahu’s diminished coalition.

Lieberman has been trying to seek an alternative path to government through an alliance with ultra-Orthodox parties.

They have long been Netanyahu allies but have been frustrated by his failure to deliver on the promise to pass broad military draft exemptions for their community into law. Lieberman has campaigned hard for universal conscription, but appears to prefer compromise on this issue to working with Palestinians.

“Has the End of Times arrived?” the leading Haredi (ultra-Orthodox) journalist Arye Erlich wrote in a column for Yedioth Ahronoth newspaper.

“Has Avigdor Lieberman … the biggest persecutor of Haredim Israeli politics has seen in decades, suddenly come to believe that he can compromise on a military draft bill and sit in a coalition government alongside the Haredim?”

Even if Eisenkot does broker a coalition deal, he may delay announcing it until Netanyahu’s speaker has been replaced. Netanyahu is a master of political survival and last-minute deals, fracturing past coalitions by luring defectors into his own alliance.

Ohana could delay signing in a new government for up to a week, and some of the opposition do not want to give Netanyahu that much time to attack the pressure points of any fragile deal.

If Eisenkot cannot form a government, the mandate is passed to other leaders, and if they fail, Israel must hold elections again. In the meantime Netanyahu and his far-right allies would stay on as a caretaker government.

Although Netanyahu is calling for his opponents to join him in a unity government, a long period of limbo followed by a new vote offers him a likelier path back to power.

If an alternative government took power, it would be harder for Netanyahu to destabilise it, said Gideon Rahat, a chair in political science at the Hebrew University of Jerusalem.

“Once they establish a government, they might be a minority government, it might be hard for them to do things, but nevertheless, they will be governing and it would be hard to oust them.”

#127. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.
