#123. Israeli opposition hurries to seize control as new Knesset is sworn in

Former military chief Gadi Eisenkot has mandate to try to form a government – with Netanyahu waiting if he fails

Opposition lawmakers have wasted no time after Israel’s new Knesset was sworn in, deploying their majority to seize control of the legislature from Benjamin Netanyhau and his allies.

Although they are still too divided to form a government, the opposition requested a vote for a new speaker, who sets the Knesset’s agenda. Once Netanyahu loyalist Amir Ohana has been replaced, the six opposition parties will be able to work on key legislation even if they fail to broker a coalition.

Priorities include setting up a state commission of inquiry into the 7 October 2023 attacks and introducing term limits for Israel’s prime ministers, which could block Netanyahu from taking power again. He has ruled Israel for 19 of the last 30 years.

Gadi Eisenkot, a former Israeli military chief, now has the presidential mandate to try to form a government. If he fails to broker a deal within six weeks, a second candidate – probably Netanyahu – will get a second chance. Further failure is likely to lead to another election.

Netanyahu responded to the swearing in of the new Knesset on Tuesday with a racist attack on two Palestinian-led opposition parties, whose voters were essential to unseating his coalition, calling their lawmakers “terror supporters”.

Eisenkot denounced his speech as a “delusional, divisive, hate-filled and shameful” exploitation of a state occasion, that showed the prime minister was unfit for office.

Yair Golan, leader of the centre-left Democrats, and Yoav Segalovich, a Jewish-Israeli former police chief who ran on the slate of Islamist Ra’am party, both walked out of Netanyahu’s speech in protest.

Palestinian citizens of Israel make up about 20% of the country’s population. Netanyahu put attacks on these voters and their parties at the heart of his campaign, and since polling day has promoted groundless accusations of fraud in their communities in a bid to undermine results.

Election officials say there was no evidence of irregularities that affected the election outcome or compromised its integrity.

A period of prolonged, difficult coalition negotiations or even a new round of elections would extend Netanyahu’s hold on power as caretaker prime minister. But if the fractured opposition can make progress on key legislation, his grip on the state would be weakened.

Eisenkot’s Yashar party submitted a series of bills to the Knesset moments after the swearing in, with backing from other opposition parties.

In addition to term limits and the 7 October inquiry, legislation includes several bills designed to revoke the “judicial overhaul” pursued by Netanyahu, which weakened the power of Israel’s judiciary and the attorney general.

Ohana has tabled the vote on a new speaker to replace him for next Tuesday, the latest date possible. The opposition have also nominated candidates to take over key defence and finance committees.

Eisenkot has admitted the path to power is “extraordinarily complex”, because of incompatible election promises made by different parties, but described ousting Netanyahu as a national emergency that could require breaking promises.

At the swearing-in ceremony, the president, Isaac Herzog, warned lawmakers they should do everything in their power to avoid a second election.

“Reach out a hand for compromise, even between ⁠rivals … Look upon one another with generosity. There is much work to be done here, and it requires the ability and experience found in every part of this House.“

#120. California police investigate after 27 fall ill at half-marathon running event

Thousands took part in the event on a hotter-than-usual day in Santa Barbara

Police in Santa Barbara, California, are investigating after nearly 30 people had to seek medical care at a half-marathon running event held in the city on Sunday.

In a statement provided to the BBC's US partner CBS News, officers said they received a report from Santa Barbara Cottage Hospital about race participants who sought medical treatment "for unusual symptoms" after the race ended.

"The cause of the reported symptoms has not been confirmed," the statement added.

Cottage Health, which operates the hospital, told CBS it had seen "a larger than expected number of emergency visits" from those who ran the race.

"There is no specific action recommended for the public at this time; however, participants who feel unwell should seek medical attention and follow guidance from healthcare professionals," police said.

Cottage Hospital said it treated 27 people from the half marathon at emergency rooms in Santa Barbara and the nearby city of Goleta.

The vast majority of them - 24 - were discharged after treatment, while three people were still in hospital as of Monday in stable condition.

Several thousand people ran the half marathon on a hotter-than-usual day in Santa Barbara along the Pacific Ocean. Temperatures reportedly reached 86F (27C) by midday, compared to a typical early November temperature of low 70s.

One of the runners who went to hospital told local news outlet KEYT-TV that they had lost consciousness and could not remember parts of the race.

"It is awfully suspicious," said the runner, who declined to be identified. "We had people saying they were hallucinating."

"I am not too familiar with heat exhaustion but I have never seen anyone not remembering a long portion of their run," the runner added.

Event director David Monico told US media that 15 people were taken from the medical tent to local hospitals because of dehydration and overheating.

The BBC has contacted the Santa Barbara Police Department, Cottage Health and the half marathon organisers for further comment.

#115. Iran rejects French claims it was behind supermarket fridge hacking

Shelves were left bare by the cyber-attacks

Iran has described French accusations that it was behind cyber-attacks that raised the temperatures of supermarket fridges and freezers as "baseless and hostile".

On Monday, the French authorities blamed Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) for the string of hacks, which foreign minister Jean-NoÃ«l Barrot said were "unprecedented" in terms of their scale and "level of aggression".

He added that those responsible would be hit with sanctions.

The French cyber-attacks, which led to the destruction of tons of frozen and chilled food, were followed by a similar hacks against supermarkets across several other European countries. It is not known if the instances are linked.

Barrot said the attack on France's supermarkets "marks the first large-scale cyber-attack aimed at sabotage on our national territory, and it constitutes a major attack on the nation's interests and the security of the French people".

He said the government had summoned the Iranian ambassador to Paris to "lodge a very strong protest against this extremely grave act".

France will take "any other necessary and proportionate measures" to respond to the incident and "deter anyone who might seek to repeat it", Barrot added.

The Paris prosecutor, Laure Beccuau, said an investigation had been opened into the head of an offensive cyber unit associated with the IRGC as well as his two deputies.

Beccuau named the unit as Shahid Kaveh, which she said had an affiliation with the electronic and cyber centre of the IRGC.

Shahid Kaveh is the name given to a cluster of hackers thought to be directly employed by the Iranian regime.

In recent weeks, a number of commentators and politicians in Iran have argued that the country should extend any retaliation beyond the Middle East if the US and Israel resume the war they started earlier this year.

On Monday, some Iranian media outlets denied the French accusations, but there had been no official comment until Tuesday's statement from Iran's foreign ministry.

On 11 October, hackers gained control of the software that regulates the temperatures of the refrigeration units used in many of France's supermarkets.

More than 100 of CoopÃ©rative U's supermarkets were hit, as well as 180 stores from IntermarchÃ© and its Netto brand. In some stores, temperatures of the fridges reportedly rose to 50C (120F) overnight.

Other major food retailers including Carrefour, Monoprix and E.Leclerc were also hit by the attack, which targeted software provided by Danish engineering firm Danfoss.

The software allows retailers to manage their cold units remotely.

Earlier this month a second wave of hacks took place, hitting more than 100 stores across Europe.

Supermarkets in Belgium and Luxembourg were affected, while a smaller number of shops were hit in countries including Sweden, Ireland, the Netherlands, Estonia and Finland.

Separately in August a small number of US military bases had fridges mysteriously malfunction, spoiling food. This incident is also being investigated as deliberate cyber-attacks, Reuters news agency has reported, citing sources familiar with the matter.

Since the waves of attacks on freezers, experts have been speculating which hacking groups could be responsible, with Iran being on a list of potential suspects.

In spite of a pair of Russian-aligned hacktivist groups claiming they were responsible on Wednesday, few believed them.

The French government has not said how it reached the conclusion that Iran was responsible, and attribution of cyber-attacks is extremely hard as hackers are able to hide their locations.

Additional reporting by Farshad Bayan, BBC Persian.

#114. Russian naval aggression prompts new defensive initiative with ‘beer-drinking nations’

New alliance with the Netherlands and Baltic and Nordic countries comes in response to 41% increase in Russian naval activity around UK

Russian naval activity around the UK has increased by 41% in the first nine months of the year, a British defence minister has said, as the Royal Navy announced plans to step up a 10-country maritime defensive effort in response.

Luke Pollard, the minister for defence readiness, described the figure as “a huge increase”, justifying a new drone and warship initiative from “the alliance of beer-drinking nations” – the UK, the Netherlands and the Baltic and Nordic countries.

The plan is for sea drones from the UK and other countries to be able to work interchangeably with warships from countries across the Northern Navies Initiative at a time when the number of British frigates is falling.

“The model that we want is where British autonomy [drones] can be teamed with a British warship or a Danish warship or a Norwegian warship and can hand that autonomy off to an allied nation’s warship,” Pollard said.

A future Royal Navy would combine warships and drones “because that would increase our lethality”, Pollard said, sidestepping a debate about the number of UK frigates – which could drop from 12 in 2021 to three by 2030.

Last month, the cross-party defence select committee warned that the navy risked failing to deter Russian aggression in the seas around the UK because of shortfalls in the number and availability of warships and submarines.

Pollard blamed the Conservatives for capacity shortfalls, accusing them of cutting budgets and delaying investment when they were in government. “Now, because of the decisions taken 10, 15 years ago, we have fewer ships and fewer submarines than I would like available,” the minister said.

An extra £10bn a year allocated to defence in 2030 by the chancellor, John Healey, in October’s budget means that the UK could “send a clear demand signal to industry”, the minister said, to help tackle the Russian threat.

However, since the budget announcement, there have been no major defence spending announcements, with the UK sticking to the defence investment plan announced in June and fully funded in the budget. That included a £1.5bn investment in sea drones over four years.

Russian spy ships, such as the Yantar, have been sailing near UK waters this year trying to obtain “an increased picture of our critical national infrastructure”, Pollard said, as well as “mapping our undersea cables”.

Its frigates have also been active in the North Sea and the Channel throughout 2026, often escorting oil tankers and other trade vessels, and at one point firing shots near a pleasure yacht south of the Isle of Wight in June.

Oil tankers linked to Russia remained active, though were now often “sailing around the UK” to avoid coming through the Channel, Pollard said, after the seizure of one Russia-linked tanker, the Smyrtos, in the same month as the shooting incident.

The minister insisted that, if necessary, the Royal Navy was “ready to fight tonight”, even though it took three weeks for the UK to send a destroyer to Cyprus in the spring after the UK base at Akrotiri was struck by a drone fired by an Iranian proxy.

Russia’s northern fleet, based in the Barents Sea, retains “a really formidable undersea presence”, Pollard said, adding that Moscow had continued to invest in it throughout the Ukraine war as a means of threatening the UK.

The minister was speaking at the navy’s International Sea Power conference held on the Prince of Wales aircraft carrier in Oslo. Sailing the warship to Norway was “a really important deterrent signal that we stand ready to defend every single inch of Nato territory”, he said.

A further £90m will be spent on underwater drones and sensors to monitor the Greenland-Iceland-UK gap for Russian submarine activity, considered to be a strategic choke point in what the navy describes as the “high north”.

Ukraine has used cheap sea drones to destroy about a third of Russia’s Black Sea fleet, forcing it to relocate from Sevastopol in occupied Crimea to Novorossiysk, on the eastern Black Sea.

The former head of the country’s military, Valerii Zaluzhnyi, now Ukraine’s ambassador to the UK, said the increasingly complex use of sea drones meant that “it is likely that the world’s navies are on the verge of the most radical transformation since the rise of torpedo technology”.

#112. Trump says FBI investigating Nvidia boss's affair with alleged Chinese spy

"He's suffering": Trump answers BBC question about Nvidia's Jensen Huang

US President Donald Trump has said the FBI is investigating the affair between the chief executive of Nvidia and an alleged Chinese spy.

Trump said he spoke to Jensen Huang on Monday following reports that the US believed he had been targeted in a Chinese espionage campaign.

"I asked him that question, 'Did you have any idea she was a spy?' He said 'I had no idea she was a spy' and I really believe him," Trump told reporters.

Beijing has rejected claims that Huang was targeted, with its foreign ministry saying the allegations were "entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China".

Speaking later at a White House press briefing, Trump told reporters, "I know the FBI is looking into" the affair but did not give further details on the investigation, or confirm when it was launched.

Trump added he thought that Huang was an "honourable guy and I feel very confident that he will be in good shape" after the investigations.

The FBI declined to comment when contacted by the BBC.

Nvidia, which has commissioned an independent investigation into the affair involving Alice Huang, alleged to be a Chinese spy, said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised.

Nvidia is the world's second most valuable company after Apple, and designs computer chips that have become central to AI technology.

The revelations knocked about $250bn (Â£190bn) off Nvidia's value on Monday as the company's share price fell by more than 4%.

US intelligence officials believe Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report. She is not related to Jensen Huang.

According to the newspaper, Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington in spring this year, warning that the Nvidia boss was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Alice Huang was managing director of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has had dealings with Nvidia.

Trump's remarks are the first indication of whether Nvidia's Huang knew about the woman's alleged ties to Chinese espionage.

Megaspeed's office in Singapore's central business district appeared to be operational and staffed on Tuesday.

The BBC was refused entry to its floor, but spoke to a staff member by phone who said they had no comment and directed all queries to the company's PR team.

The BBC has emailed Megaspeed several times for comment.

Nvidia, co-founded by Jensen Huang, is also a major focus in the AI technology race between the US and China, with officials in Washington treating its exports to Beijing as a matter of national security - a key reason why Huang's affair has sparked concerns.

Also on Monday, Huang and Nvidia were sued in a proposed class action lawsuit in California that accuses them of defrauding shareholders by concealing his ties with the suspected Chinese spy.

The lawsuit alleges that the company lied to the media about Jensen Huang's relationship with Alice Huang, "who is suspected of seeking to help smuggle Nvidia products to China."

The BBC has contacted Nvidia for comment on the lawsuit.

At the White House, Trump was asked several times when he was made aware of the relationship. He said he was informed "over a period of time" but was not specific.

"I hope there's no problem, I believe he is suffering greatly," the president added.

He said he spoke to Huang on the phone on Monday "just to see if he's feeling alright, how you doing and I asked him about that and he said he absolutely had no idea that the person in question was a spy".

Trump has raised concerns that allowing China to lead in AI would supercharge Beijing's military and surveillance capabilities.

The president added he was "sure" Chinese leader Xi Jinping would "ask me about it" when they next met.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Huang has admitted that he was "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

He said on Monday that he was "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security" and would cooperate with the investigation the company had launched.

Huang has also been requested to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees next week, however it is unclear whether Huang will appear.

A letter said the briefing would be "on Nvidia's security protocols against foreign adversary espionage and your interactions with a publicly reported People's Republic of China (PRC) intelligence asset: Megaspeed executive Alice Huang".

Nvidia has declined to comment, while the the BBC has also contacted the committees.

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

AI stocks climb after trillions wiped from market values

Some workers the BBC spoke to at Nvidia's Santa Clara headquarters on Monday said it was business as usual, despite the intense attention on the company.

Nvidia has been around for decades, with Huang founding it with two other engineers in 1993, but its rise to one of the world's most valuable and powerful companies has only been recent.

It originally designed computer chips for gaming, but its decision to shift focus to AI development turned it into the powerhouse it is today.

Its chips have become so valuable to the AI buildout that the White House previously imposed controls on where its advanced products could be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

But Huang has long been against restricting sales to China, arguing that it would be better for the global rollout of AI to be powered by US technology.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

Nvidia's most advanced AI semiconductors including the GB200 Blackwell super chip remain blocked from the Chinese market.

Additional reporting by Tessa Wong in Singapore

#110. Britain willing to discuss paying into EU to join electricity market, says minister

Hamish Falconer says UK has ‘not accepted principle’ of linking electricity trade to ‘cohesion fund’ but two sides will discuss issue

Britain is willing to discuss paying towards infrastructure schemes in poorer parts of the EU in return for access to the European electricity market, according to the minister in charge of renegotiating the bilateral relationship.

Hamish Falconer, the cabinet minister in charge of EU talks, told the Guardian he was sure the two sides would discuss the issue after this month’s summit, as ministers look to deepen ties with the union over the next few years.

Andy Burnham, the prime minister, has said he wants a much closer relationship with the EU in the future, even raising the possibility of rejoining as a full member. But while senior figures in Brussels have said they are keen to discuss such an idea, they also want to see the UK begin to start paying for access to the European schemes it says it wants.

This weekend five European leaders welcomed the UK’s desire to join the European electricity market, but said Britain would have to pay into a so-called “cohesion fund” in order to do so.

While UK officials say they do not accept the link between the electricity reforms and cohesion fund, the minister admitted the UK would enter into talks about additional contributions.

“We have not accepted that principle,” Falconer said. “I understand that that’s what the European Union have asked for, but the UK government hasn’t accepted. Again, this is something no doubt we will discuss.”

Falconer has said he hopes next week’s keenly anticipated EU-UK summit “will go some real distance in undoing some of the harm that Brexit has done”. Negotiators hope to sign deals on food and drink, carbon emissions and youth mobility.

Any move to bring Britain into the EU electricity market, however, is likely to prove controversial. The EU cohesion fund promotes economic development in poorer regions in the bloc and UK officials will be cautious about any deal in which British taxpayers’ money could be spent on infrastructure projects in central and eastern Europe.

Falconer was, however, clear that the UK would be keen to join a future EU defence fund. Last year, negotiations on Britain’s entry into the EU’s €150bn (£125bn) defence fund collapsed after a dispute over the UK’s contribution.

The UK had been seeking to join the first round of the EU’s Security Action for Europe (Safe) fund, a low-interest loan scheme that is part of the EU’s push to grow defence spending by €800bn.

EU officials suggested an entry fee of up to €6bn, significantly higher than the administrative fee the government had anticipated, and talks collapsed. “I think it’s a real shame that the UK was not part of that scheme,” Falconer said on Monday.

“The terms on which we were asked to join were clearly not terms that we could agree and I think that’s a shame – and I think lots of our friends in the EU think it was a shame … so I hope there is an opportunity for a Safe2 and one in which the UK could join on reasonable terms.”

Falconer also said he hoped negotiators would agree a youth mobility scheme ready for the 20 November summit, though there have been crucial sticking points over demands for home tuition fees for EU students at UK universities.

The Guardian reported last week that the UK was prepared to spend up to £450m a year to give EU students discounted university tuition fees, if Brussels allowed Britain access to the Made in Europe industrial programme.

“Obviously I understand that there are a number of member states who would like to see it made easier for EU students to come to the UK and have lower fees than they currently pay if they want to go to British universities,” Falconer said on Monday.

“But that is outside of the common understanding so it will require a different set of arrangements than were envisaged when these negotiations started. Clearly it’s something that on the EU side there’s real interest, so we are engaging in that discussion but I can’t comment in detail on the negotiations.”

Falconer was firmly positive about the UK’s return next year to the Erasmus+ student exchange scheme, which the UK left under Boris Johnson’s Brexit deal in 2020, and will be open not just to universities but also to schools and colleges.

“I hope around the start of the summit we will be opening applications for British young people to put themselves right in the heart of Europe,” he said, rejecting Nigel Farage’s criticisms of Erasmus and the youth mobility scheme as opening the door to uncontrolled immigration.

“People understand that it is a good thing for our young people to be able to travel widely in Europe, to be able to study, to have the kind of exchanges that make such a big difference to people’s lives.

“I think honestly Nigel Farage is being a big old grinch about this. This is in the interests of our young people and he should give his head a bit of a wobble, I think.”

#109. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#108. 'Cruise for life' ship seized in Singapore

The arrest comes weeks after the cruise came to a sudden end, leaving 200 guests stranded in Cambodia

A round-the-world cruise that left passengers stranded in Cambodia after its US-based operator ran out of money has been seized in Singapore.

The Odyssey, which set off from Belfast in October 2024 and was meant to take guests on a years-long voyage around the world, is now anchored in Singapore port waters, local authorities say.

Around 200 guests were forced to leave the ship last month in the Cambodian port city of Sihanoukville, after its owner Villa Vie Residences abruptly suspended operations citing "extraordinary increases in fuel costs".

The crew onboard are reported to be well, Singapore's port authority said.

"Arrangements for their welfare and wellbeing are being managed by the vessel's creditors," the authority said, adding that the vessel had been "placed under arrest pursuant to a court order".

Singapore law firm Helmsman LLC has been named the "arresting solicitor", but it is not clear which party it represents.

The Odyssey was meant to go on a three-and-a-half-years voyage covering 150 countries.

Passengers were given the options of buying their cabin outright, which allows them to stay onboard beyond its initial voyage, paying for a five-year residency, or renting a cabin for days-long tours.

Permanent residencies in its cabins ranged from $120,000 (Â£89,272) to $440,000. Those cabins were also available for rent from $3,500 a month, according to its website.

"For roughly the price of a car, you can own your home on a ship that travels the world," its founder Mikael Petterson had earlier said.

However the cruise was beset with problems from the start, with its departure delayed for four months because of repairs and paperwork.

While in Vietnam, crew members reportedly bought food from local grocery stores because the company owed money to a vendor who refused to deliver food, according to CBS News, the BBC's partner in the US.

CBS also reported that the operator warned passengers of its financial troubles in August, when it had sailed on a separate occasion into Singapore, and had to raise money from guests to keep the cruise going.

It had also mentioned the risk of being detained then.

Holly Hennessey, one of the ship's passengers, had earlier told the BBC guests were asked to consider paying a fuel surcharge as prices soared, and that their itinerary had been "severely curtailed".

The vessel sailed again into Singapore earlier this month, weeks after the cruise came to a sudden end while in Cambodia.

As the Iran war sent fuel prices soaring earlier this year, monthly fuel cost per cabin in parts of Asia had risen to as much as $8,000 from $1,800, Villa Vie Residences said in a statement on 27 October.

It added that it had taken "significant steps to reduce fuel consumption" but those measures turned out to be insufficient to offset the "extraordinary increase in fuel costs".

The company also said it was working with residents to "identify temporary living arrangements" while in Cambodia.

#106. French police raid Ritz hotel in Mohamed Al Fayed sexual abuse investigation

Arrests made after three properties were searched in relation to inquiry into late businessman

French police have made arrests after raiding three properties, including the Ritz hotel in Paris, in relation to an investigation into sexual abuse and trafficking by Mohamed Al Fayed.

Several individuals were taken into custody for questioning last month and later released, according to the Paris public prosecutor’s office. Another property in Paris and one in Saint-Tropez, both with links to Fayed, were also searched in mid-October.

Several of Fayed’s alleged victims were also interviewed by officers from the organised crime division of the Paris prosecutor’s office.

It added that the searches were part of a preliminary investigation into “allegations of human trafficking committed by an organised group, aggravated pimping committed by an organised group, and rape”.

In an email sent to survivors of Fayed’s alleged abuse earlier today, the Metropolitan police said its officers were present during the searches on 13 and 14 October, in a coordinated operation with French investigators.

The Paris prosecutor’s office said: “Searches and evidence-gathering operations were carried out in mid-October 2026, notably within the Hotel Ritz in the 1st arrondissement of Paris, as well as at the Villa Sainte-Thérèse in Saint-Tropez, a former property of Mohamed Al Fayed, and at the Villa Windsor in the 16th arrondissement of Paris, in the presence of British investigators.”

The investigation is ongoing and digital material seized during the searches is being analysed, it added.

One of the properties that was raided was Castel Sainte-Thérèse in Saint-Tropez, one of the most exclusive villas on the French Riviera, and where Diana, Princess of Wales, stayed with her sons in the summer of 1997, at the start of her relationship with Fayed’s son, Dodi.

The other was Villa Windsor, a former holiday home of the Fayed family. The property in western Paris was previously the family home of Charles de Gaulle, before he was president of France, and King Edward VIII, after his abdication, and his wife, Wallis Simpson.

A Met spokesperson confirmed its officers travelled to France last month and were present during the operation.

Campaign groups representing Fayed’s alleged victims called on the Met police to conduct its own searches of the late billionaire’s properties.

Co-chairs of Justice for Fayed and Harrods Survivors, Jen Mills and Lindsay Mason, said: “We want to see the same thoroughness applied to every property where survivors say they were taken or abused, including here in Britain. The searches in Paris and Saint-Tropez must be the beginning, not the end.”

A spokesperson for No One Above, a collective of survivors of Fayed’s alleged abuse, said: “The French authorities have put this in the hands of their specialist trafficking unit and their organised crime prosecutors, and are investigating it as trafficking by an organised gang.

“The evidence in this case was never confined to Harrods. For two years, the Met has avoided treating it as what France recognised from the outset: organised trafficking by a network. The National Crime Agency should now take over the investigation.”

Pelham Spong, who says she was trafficked in Paris on Fayed’s orders in 2008 before being sent to London to be sexually assaulted by him, said she was overjoyed by the French police raids. “Survivors deserve actions, not words,” she added.

Last November, she told French investigators from the Central Office for the Repression of Human Trafficking that Fayed, who owned the Ritz hotel in Paris, had forcibly kissed her and offered her a salary of €65,000 a year if she slept with him.

Survivors’ groups say more than 500 women have come forward about abuse that allegedly took place at Harrods, Park Lane, Fulham football club, the Ritz hotel in Paris, and other places owned by Fayed. The Met is investigating 169 victims who have contacted the force directly.

The Ritz Paris told Agence France-Presse news agency that it “does not tolerate any form of harassment, abuse or inappropriate behaviour” and that it was ready “to cooperate fully with the competent judicial authorities”.

#104. DR Congo sentences 66 soldiers to death for being 'cowards'

The Congolese army has been fighting rebels for decades

At least 66 soldiers have been sentenced to death in the Democratic Republic of Congo for cowardice, mutiny and refusing to obey orders, local media report.

The sentences followed a trial of 71 soldiers before a military court in Walikale, in North Kivu province, in eastern DR Congo. Four soldiers were acquitted, while another was sentenced to 10 years in prison.

The army said the trial was intended to improve discipline among troops as they battle armed groups in eastern DR Congo.

The region has endured decades of conflict, with fighting continuing between government forces and the Front for the Refoundation of the Nation (FRN), a rebel coalition that includes the M23 group.

The soldiers were reportedly arrested on 20 October in Walikale, where they were accused of abandoning their positions and fleeing during clashes with FRN fighters.

The Congolese army's campaign against armed groups, including the M23, has long been undermined by poor discipline and disorganisation within its ranks.

The government has repeatedly accused neighbouring Rwanda of supporting the M23, an allegation denied by both Kigali and the rebels despite overwhelming evidence.

The army has faced persistent criticism over its lack of professionalism and disciplined, while soldiers have complained of poor pay and inadequate equipment.

Last month, another 27 soldiers were sentenced to death on similar charges of cowardice after reportedly withdrawing from the frontline during clashes with rebels.

In March 2024, DR Congo lifted a 20-year moratorium on the death penalty, citing the need to remove "traitors" within the army.

The government said capital punishment would also apply to people involved in armed groups, insurrection, as well as committing treason and war crimes.

The decision to reinstate the death penalty was widely criticised by rights groups. However, no formal executions have been carried out since the moratorium was lifted.

Since the beginning of 2025, at least 507 soldiers have been convicted, including 381 death sentences, according to figures compiled by French broadcaster RFI.

In October 2025, former Congolese President Joseph Kabila was sentenced to death in absentia on charges including war crimes and treason. He denied the accusations, but did not appear in court to defend himself. He has since been named as the FRN's leader.

#103. Federal judge allows Trump’s $10bn defamation lawsuit against BBC to proceed

Trump-appointed judge Jeffrey T Kuntz partially denies British broadcaster’s motion to dismiss over edit

Federal judge Jeffrey T Kuntz ruled on Tuesday that Donald Trump’s lawsuit against the BBC can proceed, denying in part the British broadcaster’s motion to dismiss the case. The president’s $10bn defamation lawsuit stems from the BBC’s splicing together of disparate comments he made before the 6 January US Capitol insurrection, as rendered in a Panorama documentary called Trump: A Second Chance.

“Accepting the allegations in the complaint as true, the Court finds Trump plausibly pled damages where Trump alleges a defamatory documentary released toward the election caused reputational harm,” the Trump-appointed Florida district court judge wrote. “It would be difficult for the Court to accept a different framing without completely negating the significance and fragility of political elections.”

The judge noted the significance of the documentary’s editing decision, which spliced together comments made by Trump that were nearly 55 minutes apart, something the BBC has apologized for.

“On its face, the editing may independently establish the plausibility of actual malice,” he wrote. “BBC tries to frame this editing as ordinary editing done to condense a seventy-minute-long speech into twelve seconds. But the Court is not persuaded.”

The judge continued: “Editing is often required. However, given the time disparity between the two statements, the distance itself tends toward a plausible inference that BBC knew or should have known that the segment they were creating would not and could not have been a truthful statement. This was not trimming around the edges but creating something different than what was stated in the speech.”

The judge noted past errors in the broadcaster’s coverage and wrote that “such examples can be said to be probative of BBC’s state of mind as it pertains to recklessness in light of BBC’s non-action toward false statements about Trump. Thus, Trump paints a picture that demonstrates the BBC has a record of allowing the publication of false statements in their portrayal of Trump.”

The judge also wrote that “Trump’s allegations regarding BBC’s departure from journalistic standards are more than enough to plausibly alleged more than mere negligence”.

The BBC released a statement: “We are surprised by this decision but respect the Court’s authority and note the case will now proceed to the next stage. We remain confident in our position and will continue to respond through the proper legal process.”

Trump heralded the ruling on Truth Social, calling it a “huge victory”. “We will continue fighting against, and defeating, all of those who try to deceive the American People!” Trump said.

While the judge determined that Trump had “sufficiently pleaded” that the BBC acted with actual malice, the legal standard for a public figure to win a defamation case, he will have to actually prove they did so at trial.

The judge also dismissed Trump’s claim that the BBC violated Florida’s Deceptive and Unfair Trade Practices Act.

And the judge determined that the president will have to prove that he suffered “actual damages” from the airing of the document, which was not broadcast in Florida.

In late October, a Florida magistrate judge ruled that Trump will have to sit for a deposition conducted by lawyers for the BBC by 11 December or else he could be hit with sanctions.

#101. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#99. Police officer dies and another injured after backpack explosion in northern Spain

A police officer has died and another has been injured in the northern Spanish city of Oviedo after an explosive device went off at the scene of a shooting.

The blast happened when officers from Spain's canine unit and explosives disposal unit arrived and examined a suspicious backpack following reports of a dispute between neighbours on Tuesday morning.

A woman who had been shot and the injured officer have been taken to hospital for treatment.

The suspect fled the scene and is still at large, police said.

Spanish police paid tribute to the dead officer, who has been named as Juan JosÃ©, in a statement on X and said: "Our hearts are with his family, friends, colleagues and loved ones."

Officers attended the scene early after receiving reports of an argument between neighbours and upon arriving found a woman who had sustained gunshot wounds, police spokesman Pedro Aguado said.

While searching for the suspected attacker, officers found a suspicious backpack with a flashing light and contacted both the bomb disposal unit and canine unit.

"While the package was being moved outside to ensure the safety of the residents, an explosion occurred," Aguado added.

The explosion left a member of the canine unit dead and an officer from the bomb disposal unit seriously injured, he said.

A three-year-old police dog named Deny was also killed in the explosion.

A widespread police operation is ongoing near the scene of the explosion and officers are continuing to hunt for the suspect.

#97. Rising Tide banned from using council land for protests at world’s largest coal port in Newcastle

NSW premier Chris Minns warned council would be complicit in any ‘illegal activities that occur at these protests’

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

Newcastle council – home to the world’s largest coal port – has banned a climate group from holding any events on its land after consulting the New South Wales premier, Chris Minns, who warned the council would be complicit in any “illegal activities that occur at these protests”.

On Tuesday night the council’s independent lord mayor, Gavin Morris, used his casting vote to ban any licensing of Rising Tide’s events, claiming in a mayoral minute that the group had “broken the trust and faith of the Newcastle community” by defying police directions during past protests.

Rising Tide holds an annual event where thousands of protesters wade into the port with kayaks and rafts over the course of the six-day protest to blockade coal ships. The protest includes camping on council land and a music festival where major Australian bands have played, including Midnight Oil frontman Peter Garrett.

The protest is run to draw attention to the group’s demands for the immediate cancellation of all new fossil fuel projects, the end of coal exports from Newcastle by 2030, and taxing fossil fuel profits at 78% to fund community and industrial transitions.

During the 2024 protest, the Minns government attempted to stop the event from going ahead after it put in place around the port a four-day maritime exclusion zone, which criminalised entering the water. But just as the protest was kicking off, the supreme court found the government had acted improperly and ruled the exclusion zone invalid after Rising Tide challenged it.

During the event 173 protesters were charged, but in August police withdrew charges against 125 of those people.

For the 2025 blockade, the government and the police did not make an attempt to stop the protest from going ahead. Police arrested and charged 129 protesters who entered the water at the 2025 event, which stopped three coal ships from entering the port. They remain before the courts.

The group has a smaller paddle-out event planned for this Saturday ahead of their annual protest, which will next be held in February.

The group were before the NSW supreme court on Wednesday for the Saturday event after the police knocked back their application for a form 1 permit. The court is expected to decide later on Wednesday if it will support the police’s decision to not provide the protesters protection from being charged under the Summary Offences Act.

Minns wrote to the council ahead of the motion being passed saying it was “not possible for the council to distance itself from the illegal activities that occur at these protests”.

He wrote that the protest is only possible through the council’s licensing of the events at a local park.

“While I completely understand the right to protest and the need to protect lawful protest, we need to ensure that protests occur in a safe way, and not in a manner which disrupts shipping traffic at the Port of Newcastle,” he wrote.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

“Rising Tide has a history that indicates their intent is to disrupt the shipping channel of your harbour without regard for the law.

“I acknowledge the independence of your council to make decisions. However I believe that formally endorsing this protest will put lives at risk and make NSW police’s job impossible.”

The minister for the Hunter and police, Yasmin Catley, who was also consulted on the motion, wrote to the council supporting its decision, labelling Rising Tide “bad faith actors”.

“They flouted the ruling of the supreme court in 2024 prohibiting their assembly and ignored the statutory requirements for holding a protected protest entirely in 2025 resulting in 141 arrests.”

A Rising Tide spokesperson, Zack Schofield, said: “Desperate attempts like this to suppress protest are proof that Rising Tide has been effective in building pressure against the world’s largest coal port.

“We intend to exercise our right to protest because the world is burning, it is flooding, our climate is collapsing and time is running out. Paddle for the Planet will go ahead this Saturday at Horseshoe Beach, as will future protests.”

#95. Foo Fighters interrupt Sydney concert to pay tribute to Beaconsfield miner Todd Russell

Dave Grohl tells Sydney crowd Russell, whose death was announced this week, was a ‘big’ hero of his

Todd Russell, Beaconsfield mine collapse survivor, dies aged 54 due to illness

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

A solemn moment has broken up an otherwise raucous Foo Fighters Australian concert as the band’s frontman made a lengthy on-stage tribute to Beaconsfield survivor Todd Russell.

The US band are touring Australia for the 16th time in their 32-year history and Tuesday night’s Sydney show coincided with the news of Russell’s death at the age of 54.

The Tasmanian mine collapsed on Anzac Day 20 years ago and trapped Russell a kilometre below ground with co-worker Brant Webb.

The pair were presumed dead in the disaster, which killed a third miner, Larry Knight.

The two survivors were trapped in a vehicle underground and their 14-day rescue effort became global news.

When the duo were asked what supplies they needed, Russell asked for an iPod with Foo Fighters music to keep his spirits alive as loud drilling took place for the rescue effort.

The Foo Fighters frontman, Dave Grohl, told the Accor Stadium crowd his band had a funny relationship with the Beaconsfield miners.

While the duo were trapped, Grohl sent a message to say he had two tickets and two beers waiting for them for any Foo Fighters show when they got out of the mine.

He kept his promise and met the pair backstage at the Sydney Opera House show later in 2006.

Grohl wrote the duo a song the night before, called Ballad of the Beaconsfield Miners.

At Tuesday’s show, band members left Grohl standing on stage as a picture of Russell appeared on screen and the singer recalled their get-together 20 years earlier.

“Afterwards we all went to the bar and we had the beers I promised, and I got so drunk I promised I’d put [Ballad of the Beaconsfield Miners] on our next record,” he said.

“And I did. I’m glad I did because I’ve met a few heroes in my life but that was a big one, so I’m doing this one tonight for Todd Russell.”

Russell’s death came one week after he announced a move home to “see out his days” following an illness.

#94. Arrests made in Mohamed Al Fayed probe in France, police say

Hundreds of women have come forward with abuse allegations against the former Harrods and Ritz Paris owner

French police have made arrests after three properties, including the Ritz hotel in Paris, were searched in relation to an investigation into Mohamed Al Fayed's abuse.

The Paris prosecutor's office told the BBC that several individuals were taken into custody for questioning last month and later released.

In a confidential letter sent to survivors, the Metropolitan Police said its officers were present during the searches on 13 and 14 October, in what is understood to be a joint operation between British and French authorities.

It is the first time any arrests have been made in relation to alleged abuse and trafficking by the former Harrods and Ritz owner.

Mohamed Al Fayed accused of multiple rapes by staff

Several victims of Al Fayed were also interviewed by French investigators, the prosecutor's office said.

It added that the searches were part of a preliminary investigation into "allegations of human trafficking committed by an organized group, aggravated pimping committed by an organized group, and rape".

The investigation is ongoing and digital material seized during the searches is being analysed.

The late Egyptian billionaire bought the Ritz Paris hotel in 1979, and it is still owned by the Al Fayed family.

The Villa Windsor in western Paris, another property involved in the search, was a holiday home for the Al Fayed family. It was previously the home of King Edward VIII, following his abdication, and his wife Wallis Simpson.

The Met shared this statement from French authorities with victims:

"At this stage of the proceedings, several victims have been interviewed by French investigators, and the investigations are being conducted in liaison with the British authorities.

"In this context, searches and evidence-gathering operations were carried out in mid-October 2026, notably within the Hotel Ritz in the 1st arrondissement of Paris, as well as at the Villa Sainte-ThÃ©rÃ¨se in Saint-Tropez, a former property of Mohamed Al Fayed, and at the Villa Windsor in the 16th arrondissement of Paris, in the presence of British investigators.

"Several suspects were taken into police custody, measures which were lifted following their interviews, specifically for the purpose of analyzing digital material seized during the search operations.

"The investigations are continuing under the direction of the Paris Public Prosecutor's Office."

A Met Police spokesperson confirmed its officers travelled to France in October and were present during the operation.

The Ritz hotel in Paris is still owned by the Al Fayed family

Al Fayed, who died in 2023 aged 94, was accused by more than 20 women of sexual assault in a BBC documentary and podcast in September 2024.

One of the women featured in the documentary said she was sexually assaulted by Al Fayed at the Ritz hotel in Paris, while another woman said she had been raped by him at the Villa Windsor residence.

Since those allegations, more than 400 alleged victims or witnesses have come forward to lawyers.

A woman has also told BBC Scotland she was trafficked, drugged, sexually assaulted and held prisoner on the Balnagown Estate by Al Fayed's brother, Salah Fayed, who died in 2010.

Both brothers died without facing legal charges in relation to these allegations.

Earlier this year, Police Scotland said it was carrying out further investigations into allegations connected to Balnagown Estate.

In a statement, a force spokesperson said: "Three people have been identified in connection with offending against a number of victims.

"Our inquiries are ongoing and it would be inappropriate to comment further."

The Justice for Fayed and Harrods Survivors group said it welcomed the "significant developments" and cooperation between the Met and French authorities.

"We believe that all properties owned by Fayed should be investigated with the same thoroughness as those already searched, wherever they are located, to ensure that no potentially relevant evidence is overlooked," the group said.

"We hope these developments represent meaningful progress towards identifying and holding to account all those who facilitated, concealed or assisted his offending, wherever they operated."

The survivor advocacy group No One Above said: "France is treating this as organised human trafficking, led by specialists, with properties searched and evidence seized. That is what a serious investigation into a network looks like.

"Two years on, survivors are still waiting for the same from the Met. It is time the National Crime Agency took this investigation over."

The management of the Ritz Paris told AFP news agency that it "does not tolerate any form of harassment, abuse or inappropriate behaviour" and that it was ready "to cooperate fully with the competent judicial authorities".

#91. Underworld figure Mick Gatto gave CFMEU official gold bracelet after construction site assault, Big Build royal commission told

Former CFMEU official Paul Tzimas tells inquiry he suffered fractured ankle and a colleague was left with major injuries and severe PTSD

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

A former CFMEU official has told a royal commission that underworld figure Mick Gatto attended a meeting that was called after the union official and a colleague were seriously assaulted at a construction site, claiming the underworld figure later offered him a gold bracelet.

Paul Tzimas is the first witness associated with the Construction, Forestry and Maritime Employees Union to appear before the royal commission into the integrity of major public and civil infrastructure construction projects in Victoria.

Tzimas told the commission on Wednesday that he received a fractured ankle and a colleague was left with a broken eye socket, damage to his teeth, severe PTSD and the partial loss of his vision after they were assaulted by people linked to the builders and developers at a construction site.

No other details were given about where and when the assault occurred, but Tzimas agreed that he believed the people who assaulted him may have been linked to an outlaw bikie gang.

Tzimas, a former CFMEU official who left the union in January and is now unemployed, told the commission that about a week after the assault a meeting was called between union officials and the builders, who also arranged for Gatto to attend.

He said Gatto was known to work for developers and builders in order to resolve issues with their projects.

Tzimas said that Gatto later gifted him a gold bracelet, which he initially refused.

He agreed with counsel assisting Chris Carr that he first knocked back the gift as he knew it could be “perceived as inappropriate for someone in your position to receive a gift from someone on the other side of the fence”.

Tzimas also agreed with Carr that he knew the bracelet was not a “worthless trinket” but that, after discussing with Gatto why he was being gifted it, he decided to take it.

“Mick had expressed that – especially because I refused it – that it wasn’t his way of … trying to earn any favour.

“He said he had had a poor opinion of me as an official, he believed he probably judged me a bit harshly, and this was an olive branch so to speak, for people he considered a friend.

Tzimas said he was aware other union officials also received gold bracelets from Gatto.

Tzimas was also asked about a letter the CFMEU wrote on behalf of a man convicted of manslaughter who was seeking parole that confirmed he would be offered employment.

He told the commission that he was unclear why the union assisted the man but said “his dad at the time was a union delegate … so that probably holds some sway as well”.

Carr also read the names of 15 men to Tzimas and asked whether all of them were union members and affiliated with bikie gangs. Tzimas agreed that 11 of them were.

A level crossing removal project at Gap Road in Sunbury, north of Melbourne, is the focus of the commission’s November hearings.

Tzimas attended the work site as part of union action that eventually halted construction.

He also attended a meeting between project managers and the union officials at CFMEU headquarters on 7 June 2022 that eventually resulted in work recommencing on the site.

The commission has previously heard that in that meeting the union demanded that its own health and safety representative, and union affiliated traffic management and labour hire companies, had to be installed on the Gap Road project in order for work to resume.

The hearing before commissioner Christopher Kourakis continues.

#90. US federal authorities could have prevented Epstein crimes, report finds

Jeffrey Epstein died in a New York prison cell in 2019

Crimes committed by the late disgraced financier Jeffrey Epstein after 2005 could have been prevented were it not for "failures" by federal investigators and prosecutors, a congressional investigation has concluded.

The Republican-led US House Oversight Committee on Tuesday released its final report into what it described as the "decades-long" mishandling of the criminal investigations into Epstein and his long-term associate Ghislaine Maxwell.

The report's findings are based on interviews and depositions with witnesses, government files, and financial records over a 15-month period.

Epstein died in 2019 as he awaited trial on sex trafficking charges.

"This is my way of trying to breathe," says Joanna Harris on speaking about Epstein for first time

"While some of these failures involved an exercise in poor judgement by the USAO, others appeared to be the result of Epstein's pervasive and wide-reaching influence," the report reads, referring to the US Attorney's Office.

It states that, rather than fully investigating allegations in other states, the USAO for the Southern District of Florida entered an agreement with Epstein in 2007 allowing him to evade federal prosecution.

'There is no justice' - Epstein survivor on how model agency sent her to abuser's home

Regarding Epstein's death in a New York prison, investigators concluded this was a result of "a series of institutional failures at the facility," which included staffing shortages, overworked employees, poor recordkeeping, and lax security practices.

Testimony the panel solicited was "unable to definitively dispel certain theories" surrounding Epstein's death, the report states, including accounting for a lapse in surveillance footage.

It found the justice department (DoJ) failed to communicate about document releases and that deficient redactions exposed survivors' sensitive personal information.

The DoJ has released millions of pages of Epstein-related files in response to legislation from Congress.

Earlier this year, the department had to remove thousands of documents related to Epstein from its website after victims said their identities had been compromised.

The report also alleges that financial institutions like American Express enabled human trafficking and immigration fraud, by allowing Epstein to use decoy flights and dummy itineraries to transport foreign workers across borders. The BBC has contacted American Express for comment.

The investigative panel recommended that Congress should pass legislation to prevent the weaponisation of non-disclosure agreements, which they said Epstein used to silence survivors.

"Epstein operated with impunity, shielded and enabled by powerful connections and a justice system that repeatedly failed his victims," said James Comer, the Republican chairman of the committee.

He called upon the DoJ to "interview or re-interview key witnesses, investigate Epstein associates accused of criminal activity, and ensure survivors have access to their records.

The department remains under bipartisan scruntiny regarding the Epstein files.

In the state of New Mexico, where Epstein had a ranch, the top state prosecutor accused the DoJ of stonewalling their criminal investigation.

"With all due respect, the Chairman's recommendations have already been addressed," a spokesperson for the department said in response to Comer's report.

"The Attorney General has repeatedly said that the Epstein case is not closed and there is no statute of limitations on human trafficking."

The report accuses Democrats of being "obsessed" with trying to implicate US President Donald Trump and that an "unfounded witch hunt continued without regard for the interests of survivors".

Trump has previously said he was friendly with Epstein before they fell out in 2004. The president's name has appeared thousands of times in the Epstein files but there is no suggestion that appearing in the documents implies any wrongdoing.

The committee's review of documents did not identify any that would implicate Trump, the report concluded.

In a statement, Rep Robert Garcia, the top Democrat on the Oversight Committee, alleged his Republican colleagues "released a report based on an incomplete investigation and zero public hearings on Jeffrey Epstein".

Garcia accused the justice department of withholding "over 2.5 million pages of documents".

"We will not allow the President and his allies to dismiss this investigation," said Garcia, who is expected to lead the committee when Democrats take control of Congress in January following the recent midterm elections.

The family members of one of Epstein's most prominent accusers, the late Virginia Roberts Giuffre, thanked Comer for releasing Tuesday's report.

"A sentence from the report stands out to us: 'Questions remain as to whether the DOJ has released all its relevant documents.' We could not agree more and urge that all 7 million-plus files be released," Sky and Amanda Roberts said in a statement.

"There are many more individuals who have yet to be identified and brought to justice," they said.

#88. Labor won’t scrap ABC and SBS ombudsmen in favour of external body as suggested by antisemitism envoy

Exclusive: Jillian Segal suggested ‘transferring’ powers in updated report released a fortnight ago but government sources note media regulator already exists

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The Albanese government won’t follow a suggestion by the antisemitism envoy to scrap the ABC and SBS ombudsmen and replace them with an external oversight body.

In a list of updated priorities released last month, Segal proposed an “external independent ombudsman” to monitor ABC and SBS coverage of the war in Gaza and to ensure publicly funded broadcasters were “held accountable to their own stated standards of accuracy and impartiality”.

“Many in the Jewish community remain concerned that coverage they perceive as one-directional may contribute to a negative – and even hostile – view by audiences of those who support Israel, or have sympathy for it,” Segal said in her updated plan to combat antisemitism.

Segal said the government should consider “transferring” responsibilities from the ABC and SBS ombudsmen to an “external independent ombudsman consistent with other sectors such as telecommunications and banking”.

The ABC said at the time its complaints process was “the most robust and transparent in the Australian media” and both broadcasters stated they were subject to external review by the Australian Communications and Media Authority (Acma).

This week, government sources noted that Acma already acted as an external independent regulator that could investigate complaints if they needed to be escalated beyond the ABC and SBS ombudsmen.

A spokesperson for the federal communications minister, Anika Wells, on Wednesday thanked Segal for her work and said there was no place for antisemitism in Australia.

It was an evil “that tears at the fabric of our country … [and] puts the peaceful, free and equal society we cherish at risk”, they said.

It is understood Labor could consider changes in response to recommendations from the royal commission into antisemitism that is scheduled to report in mid-December. The commission, headed by former high court judge Virginia Bell, was established after the antisemitic Bondi beach massacre in December 2025.

“The government will soon receive the final report from the Royal Commission into Antisemitism and Social Cohesion,” Wells’ spokesperson said on Wednesday.

“[It] will continue to work with the special envoy, the Jewish community and all Australians to combat antisemitism and strengthen our social cohesion.”

Segal’s latest suggestion regarding the ABC and SBS received a mixed response a fortnight ago.

Alex Ryvchin, the co-chief executive of the Executive Council of Australian Jewry, welcomed Segal’s suggestions, warning meaningful oversight of coverage by public broadcasters could only be achieved “through external accountability”.

But the progressive Jewish Council of Australia criticised the envoy’s new plan as “Trumpian”, arguing it threatened freedom of speech and democratic rights.

The Australian Palestine Advocacy Network criticised attempts to conflate legitimate criticism of Israel with antisemitism and stated: “Speaking out against Israel’s ongoing atrocities is the prerogative of anyone who believes in human dignity and human rights.”

Segal in October wrote that the “independence of public broadcasters must, of course, be protected”.

“That independence, however, does not – and should not – amount to a right to be free from external scrutiny and empirical review. Indeed, ensuring that the processes withstand fair scrutiny can only increase confidence.”

The ABC ombudsman has previously investigated public complaints related to coverage of the Middle East and the war in Gaza, including the Israeli president’s visit to Australia and a press club address by the Israeli ambassador.

ABC Alumni, which represents hundreds of former staff, has said that since October 2023 the ABC ombudsman has upheld four out of thousands of complaints of bias or inaccuracy relating to Middle East coverage.

Acma, “to which dissatisfied complainants can appeal, has upheld none”, the group said in a statement, adding it was “outraged at the continued insistence by Jillian Segal … that the ABC and SBS, Australia’s most trusted news outlets, are contributing to antisemitism”.

In her first report in mid-2025, Segal initially proposed the special envoy be given powers to monitor media organisations to encourage “accurate, fair and responsible reporting” to ensure impartiality and balance and to avoid “accepting false or distorted narratives”.

She then told the royal commission into antisemitism in mid-2026 she wanted a new “oversight” committee that could give ABC and SBS coverage a “tick” or “guidance”.

Segal told Senate estimates this week she was concerned about “the tone [and] the frequency of reporting about Gaza from the ABC”.

#87. Twelve dead after motorway pile-ups in Bulgaria fog

Burnt out and crushed vehicles were strew across the motorway in the aftermath of the crash

Twelve people have been killed after dozens of cars and lorries were involved in two pile-ups on the Trakia motorway in south-eastern Bulgaria, Prime Minister Rumen Radev has said.

Health Minister Katya Ivkova said 11 people died at the scene on Tuesday, near the town of Zimnitsa, while another person later died in hospital. About 50 others were taken to hospital with injuries.

Sliven regional governor Mihail Kasherov told journalists that more than 70 vehicles were involved in the incident, Bulgarian state news agency BTA reported.

Officials have said dense fog reduced visibility and was the likely cause of the first crash, which occurred on the eastbound carriageway.

Radev declared Wednesday a day of national mourning following the incident.

The second crash, on the Sofia-bound carriageway, was likely also caused by the fog, with drivers also distracted by the first crash, BTA reported an official as saying.

The Trakia motorway runs from west-to-east of Bulgaria, and connects the capital Sofia to the Black Sea coast.

Road administration director Galin Kostov added that speeds were unsuitable for the conditions, which included wet road surfaces, and the motorway would remain closed until it had been cleared, BTA reported.

Yambol regional governor Georgi Chalakov said reports first came in at 07:10 local time (05:10 GMT), with the injured taken by ambulance to hospitals in the Yambol, Sliven and Burgas regions.

Chalakov told BTA rescue teams had to remove sections of the crash barrier to reach people. He added that the fog meant that helicopters could not be used in the evacuation efforts.

A drone captured the scene from above, just visible through the dense fog

One witness who helped rescue a man from a burning vehicle blamed "very thick fog" for the pile-up.

"I got out of the car, I ran forward and pulled that man out," the witness told Reuters news agency.

Another told Reuters that "many, many people died".

"As for me, there were four cars behind me - trucks - and I don't know how I got out or what happened, but in the end, thank God I'm still alive," that witness said.

Radev - who had been hosting European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen - cut short a news conference after learning of the incident and visited the scene.
