#85. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

The former US President was in Dublin to see his son Hunter awarded the Gold Medal for Outstanding Contribution for Public Discourse

#82. Makers of GLP-1 drugs ordered to add warnings about rare eye condition that can lead to blindness

The US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) told makers of popular weight-loss GLP-1 drugs that they must add warning labels about a rare eye condition that can cause sudden blindness.

Manufacturers of Ozempic, Wegovy, Mounjaro and Zepbound must warn about nonarteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (NAION). The disorder happens when vessels supplying blood to the optic nerves become blocked, depriving the nerves of oxygen.

The condition can cause painless decreased vision in one eye, which can be permanent, the FDA said.

In a statement to the BBC, the American Academy of Ophthalmology said "any added risk appears small, and it has not been established that these medications cause NAION".

"GLP-1 medications offer important benefits for people with diabetes, obesity, and heart disease, so our message to patients is simple," the group said. "Don't stop your medication without talking to your doctor, and if you notice sudden, painless vision loss in one eye, see an ophthalmologist promptly."

There is no known treatment to reverse vision loss caused by NAION. It is the most common cause of acute optic nerve injury in people over 50 years old, according to the academy.

The FDA said it conducted a detailed review of reports of NAION in GLP-1 users as well as a review of other studies and concluded there is an "increased risk of rare NAION events" in people taking the drugs.

In a statement, Novo Nordisk, the manufacturer of Wegovy, one of the most popular weight-loss medications, said the company believes the data "do not establish a cause-and-effect relationship".

"We recognize that NAION can have a profound impact on patients and their families. Patient safety is a top priority for Novo, and we continue to monitor the safety of our medicines," the manufacturer said.

Some research was challenging to review because people taking GLP-1 drugs for obesity and type 2 diabetes may already have risk factors for NAION separate from the drugs, according to the regulator. The FDA said it was not known why those taking GLP-1 drugs may be more at risk for the eye condition.

The drugs already have warning labels for several other conditions including pancreatitis and gallbladder disease. Some of the most common symptoms for semaglutide - a key ingredient in many GLP-1 drugs - include nausea, vomiting and stomach pain.

â Last year, a safety arm of the European Medicines Agency, a part of the European Union, said up to 1 in 10,000 people taking semaglutide may be affected by NAION.

In a study published two years ago analysing 17,000 patients, US researchers found those taking semaglutide were four times more likely to be diagnosed with NAION than those not taking the drug.

But one of the authors of the study, Harvard Medical School ophthalmology professor Dr Joseph Rizzo, said the findings were "significant but tentative", and more research was needed on the relationship between the drug and the eye condition in a more diverse population.

#80. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#77. David Jones to close half its stores, triggering mass job losses in ‘urgent restructure’

Australia’s oldest department store collapsed into voluntary administration last week after years of steep losses

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

David Jones is set to shutter half of its department store network, triggering mass job losses under a sweeping restructure, the appointed receivers said on Wednesday.

David Jones employs more than 6,000 staff across 38 stores.

The receivers FTI Consulting said in a statement that it would proceed with an “urgent restructure of the business”.

“As part of this restructure, the receivers have commenced an immediate consultation process for all staff associated with 19 stores that have been identified for closure,” FTI said.

“For the immediate term these stores will continue trading and while there is no final date for the closures, we anticipate they will cease trading by the end of the year.”

The closures comprise seven stores in New South Wales, three each in Victoria and Western Australia, two each in Queensland and South Australia, one in the ACT, and David Jones’s sole New Zealand store in Auckland.

Under a dual appointment, receivers FTI has day-to-day control of operations, while administrators McGrathNicol seek a buyer or fresh injection of capital.

The upheaval follows years of steep losses after Australia’s oldest department store failed to carve out a profitable niche in a sector disrupted by online competitors and stand-alone upmarket stores.

The company collapsed into voluntary administration last week. The David Jones chief executive Erica Berchtold told suppliers the business could not continue operating as it was.

“Put simply, our store portfolio is no longer the right shape for the business, and this change is about creating a network that is financially sustainable for the future,” Berchtold said in correspondence viewed by Guardian Australia.

As David Jones’s primary secured lender, Hilco Global holds a decisive role in the retailer’s fate, having appointed receivers FTI to protect its financial interests.

Hilco will place its workers inside the stores marked for closure from Thursday.

“These representatives will have a greater presence in the stores that are closing to assist in accelerating sales and promotional activity at a store level,” Berchtold said in the correspondence.

In documents released late on Tuesday, David Jones’s administrators revealed they reviewed the private equity-owned department chain’s finances months before it fell into administration.

Some small suppliers are owed money dating back to stock sent in April, well before David Jones missed rent payments and fell into administration.

Its private equity owner, Anchorage Capital, has long sought a slimmed down version of David Jones, free from long-term leases on underperforming sites. It is expected to make a proposal to administrators to repurchase the 188-year-old chain.

The following stores will be closed: Carousel, WA; Garden City, WA; Glen Waverley, Victoria; Green Hills, NSW; Hay Street Mall, WA; Highpoint, Victoria; Hornsby, NSW; Macarthur Square, NSW; Macquarie Centre, NSW; Malvern Central, Victoria; Marion, SA, Newmarket, NZ; Parramatta, NSW; Robina Town Centre, Queensland; Sunshine Plaza, Queensland; Warringah Mall, NSW; West Lakes, SA; Woden, ACT; Wollongong Central, NSW.

#76. French wartime resistance heroine Solange Alexandre dies aged 104

Solange Alexandre twice turned down the LÃ©gion d'Honneur before relenting in 2024

One of France's last Resistance heroes from World War Two has died at the age of 104. Solange Alexandre was also a rare woman who, with her future husband, created and commanded her own unit of anti-German fighters.

After serving as a Resistance courier from 1941, three years later she founded the Pas-de-Boeuf network near the town of RuillÃ©-sur-Loir.

With some 30 men under her orders, she organised sabotage operations, sheltered Allied airmen and, after the Allied landings in Normandy on D-Day in 1944, joined the fight against the occupying German forces.

President Emmanuel Macron was among many to pay tribute to Alexandre, after her death was announced at a nursing home in Le Mans.

"She said she only ever did her duty. But her duty was our freedom. France will never forget. Thank you," Macron posted on social media.

Born in 1922 in the Sarthe department of western France, Solange Alexandre, nÃ©e Dubuisson, grew up in a patriotic family that had suffered in World War One.

"My mother lost her first husband in the war, and my father was wounded in 1917. The words homeland and honour were very important to us. They were in my blood," she recalled in her memoirs.

In June 1940 she heard on the radio the appeal from General Charles de Gaulle in London, calling on the French not to give up the struggle against Germany. She had never heard of him, but that did not matter.

"From that moment I knew I had to join up," she wrote.

Some months later she was recruited by a friend of her father's â a Tunisian-born French army veteran called SaÃ¯d Belhaffaf, who had joined the Resistance.

Using the code-name Corlay, she criss-crossed the region around Le Mans by bicycle, gathering information and passing on messages.

"There were no mobile phones back then, and no private cars either because of the lack of petrol. The bicycle was king," she said in an interview two years ago.

In 1943 she met her first husband Guy DÃ©liot, who had deserted from the French gendarmerie after refusing to arrest some Jews.

The following April, with the Allied invasion approaching, they decided to take to the maquis, in other words live clandestinely in the forested hills, supplied with food by local farmers.

Their group consisted of deserters from German forced labour programmes, as well as survivors from another Resistance outfit that had been destroyed by the enemy. There were also two airmen, one American and one Canadian.

"Twice the Gestapo came for me, and twice I slipped through their hands," she later recalled.

After taking part in the liberation of Tours in September 1944, she ended the war with the rank of sub-lieutenant in the French Interior Forces (FFI).

Later she worked as a teacher and followed her husband on his many postings as an officer of the gendarmerie in French overseas territories. He was killed in 1960 in Martinique, and in 1981 she remarried.

Twice Solange Alexandre turned down the LÃ©gion d'Honneur, saying that she had only done her duty and that others were more worthy.

On receiving the award she said: "It is a great pleasure and a great honour. I am very proud â even if it is only a few weeks, or a few months, before I die."

#72. Nvidia hires major US law firm to investigate alleged Chinese spy affair

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has become the face of the US AI boom

Nvidia has hired one of the largest law firms in the US to investigate the affair between its own chief executive and an alleged Chinese spy.

Jensen Huang, who co-founded the tech company, has admitted to having a romantic relationship outside of his marriage. The woman, Alice Huang, is alleged to be a spy for Beijing.

Nvidia said on Tuesday that it had hired New York law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz, and that its attorney Joon Kim would lead "a thorough and independent investigation". The FBI is also conducting its own probe.

The company's share price has fallen almost to a yearly low, with $500bn (Â£375bn) wiped of its stock market value since Friday.

Nvidia is widely considered one of the most important companies behind the artificial intelligence (AI) boom, given it is the sole manufacturer of a computer chip used in AI tools.

It is the world's second most valuable company after Apple and its chips have become a matter of national security for US officials who are keen to win the AI race.

Alice Huang, who is also known as Huang Le, and is not related to Jensen Huang, was an executive at Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has been a customer of Nvidia.

Megaspeed had been suspected of helping divert Nvidia chips to China, a concern that Nvidia has said was investigated and debunked.

Nvidia has also maintained that the US government did not inform it that Megaspeed posed an intelligence concern.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

According to the New York Times, Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington earlier this year warning that Jensen Huang was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

Beijing has rejected claims that the Nvidia chief has been targeted, with its foreign ministry saying the allegations were "intended to smear and malign China".

US President Donald Trump said on Monday that he had spoken to Jensen Huang, who had said he had "no idea she was a spy." Trump said he believed him.

However, the relationship has drawn more political attention. Congressional leaders of the House Committee on Foreign Affairs have asked Huang to appear next week for a closed door briefing.

The Nvidia boss has yet to say whether he will attend, a person familiar with the committee's demand told the BBC. The committee has the authority to subpoena Huang, effectively forcing him to appear before it, if deemed necessary.

When announcing on Sunday that it would investigate the affair, Nvidia's board said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised.

It added that a committee of independent directors would oversee the probe.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Wachtell is one of the largest law firms in the US and has several notable clients in the tech industry. Among them are OpenAI and Salesforce.

Joon Kim is a former US Attorney for New York's Southern District and spent several years working as a government attorney focusing on fraud and other criminal acts.

The Nvidia investigation will likely be treated as urgent, according to sources familiar with the matter, as Jensen Huang's public image is tightly intertwined with the company.

He has become a key public figure in the development on AI, arguing the technology is a "new industrial revolution" and is on par with the advent of electricity.

It is unclear, however, if Wachtell will be able to interview Alice Huang.

A spokesperson for Nvidia declined to comment on Tuesday when asked if Jensen Huang had retained any of his own legal representation amid the company investigation and the separate one launched by the FBI.

#70. New Zealand election coalition talks will likely take weeks – as it happened

This blog is closed, you can read our report on the New Zealand election here

Thanks for following the blog tonight. The election has ended without a clear winner. Here are the top news lines from what will go down as one of New Zealand’s closest elections in many decades.

Christopher Luxon’s National party and its right-leaning coalition partners are on the brink of a wafer-thin majority that could allow it to eke out a return to power.

The left-wing bloc, led by Labour’s Chris Hipkins, fell short of a majority.

Neither Luxon or Hipkins claimed victory or conceded defeat. Both acknowledged an expected wait before a governing coalition is negotiated.

A centrist newcomer, the Opportunity party, has entered parliament, polling above the 5% threshold. They could potentially be a kingmaker in a coalition.

Final results are expected on 27 November. Coalition talks will likely take weeks.

Hundreds of thousands of special votes are yet to be counted and could change the shape of parliament.

Saturday figures showed there could be a 130-seat parliament, with 66 seats needed for a majority.

Results suggested a coalition of National, Act and NZ First would hold 67 seats.

The 67 included 42 for National, 13 for Act and 12 for NZ First.

The left-bloc totalled 56 seats: Labour 35, Greens 16 and 5 for Te Pāti Māori.

Opportunity is projected to gain 7 seats.

Over the coming days and weeks we will get a sense of the magnitude of Qiulae Wong and the Opportunity party’s success tonight. Opportunity are currently projected to claim seven seats in parliament.

Wong has said that if she held the tiebreaker power, she was open to negotiating with either a conservative or a left-wing government and would deal first with whichever major party won the most votes.

The right-wing bloc might not need her, and Luxon has ruled out governing with Opportunity. Analysts say, however, that both major party leaders would likely answer the phone if Wong held the only path to power.

You can read more about the rise of Opportunity here, in this piece from Michelle Duff.

A couple of pics of Christopher Luxon as he arrived to speak to supporters tonight.

On the current count, the Greens are celebrating a best-ever result of what looks to be 16 seats in parliament.

With 0.56% support, Te Pāti Māori will be stoked with its result tonight, writes Jamie Tahana.

It seems odd, but the Māori party has spent the election deliberately asking people not to give them their party vote. Instead, they asked for electorate votes only.

Just a reminder, seven of the seats in New Zealand’s parliament are reserved for representatives of Māori voters – you can read more about them here.

The party’s co-leaders, Rawiri Waititi and Debbie Ngarewa-Packer, have comfortably won their respective seats of Waiariki and Te Tai Hauauru; Oriini Kaipara swept the Auckland seat of Tamaki Makaurau; Hana Rawhiti Maipi-Clarke, who hit international stardom for her haka in parliament, has cruised across the line in Hauraki-Waikato; while Aperahama Edwards holds a narrow lead over the Greens’ Hūhana Lydon in the far North seat of Te Tai Tokerau.

That’s five seats. But with a party vote so low, they will be five overhang seats.

Still, after a term that has seen plenty of in-fighting in Te Pāti Māori, there are signs of frustration in the Māori electorate, with Waititi and Ngarewa-Packer seeing significantly reduced majorities.

The Greens have fallen short of gaining their first ever Māori electorate, but across the seats party vote has risen to about 40%, making the party a significant force in Māori politics.

Labour, having been punished by the Māori electorate in 2023, has improved, particularly in terms of party vote, and has claimed a new MP in Mananui Ramsden, who won the country’s largest electorate, Te Tai Tonga.

So, hundreds of thousands of special votes are going to potentially change where things stand right now.

In his speech tonight, Luxon said 300,000 special votes were yet to be counted. Those votes, which will be counted and known by 27 November, typically sway left.

In the 2023 election there 600,000 special votes, and National dropped from 50 seats in the preliminary count to 48 seats.

In a 130-seat parliament, in which 66 seats are needed to govern, this is where things stand with 97% of the vote counted. However, special votes are still to be counted and the lay of the land will not be clear until 27 November.

Labour 35, Greens 16, Te Pāti Māori 5 = 56

National 29.3%, Labour 29.2%, Greens 13.3%, Act 11%, NZ First 9.8%, Opportunity 6%, Te Pāti Māori 0.6%.

Jamie Tahana has this hot-take on the Luxon speech:

It was a tough line to tread for a speech: National is the biggest party on current results – 29.30% – but Luxon has also led the party to one of its most unpopular points, only 0.1% ahead of Labour. Luxon has also lost some of his most senior and experienced MPs, which he conceded required “some tough phone calls”.

Luxon said that because of the closeness of the result, and with special votes still to be counted, caretaker conventions will apply.

“For now, I want to tell New Zealanders that well established caretaker conventions apply … The daily business of government will continue.”

Caretaker convention basically means that while a new government is being formed, the incumbent government is in “caretaker” mode, which means it will carry on the normal business of governing.

Jamie Tahana notes that for Luxon to continue as prime minister, he will have to cobble together a three- or four-party coalition, from a weakened position compared to 2023.

In his speech, Luxon said: “Negotiations and compromises are going to be required … A number of races are still too close to call.”

Jamie Tahana has some more from the Luxon speech:

Luxon called the election “too close to call”, and said “meaningful negotiations” for a new coalition government will start after the final results are known on 27 November.

“It looks like an MMP special,” he said. “It’s heartening to see that so far National is once again the largest party in parliament and we have a path to forming the next government.”

Luxon concedes result unlikely tonight, saying that meaningful negotiations will begin after final results on 27 November.

Luxon arrived to a crowd of about 700 raucous supporters chanting “three more years”. He was flanked by his wife Amanda and two children, waved and grinned as he descended a stairwell flanked in blue light.

A heavy music track - which could not be immediately identified through Shazam - thrummed as he walked down a long aisle, hugging supporters.

When he took the podium, Luxon conceded the election was “too close to call” but the crowd cheered as he announced National had secured the highest party count.

He went on to acknowledge Labour leader Chris Hipkins, and admonished supporters who booed Quilae Wong and Opportunity, saying it was “not the kiwi way”.

Luxon is also paying tribute to those high-ranking National MPs who find themselves out of a job tonight: Nicola Willis, Paul Goldsmith and Gerry Brownlee. “I want to thank you … for your years of service to our party”.

Luxon has called tonight’s result “an MMP special” and says “on the numbers so far, National is once again the largest party in parliament”.

It will be interesting to see what Luxon has to say. How will he frame the vote count and National’s performance?

Christopher Luxon is high-fiving and hugging supporters. He’ll be speaking soon.

#67. First day of Ukraine peace talks constructive, Zelensky says

Senior officials from Ukraine, Russia and the US will reconvene for a second day of talks on Wednesday

The first of two days of trilateral talks between Ukraine, Russia and the US aimed at ending the war in Ukraine has concluded in Abu Dhabi.

While senior officials from Kyiv and Moscow were tight-lipped after the four hour-long discussions, President Volodymyr Zelensky, who was not at the talks, said they had proved "constructive and quite substantive".

Speaking from Paris, US Secretary of State Marco Rubio also said the meeting had been "very productive".

Although expectations for a breakthrough that ends Russia's full-scale war on Ukraine are low, US officials said earlier that Moscow and Kyiv were privately showing "a little bit of flexibility" on the issue of who would control Ukraine's eastern regions in a postwar scenario.

Zelensky also said that humanitarian issues such as prisoner of war exchanges had been discussed.

At the start of Tuesday's talks President Donald Trump's son-in-law, Jared Kushner, told delegates that the US had "narrowed the gaps" between the two sides, without providing details, and hoped to move towards a "solution that hopefully ends the war".

Kushner had said earlier the talks would be focused on addressing the "postwar security concerns" of both Russia and Ukraine.

Asked later at a news conference in Copenhagen about the approach of US officials to negotiations, Zelensky said: "I see changes. I'm very happy with what I hear."

Kushner and Trump peace envoy Steve Witkoff also met Ukrainian and European and Nato officials on the sidelines of the main meeting.

Until now European leaders have largely been excluded from peace talks, but they have pushed to be included in the discussions amid concerns that decisions about their continent's security would be made without them.

The Americans appear to support this, with Rubio acknowledging during a visit to Paris that the Europeans "are a part of solving this problem for the long term".

However, "the Russians have a bad taste in their mouth with the Europeans", according to a US official.

Some proposals favoured by EU officials - such as calling an unconditional ceasefire as a prerequisite for peace negotiations - are routinely rejected by Moscow. Russian Foreign Minister Sergei Lavrov said on Tuesday that a truce would just allow Kyiv to regroup. "We will not agree to it," he said.

Moscow and Kyiv's positions are still far apart on ending the conflict.

A key area of disagreement involves Ukraine's eastern region of Donetsk, which is mostly occupied by Russia.

Moscow has demanded that Ukraine relinquish the parts of Donetsk it still holds, while Kyiv wants to freeze the contact line to maintain control over those areas, which it says are crucial to its future security.

While neither side has budged on these demands, the US official speaking on the eve of the talks suggested that this was just the public stance and that Moscow and Kyiv had shown "a little bit of flexibility, privately".

However, he acknowledged the issue would not be solved without discussions about a new "security architecture".

"They will not solve the Donetsk issue and the territorial issue until they know what the security architecture is going to be after the war," the official said. "And keep in mind, we're not going to be able to finalise one without the other."

For Ukraine, a post-war "security architecture" may include reassurance that allies will come to its aid if it comes under attack again. For Russia, that could be its longstanding ambition to see Nato roll back from Eastern Europe â which would be alarming to Europeans.

The framework being proposed includes security consultations and crisis communications hotlines, as well as "reciprocal de-escalation measures" between the two. The officials also floated the re-activation of the Nato-Russia Council that has been largely paused since 2022.

Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff are part of the US delegation that will attend the talks in Abu Dhabi

The last round of trilateral talks, held in Geneva in February, ended without a breakthrough. Soon after, the US-Israel war on Iran diverted Washington's attention from Ukraine, and no other discussions were held since.

From the start of his second term in office, US President Donald Trump â who once boasted he could end the war in Ukraine within a day â has spearheaded several rounds of talks to end the conflict, albeit with little success.

Meanwhile, his increasingly conciliatory approach towards Moscow has fuelled deep unease in Kyiv and across the European Union.

After meeting Kremlin envoy Kirill Dmitriev in Washington last week, Trump touted a forthcoming "big, beautiful agreement" on Ukraine that "might involve all of Europe".

Many in Ukraine are sceptical that this would result in a fair peace.

Ukrainian MP Oleksandr Merezhko, head of the foreign affairs parliamentary committee, told the BBC he believed Trump wanted "a quick deal at the expense of Ukraine", which will certainly be rejected by Kyiv.

The only way to make Russia's Vladimir Putin start real negotiations was by putting greater pressure on Russia through sanctions and deep strikes, Merezhko said.

"While Putin has a war machine up and running, he has no incentives to negotiate." He also mentioned recent moves by Trump, such as his deal to buy diesel from Russia, as something that "only encourages Moscow to continue the war".

Moscow's delegation in Abu Dhabi includes Kremlin aide Vladimir Medinsky, deputy foreign ministers Sergei Ryabkov and Mikhail Galuzin, spy chief Igor Kostyukov and army general Yury Zhigarlovsky.

Kyiv has sent several senior officials who have been part of previous negotiating teams, including Foreign Intelligence Service head Rustem Umerov, Volodymyr Zelensky's chief of staff Kyrylo Budanov, and MP Davyd Arakhamiya.

Away from Abu Dhabi, Russia continues to pummel Ukraine with deadly bombardments and strikes on energy infrastructure, viewed in Kyiv as proof that Moscow wants to corner Ukraine into accepting a deal on Russia's terms.

On Monday, Kushner was cautious to point out that the Abu Dhabi talks could at most "build momentum toward the next phase of negotiations".

"This is an extraordinarily difficult and complicated conflict," he wrote on X, adding the US was "approaching these discussions with determination and realism."

#65. NZ election 2026: New Zealand faces weeks of uncertainty with results ‘too close to call’

National party will be largest in parliament but ultimate shape of next government won’t be settled for some time

New Zealand could be facing a weeks-long wait to know the shape of its next government, after its nail-biting election failed to hand a decisive victory to either the incumbent rightwing coalition or opposition left bloc.

On preliminary results, the centre-right National party, led by the prime minister, Christopher Luxon, managed to secure enough seats to govern alongside its traditional coalition partners, the libertarian Act and populist New Zealand First parties.

But with some seats won by very slim margins, and special votes yet to be counted, that success is precarious, resulting in an election too close to call until official results are released on 27 November.

Special votes include overseas votes and ballots cast outside a voter’s electorate. In 2023, 600,000 such votes were made, 21% of the total. These votes tend to favour the left – in 2023, special votes resulted in Te Pāti Māori (the Māori party) and the Greens gaining three seats between them.

With nearly 100% of the regular votes counted on Saturday night, National secured 42 seats, Act 13 and New Zealand First 12. The combined 67-seat majority is wafer thin, with the left bloc just three seats behind.

Speaking to a room full of the party faithful at Auckland’s Viaduct events centre just before midnight, Luxon said the results were “too close to call” and the special votes would need to be counted before negotiations could begin.

National was still the largest party in parliament, he said, and had “a pathway to forming the next government”. National pipped Labour by 0.02% in the party vote.

Labour, led by Chris Hipkins, secured 35 seats, while its traditional coalition partners the Greens won 16 and Te Pāti Māori five. Even with the seven seats from the centrist newcomer party, Opportunity, the left bloc is three seats short of forming a government.

Before a rowdy crowd of supporters at the Cosmopolitan Club in Upper Hutt, north of Wellington, Hipkins joked that New Zealand had spoken but “it may take a bit longer to work out what they’ve said”.

“As things stand tonight, it is clear that we are not going to know the shape of our next government for a little while longer yet,” Hipkins said, promising that Labour would get “straight to work” should the final count land in its favour.

The collapse of the two major parties, amid a sluggish economy and stubbornly high ⁠inflation, is feeding record support for the smaller parties, including Act, New Zealand First and the Greens. It is also helped propel Opportunity into parliament for the first time.

Opinion polls in the lead-up to the election had Opportunity placed to become the kingmaker. At this stage, the right bloc does not need the party to govern, but if special votes change the landscape, its predicted role could come to fruition.

Currently at 5.9%, the centrist party has met the threshold needed to enter parliament, a historic first. Since New Zealand adopted the mixed-member proportional (MMP) electoral system in 1996, no new party has been able to meet that threshold – all the other minor parties have been born from MPs or groups splintering from larger parties.

The party’s leader, Qiulae Wong, told her supporters in Auckland she was “stoked”.

“It’s looking very promising for us,” she said. “Let’s just see how the rest of the votes fall.”

In New Zealand, voters cast two votes: one for an electorate MP and one for a party. The system is meant to produce a parliament that reflects each party’s share of the nationwide vote.

However, if New Zealanders vote for more electorate MPs from a particular party than their party vote would entitle them to, they get to keep the extra MPs in what is called an overhang.

National won more seats that its party support allows for, meaning it could gain an extra five seats to make up the balance.

That will be good news for its path to power, but comes with some significant trade-offs for the party. Four of its most senior ministers, including Nicola Willis, Paul Goldsmith, Chris Bishop and Gerry Brownlee, will not return to parliament because they did not win a seat, or the party didn’t win enough votes to bring in list-only MPs.

On the other side of the house, Te Pāti Māori picked up five overhang seats.

With all the extra seats, there could be 130 in the next parliament – a record under MMP – which means 65 seats are needed to form a coalition.

The country faces a two-week wait until special votes are announced, followed by the possibility of weeks of negotiations. Luxon said caretaker government conventions would apply in the meantime.

“For now, I want to tell New Zealanders that well-established caretaker conventions apply … The daily business of government will continue.”

#62. Board of Peace says Israel's aid response after Gaza floods 'insufficent'

Heavy rain and stormy weather made life harder in Gaza, says resident

US President Donald Trump's Board of Peace has called on Israel to immediately allow all necessary humanitarian supplies into Gaza after rain flooded tens of thousands of tents.

The US-backed body, established to oversee the implementation of Trump's Gaza peace plan, acknowledged the steps now being considered by Israel, but said "the response remains insufficient".

Israel's military agency in charge of co-ordinating aid into Gaza said it had allowed in tens of thousands of tents and trucks with winter supplies, but added reconstruction "can only begin after Hamas fully disarms".

On Sunday, three children and a woman were killed when a sand embankment collapsed onto tents in the central Strip.

Most people in Gaza have been displaced because of Israel's military actions. Around 65% of the population, about 1.4 million people, now live in tents or makeshift shelters, the UN says.

Standing soaked in Gaza City, Mohammed Jneid told the AFP news agency that heavy rains hit from one side and sewage overflowed on them from the other, with the ground and tents no longer able to hold.

"We have nothing left, no bedding, no food, nothing," he said.

Sitting inside her tent, Islam Al Mobayed told AFP there were seven children there, five of them young.

"This is not life," she said. "We are exhausted, we are tired of this entire life."

In rare criticism of Israel, the Board of Peace said it had been urging the Israeli government to authorise the entry of essential goods for temporary shelter and water and sanitation infrastructure.

The Board added that delays in approving the entry of supplies "exacerbated the consequences of a foreseeable humanitarian challenge" and delayed the start of decommissioning weapons and transferring Hamas authority to a transitional government.

It also said Hamas's failure to disarm and transfer power had compounded the difficulties facing Gaza's civilians.

The UN's humanitarian agency OCHA said more than 150,000 tents were outside Gaza ready to go in, but aid efforts were constrained "by Israeli restrictions on the entry of supplies and equipment and funding shortfalls".

UN humanitarian coordinator Ramiz Alakbarov said on Monday that stocks of essential shelter and winter items inside Gaza had been exhausted.

"Israeli authorities must immediately lift all restrictions on entry of the supplies," he said, adding "we have run out of time".

Israel's foreign ministry spokeswoman Michal Maayan on Monday said Israel had in recent months put in place a winter plan in coordination with the Board of Peace and international charities.

That plan has brought in more than 91,000 tents and 8,200-plus trucks with tarpaulins, blankets, winter clothing, and other equipment, according to Cogat, the Israeli military body responsible for aid.

Cogat said it would not allow in equipment that could be used for military purposes.

Maayon accused Hamas of hoarding aid, saying: "Hamas is responsible for the delays in making more progress in the Strip, and winter-related hardships are a consequence of Hamas's action and policy."

In response to the Board of Peace, Hamas said in a statement published on Palestinian media that its comment "presents a distorted account of the causes of the disaster". The group denied it was responsible for ceasefire delays, pointing to a statement in August , externalfrom Egyptian, Qatari and Turkish mediators that accused Israel of violations.

Hamas said Israeli restrictions prevent the entry of materials necessary for shelter and recovery - and added the Board as the ceasefire implementor should look at its own "serious shortcoming".

Under the deal to end the two-year-long war in October 2025, Hamas agreed to give up governance and its weapons in exchange for a phased Israeli troop withdrawal from the 60% of Gaza it still occupied. Israel, however, has insisted Hamas must fully disarm before withdrawal.

The dispute has left the next stages of the peace plan stalled, with both sides blaming each other.

The war in Gaza began after the Hamas-led attack on Israel on 7 October 2023 when around 1,200 people were killed and 251 others taken hostage.

The Israeli military campaign in response has killed 74,645 people, according to the Hamas-run health ministry, whose figures the UN considers reliable. More than 1,600 people have been killed since the ceasefire in October last year.

Israel says six of its soldiers have been killed in Gaza since then.

#61. Uyghurs facing deportation by Thailand say they will be ‘like slaves’ in China

Activists fear Bangkok will give in to pressure from Beijing despite reports of torture and human rights abuses against the mostly muslim ethnic minority

The deportation of two more Uyghurs to China from Thailand would put their lives in grave danger, activists have said, with one of the men saying he would be “like a slave” if he was sent back.

Last year, Thai authorities forcibly deported 40 Uyghur men to China despite pleas from the men themselves and the UN. The US and Canada had reportedly offered to resettle them, but Bangkok appeared to want to avoid upsetting Beijing. The UN has said the returnees’ whereabouts remain unknown.

Now, two more men fear becoming the latest victims of Beijing’s repressive crackdown against the largely Muslim ethnic group, condemned by the UN as a potential crime against humanity.

The girlfriend of one of the men said her boyfriend, whose name is being withheld for security reasons, told the Guardian he wanted to go “anywhere else but China”.

Human rights groups said two Uyghur men were at risk of deportation after recently being transferred to an immigration detention centre and they feared Thai authorities may once again give in to pressure from Beijing.

Elaine Pearson, Asia director at Human Rights Watch, called for the men to be allowed to travel to a safe third country. “Previous Thai governments have kept Uyghurs in inhumane detention for years and then complied with Beijing’s demands to send them to China, where they were forcibly disappeared,” she said.

The men had been serving a seven-year prison sentence for offences related to an attempted escape from immigration detention in January 2020. They were part of a group of more than 200 Uyghur men, women and children who were apprehended by Thai police in 2014 near the border with Malaysia after they fled from China.

Since 2014, women and children in the group have been released to Turkey, while the men have been deported to China, in separate batches, attracting criticism from the United Nations and the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio.

Uyghurs, a Turkic minority mainly from the Xinjiang region in China, have been subjected to decades of oppressive and discriminatory treatment from the Chinese authorities. This has included the incarceration of hundreds of thousands of people in detention camps aimed at stamping out their cultural identity.

Beijing has defended its policies in Xinjiang as being aimed at poverty alleviation and counter-terrorism.

In July, Marzeyya Kasem’s (who asked to be referred to by her Islamic alias) boyfriend wrote to her in a letter that he would be “like a slave” if he was sent back to China. “Sometimes I thought why I’m a human being. What I do wrong against China … no want to go back to China,” he wrote. She shared the letter with a Thai rights group.

The two men facing imminent deportation reportedly pleaded their case to Thailand’s office of the National Human Rights Commission on Wednesday.

Relatives of Uyghurs have said the Chinese government was “paranoid about Uyghurs going to a safe country in large numbers and living together”, and preferred to have them deported.

It is not clear whether China has issued a formal extradition request for the two men. But Chaipruak Doungprapat, Thailand’s national security council chief, told reporters that the men, along with three other Uyghurs who are still in prison, had arrest warrants outstanding in China.

Some have questioned whether criminal warrants could be credible considering that at least one of them, Kasem’s boyfriend, was a minor when he left China. “If they are really wanted for a crime in China, why didn’t China immediately issue a [Interpol] red notice [when they were detained in 2014]?” asked Kasem.

Kasem believes that because Thailand was criticised for last year’s mass deportation, this time the authorities were trying to establish a more credible legal basis for the move.

A spokesperson for China’s foreign affairs ministry said: “The cooperation between China and Thailand in combating cross-border illegal and criminal activities such as smuggling is consistent with the laws of the two countries, international law and international practice.”

A spokesperson for Thailand’s foreign affairs ministry declined to comment.

#58. Nvidia opens investigation after report CEO Jensen Huang was target of foreign intelligence

CEO reportedly ‌had close relationship ‌with a Chinese businesswoman who passed information to ‌Beijing

Nvidia said ⁠on Sunday it “is initiating an independent investigation in light of recent reporting that founder and CEO Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort”.

The New York Times reported in ​October that Donald Trump had been briefed last year on intelligence that Huang ‌had a close relationship ‌with a Chinese businesswoman who passed information to ‌Beijing.

Speaking to reporters on Sunday, Trump downplayed the revelations about Huang, who is an influential adviser to the president on artificial intelligence, saying: “I think he’s an honorable guy. I think he probably has an excuse for it.”

Trump also brushed off a question about whether he now regrets not acting on the intelligence when he was briefed on it, and suggested that he would be happy to take Huang with him on a trip to Asia next week. “I haven’t heard he’s done anything wrong,” Trump said. “He dealt with somebody. But, you know, so does everybody else.”

Republican lawmakers have expressed more alarm, asking Huang to give the House foreign affairs and intelligence committees a closed-door briefing next week on Nvidia’s “security protocols against foreign adversary espionage” and his relationship with Alice Huang or Huang Le, an executive at a Singapore-based datacenter company called Megaspeed, who is of no relation to the Nvidia CEO. She had previously been suspected of smuggling Nvidia chips to China, prompting American officials to surveil and monitor her.

Those officials have determined that she was acting as a Chinese intelligence asset, passing commercial information to Beijing, the New York Times reported, citing unnamed sources.

The company’s board said in a statement on Sunday that, while it has no reason to believe that either US national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised, its inquiry will examine all available information.

“The Board prioritizes US national and economic security, which is paramount to the interests of NVIDIA and its shareholders,” the company said. “The Board has confidence in Jensen and his leadership team and will provide further updates as appropriate.”

Huang fully ‌supports the review and has committed to cooperating with it, it added.

Axios reported on ​Sunday, citing a draft email by Huang to employees, that Huang admitted to having a romantic relationship with a suspected Chinese spy and took full ⁠responsibility for his actions.

“I was unfaithful to my ​wife. My ​actions hurt her ​and our children. There is no excuse ​for what ‌I did,” ​Huang said ​in the draft email also seen by Reuters.

Nvidia did not immediately respond to a request for comment on the Axios report.

The Times reported on Sunday that a memo drafted by US secretary of state Marco Rubio circulated among top policymakers in the Trump administration last spring, citing intelligence that the businesswoman who had a relationship with Nvidia’s chief executive was a spy for China’s ministry of state security. Despite those concerns, Huang was invited to join Trump’s delegation when he traveled to China in May. Huang and his wife, Lori Huang, were also invited to a state dinner at the White House in September for Xi Jinping and seated at the top table with Trump and China’s president.

Nvidia’s powerful chips serve as a cornerstone of the AI boom, placing Jensen Huang into one of the most influential leadership positions in the industry. In that position, he has developed a close partnership with Trump as the US government attempts to defend its position as a leader in AI development.

However, national security concerns have prompted strict US export controls, effectively barring the company from selling its top-tier processors to customers in China.

US officials have cited threats to the US supply chain and fears over national security risks as a rationale for the restrictions. Scott Bessent, the US treasury secretary, has also suggested that China could be sanctioned for theft of US data and intellectual property.

The Trump administration has imposed restrictions on exports of advanced AI chips to China as a matter of national security. Megaspeed’s Alice Huang was suspected to have been helping the government get around those restrictions.

The investigation also follows a rough few days on Wall Street for AI tech firms, particularly Nvidia. A sell-off of tech stocks that began earlier this month and gained steam with disappointing earnings led to a total decline of nearly $1tn in value off the tech-heavy Nasdaq exchange in one week and erased about $2tn in a month.

A drop of 3.1% for Nvidia, previously worth about $5tn, whose chips have made it the poster child of the AI frenzy, was the heaviest force pulling the S&P 500 downward recently.

Nick Robins-Early and Blake Montgomery contributed reporting

#57. Malaysia's disgraced former PM leaves jail for house arrest after embezzling millions

Najib smiles to reporters at the Kuala Lumpur High Court after it cleared the way for him to go home

Malaysia's disgraced former Prime Minister Najib Razak has arrived home to begin serving the rest of a jail sentence for corruption under house arrest.

There was a big media presence at his Kuala Lumpur residence to see the 73-year-old return from prison in an SUV late on Tuesday night, escorted by a heavily armed police convoy.

Najib's return ended a four-year prison stint after he was found guilty over the embezzlement of billions of dollars from the country's sovereign wealth fund.

The 1MDB scandal brought down his government and made headlines around the world when it came to light a decade ago, embroiling prominent figures from Malaysia to Goldman Sachs and Hollywood.

Investigators estimated that $4.5bn (Â£3.3bn) was siphoned from the state-owned wealth fund, which Najib co-founded, into private pockets, including his own. More than $1bn allegedly ended up in accounts linked to Najib, according to prosecutors.

Najib's lawyers claim he had been misled by his advisers - in particular the financier Jho Low, who has maintained his innocence and remains at large.

Journalists jostled for a glimpse of Najib as he was driven to his home, which has been declared a federal prison

Najib's mansion in one of the capital's most exclusive neighbourhoods was declared a federal prison ahead of his homecoming on Tuesday.

Critics said his release from jail undermined efforts to fight corruption and questioned its legal basis, saying Malaysian law made no provision for house arrest.

Najib, who was prime minister from 2009 to 2018, denied wrongdoing but was convicted at two separate trials.

In the first trial he was sentenced in 2020 to 12 years in prison and jailed in 2022. The term was later halved on appeal.

In September, the King of Malaysia granted him a conditional pardon to see out the remaining two years of his sentence at home.

The transfer to his mansion was to take place on Friday but put on hold after the attorney general sought clarification of the royal decree. On Tuesday the high court ruled the house arrest was effective immediately.

Najib's party paid a $12m fine as part of the terms of the royal pardon allowing him to serve the rest of his term at home.

He was sentenced after being convicted of abuse of power, money laundering and breach of trust over 42 million ringgit ($10m; Â£7.7m) transferred from SRC International - a former unit of 1MDB - into his private accounts.

Najib is appealing against a 15-year prison sentence, handed down at a second trial in December 2025, in a case involving much larger sums of money. In that case he was convicted of misappropriating nearly 2.3 billion Malaysian ringgit ($569m; Â£422m) from the nation's sovereign wealth fund 1Malaysia Development Berhad (1MDB).

He is due to start serving that sentence after his house arrest ends, which is expected in August 2028.

#53. US and rest of world must follow China’s lead on green energy, says John Kerry

Former US secretary of state says most nations are ‘stumbling along’ while Beijing has ‘hugely accelerated’

China is leading the world in moving away from fossil fuels, and the rest of the globe – including the US – must now follow, the former US secretary of state John Kerry has told the Guardian, as Cop watchers wonder exactly how China is going to approach this year’s climate summit.

As Cop31 opens – this year the climate conference is in Turkey – there is plenty of speculation about whether China will finally step into the leadership role that it has long avoided. In its latest “five-year plan” adopted in March 2026, China for the first time pledged to “actively participate in and lead global climate governance”. Cop31 could provide the first indications Beijing is ready to put that rhetoric into action, something it has historically been reluctant to do.

Green energy is a vital component of the export engine that has kept China’s sluggish post-pandemic economy on track. The success of its exports stands in contrast to its failure to significantly boost domestic general consumption, a structural weakness that has persistently vexed leaders.

In 2025, clean-tech exports surpassed $220bn, and they should grow again this year; the world’s largest emitter is now the world’s leading supplier of clean energy technology. China accounts for about 85% of solar and 80% of lithium-ion battery supply chain production capacity, and in 2025, nearly 75% of electric cars were manufactured there.

Smart moves now by China would position it as a linchpin in a future electrostate world order, persuade rivals to cooperate, and solidify its influence in the global south.

Meanwhile other countries, including the US under Donald Trump, have fallen badly behind. Kerry said they would lose out economically – as well as putting the climate in further danger – unless they, too, embraced low-carbon power and rapid electrification. “The only country that has hugely accelerated is China. The rest of the world is stumbling along while Mother Nature is sending us more powerful signals every week, every day,” he said.

“[China] realised suddenly they had way too much dependency on oil and gas from other places, and they decided they needed to be independent. Other countries, since the war in Iran, and since the strait of Hormuz has been interrupted, have made a similar decision, and they’re moving independently. There’s been a massive uptick in deployment of renewables. Thank you, Donald Trump.”

For autocratic states without free and fair elections, seeing the economic benefits reaped by China would be the spur to adopt low-carbon growth, he added.

“They’ll see where the market is going, and they will recognise where the cheaper alternatives are. Even they can read a spreadsheet, and understand where costs are exorbitant [in fossil fuels], and where you have a better deal,” Kerry said. “Right now, renewables together with storage, together with some transitional gas, is cheaper and more effective, than just going with fossil fuels.”

He added: “The marketplace has made its decision and globally is moving to electrons, and electrostate status. People are going to electricity and they’re going to try to do that as clean as humanly possible. That’s the choice that I see being implemented in many different places. It is unstoppable.”

China has already said it wants Cop31 to discuss the free flow of green technology against a backdrop of “intensifying” geopolitical conflicts – widely seen as a reference to US and EU tariffs on items such as Chinese electric vehicles and solar products.

Li Shuo from the Asia Society Policy Institute (ASPI) puts it this way: “In a world where climate action was once seen as a constraint on growth but is now increasingly a driver of it, China faces a singular question: will it step into a leadership role not out of obligation, but out of self-interest?”

The summit’s presidency has promised this Cop will be one of “implementation and acceleration” – and when it comes to the green transition, nowhere has implemented or accelerated quite like China. As a result, emissions have been in a “flat or falling” trend since March 2024, analysis for Carbon Brief shows.

Beijing sees the energy transition as “central to economic modernisation and energy security, with significant implications for international influence”, said Kevin Tu from the Oxford Institute for Energy Studies. The wars in Ukraine and the Middle East and their effect on oil and gas supplies have only hardened that stance.

The conflicts have also highlighted the geopolitical implications of a planetary shift to green energy. In the fossil fuel era, the designation of the US dollar as the global trading currency for oil gave Washington “what has been termed the exorbitant privilege of being the global lender of last resort”, said Isabel Hilton, the founder of the specialist environment outlet China Dialogue.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

“Without that, the US position in the world looks weaker, and if you add China’s dominance of the supply of clean technologies and the supply chains that produce them, US dominance looks more vulnerable still.”

Meanwhile, President Xi Jinping describes China as a “doer and go-getter working for the cause of global development” and state media frequently showcase Chinese renewable projects in countries ranging from Indonesia to Pakistan and Kenya. The value of the green energy deals in China’s belt and road initiative in the first half of 2026 has already surpassed the total for the whole of 2025, a recent analysis showed.

Those countries could be useful allies if China can get the subject of tariffs into the talks. “China is likely to find support from Brazil, India, South Africa and other developing countries concerned about the costs and market-access effects of climate-related trade measures,” said Tu.

China has generally held back from policy ambition or agenda-setting at forums such as Cop. Its attitude to the plans to set a global target for electrification has been ambivalent, even though it would easily hit it, according to ASPI’s Kate Logan. Partly, China has an aversion to prescriptive outside targets, but it is also trying to navigate the divergent interests of its global south partners.

Yixian Sun, a professor of sustainability governance at the University of Bath, said: “I still think there is a mismatch between Chinese policymakers’ views on the role that China can play in global climate governance and international expectations on the influence that China should exert.”

If the world wants China to play a leadership role, the environment needs to change, and more trust extended, he says. “They have to be ready to accept China’s leadership, and I don’t think this is the case, especially for global north countries”.

Analysts will watch China’s actions around climate finance commitments and support for a “just transition” – a framework designed to secure workers’ rights and livelihoods in the move away from fossil fuels.

They will also be looking for signals Beijing will bid for Cop33 in 2028; officials are said to be trying to decide if hosting would be beneficial.

For now, said Logan, “China would be happy with a Cop that doesn’t necessarily produce anything monumental, but comes out with a relatively cooperative outcome … so no major drama.”

#52. Spain's king and queen attend memorial service for rail crash victims

Spain's King Felipe VI and Queen Letizia were greeted by bishops as they arrived at the funeral mass on Tuesday

Spain's King Felipe and Queen Letizia were among those who have attended a memorial service in tribute to the 69 victims of last month's deadly train crash.

The accident occurred when one train, travelling at high speed, ploughed into the back of another that was stationary on the Madrid-Seville line, near the small town of Urda, about 40 miles from Toledo.

It was the worst rail tragedy in Spain since 2013. Twenty-eight people remain in hospital.

"The tragic accident in Urda has left our families, our towns and our society with a pain that is difficult to accept," Archbishop Cerro Chaves told those present.

Representatives of those killed carried lit candles to the front of the cathedral and placed them next to the main altar. The first reading of the service was by Ana BenÃ­tez, sister of David BenÃ­tez, a 38-year-old father-of-two who died.

The king and queen were met with applause by bystanders as they entered the cathedral and at the end of the service, after Catholic Mass, they spoke to relatives of the victims.

Senior politicians also attended the service, including the leader of the opposition People's Party, Alberto NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o, the president of the Castilla-La Mancha region, Emiliano GarcÃ­a-Page, and Transport and Economy Minister Carlos Cuerpo.

Cuerpo took on the transport portfolio following the resignation last week of Ãscar Puente from the post.

The Archbishop of Toledo officiated the memorial service

Puente had come under mounting pressure following the crash, Spain's second major rail tragedy this year after 46 people died in a collision on the same high-speed line in January.

Some local people from the town of Adamuz in Andalusia, where that accident happened, as well as residents of Urda, were present for the memorial service.

The investigation into the recent accident is still in progress. Puente suggested that human error combined with a glitch in the safety system may have been the cause.

The tragedy happened a month before the 29 November general election and is seen as likely to hurt Socialist Prime Minister Pedro SÃ¡nchez's attempts to secure re-election.

The ceremony took place on the same day as a nationwide strike by train drivers, who are demanding greater safety on the rail network.

Rail services were also expected to be affected across the country on Wednesday due to a general strike, led by housing activists calling for urgent measures to control rental costs.

#48. Asymmetric cyberwarfare: 'States increasingly use cyberattacks in support of their other objectives'

Erin Ogunkeye is pleased to welcome Rafe Pilling, Director of Threat Intelligence in the Sophos Counter Threat Unit (CTU). How can a minor cyber intrusion disrupt food supplies, expose the weaknesses of industrial infrastructure and become an instrument of geopolitical confrontation? The cyberattacks targeting supermarket refrigeration systems in France offer a revealing illustration of how digital vulnerabilities can translate into tangible economic disruption and potentially broader threats to society. As Pilling explains, “With a small amount of effort from the adversary side, they can create a large impact, in this case destroying food and producing empty shelves or empty refrigeration units in stores.” This asymmetry lies at the heart of contemporary cyber conflict, in which modest technical interventions can produce disproportionate economic and operational consequences

#47. Israel's new parliament sworn in as leaders compete to form government

Gadi Eisenkot has vowed to oust Benjamin Netanyahu, but still has to get enough parties on board

Israel's new parliament has been sworn in, as opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot seeks to oust caretaker Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu.

The former army chief has been tasked with forming a government after receiving more recommendations from members of the Knesset than Netanyahu, although not all those backing him are willing to govern together.

Eisenkot's bloc of four parties won 48 seats in October's election, while Netanyahu's bloc won 53, leaving both short of a majority in the 120-seat Knesset. He has 28 days to build a coalition, with a possible two-week extension.

Almost half of the new MKs did not serve in the previous parliament, while Arab-led parties have a record number of seats.

Shortly after they were sworn in, Netanyahu and Eisenkot warned parliament about the perils of supporting a government led by the other.

"[And] to those who are thinking of establishing a government that includes supporters of terror and haters of Israel, or that will be dependent on them, I ask you, are you prepared to be dependent on those whose goal is the destruction of the Jewish state?" said Netanyahu, alluding to the prospect of an Eisenkot-led government reliant on support from Arab parties.

He also appealed for the formation of a government of national unity as the only way to confront what he said was the threat of "a nuclear Iran".

Eisenkot sharply criticised Netanyahu's comments.

"I sat and listened to the outgoing prime minister's delusional, divisive, hate-filled and shameful speech, exploiting a state occasion," he said. "I am now even more convinced that he is unfit to remain in office for even one more minute."

The outcome of the election, which was widely seen as a referendum on Netanyahu, means there is a period of uncertainty over who will form the next government. Netanyahu will remain caretaker prime minister until then - a process which could take from days to months.

One of the most dramatic results was the performance of Israel's two Arab-led alliances - the United Arab List and the Joint List - which won an unprecedented 19 seats between them following a surge in turnout among Arab voters.

The Arab parties could therefore hold the balance of power, although Netanyahu has ruled out governing with them and three of the parties in Eisenkot's bloc have opposed including them in a coalition.

On Sunday, President Isaac Herzog tasked Eisenkot with trying to form a government.

Speaking at the president's residence, the centrist Yashar party leader called for the formation of a "national emergency government" - an implicit appeal to parties to put ideological differences aside to remove Netanyahu from office.

This, he said, would "require creative and unconventional political steps".

Eisenkot said any party or MK prepared to accept four principles could join his proposed government. Those are recognition of Israel as a Jewish and democratic state; accepting the values of the Declaration of Independence; backing mandatory military, national or civilian service for all, including communities currently exempt; and recognising Hamas as a terrorist organisation and committing to dismantling its military and governing capabilities.

The four requirements, however, could present significant obstacles to Eisenkot's potential partners.

The conscription of ultra-Orthodox Jewish men, who are exempt from military service if they are in full-time religious study, was a major election issue.

The ultra-Orthodox United Torah Judaism (UTJ) and Shas parties, which won 13 seats in total, have fiercely objected to attempts to change the status quo - something that all parties in Eisenkot's bloc campaigned to do.

Eisenkot's four principles are also contentious for the Arab parties. Both emphasise equal national and civic rights for Israel's Arab citizens, who form about 20% of the population; the vast majority of Israeli Arabs are not required to serve in the military; and neither has publicly recognised Hamas as a terrorist organisation.

The Arab parties could, though, help Eisenkot form a minority government. The United Arab List, which won eight seats, has said it is willing to be part of an Eisenkot-led coalition, while the Joint List, which won 11 seats, has offered parliamentary support from the outside.

If Eisenkot accepted such an arrangement, he would still have to persuade other parties in his bloc to agree. Avigdor Lieberman, whose Yisrael Beiteinu party won seven seats, has so far refused to join a minority government dependent on Arab MKs.

In return for their support, the ultra-Orthodox parties would be likely to demand major concessions on military conscription, which might be unacceptable to most of the parties in Eisenkot's bloc.
